admits a minimal invariant set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
. First note that
\Lambda
cannot be a discrete orbit, as this would imply the existence of elements without fixed points, contradicting Lemma
1.9
. Thus,
\Lambda
is unique and contained in every
\varphi
-invariant closed subset of
\mathbb{R}
; equivalently, it does not intersect any
\varphi
-invariant proper open subset. However, cofinality of
\mathcal{W}
gives an interval
J\in\mathcal{W}
such that
J\cap\Lambda\neq\varnothing
, and we have remarked that its
\varphi
-orbit gives a proper
\varphi
-invariant open subset and the desired contradiction. Finally, let
S\subset G
be any finite symmetric subset, and
(J_{n})\subset\mathcal{W}
an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
by wandering intervals. For every
s\in S
and every sufficiently large
n
, we must have
s.J_{n}\cap J_{n}\neq\varnothing
, and thus
s.J_{n}=J_{n}
. It follows that the subgroup
\langle S\rangle
fixes the endpoints of
J_{n}
for every sufficiently large
n
, and thus it is totally bounded (in the sense that it fits the conditions in
(

L

1)
). This describes the horocyclic case.

In the remaining case,
\varphi
is irreducible and there exists
I\in\mathcal{L}
with a cofinal orbit. As before, the action
\varphi
cannot have a discrete orbit, so
\varphi
has at most one minimal invariant set; it is enough to show that such a set exists. Since
\mathcal{L}
is covering, every non-empty closed
\varphi
-invariant subset of
\mathbb{R}
intersects an element of
\mathcal{L}
and so, by cofinality of the orbit of
I
, it intersects
I
as well. By compactness of
\overline{I}
, Lemma
1.11
provides a minimal invariant set.
∎

Definition 1.11
.

In the sequel, we adopt the terminology introduced in Proposition
1.10
. If
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is a laminar action, we also use the same terminology to distinguish different subgroups of
G
. For instance, we shall say that a subgroup
H\subseteq G
is horocyclic if
\varphi|_{H}
satisfies
(

L

3)
. Note that the homothetic case is a subcase of the pseudo-homothetic case, but it is convenient to single it out.

A particularly relevant special case of Proposition
1.10
is when
H
is a cyclic subgroup. In this case, we have a classification of individual elements into two types, which are analogous to the classification of isometries of trees into loxodromic and elliptic elements. This leads to the introduction of the following terminology.

Definition 1.12
.

We say that a homeomorphism
g\in\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is an expanding (respectively, contracting)
pseudo-homothety
if the set
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(g)
of fixed points is (non-empty and) compact, and for every open interval
I\supset\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(g)
, we have
\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}g^{n}(I)=\mathbb{R}
(respectively,
\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}g^{-n}(I)=\mathbb{R}
). If in addition
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(g)
is reduced to a single point, we say that
g
is a
homothety
.

Proposition 1.13

(Dynamical classification of elements)
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a laminar action, with invariant covering lamination
\mathcal{L}
. For
g\in G
, set
\mathcal{L}^{g}=\{I\in\mathcal{L}:g.I=I\}
. Then the following alternative holds:

—

if
\mathcal{L}^{g}
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
, then
\varphi(g)
is totally bounded; otherwise,

—

\varphi(g)
is a pseudo-homothety. Moreover,
\varphi(g)
is a homothety if and only if
\mathcal{L}^{g}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Most of the statement is Proposition
1.10
, specialized to the cyclic subgroup generated by
g
, which must satisfy one of the first two cases by Lemma
1.9
. We only need to justify the part of the statement characterizing when
\varphi(g)
is a homothety. Observe that if
\mathcal{L}^{g}\neq\varnothing
, then
\varphi(g)
has at least two fixed points (the endpoints of any
I\in\mathcal{L}^{g}
). Conversely, suppose
\varphi(g)
is a pseudo-homothety with
|\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(g)|\geq 2
; then the set
\{I\in\mathcal{L}:\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(g)\subset I\}
has a smallest non-trivial element, which belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{g}
. ∎

We now justify that all notions discussed above are stable under semi-conjugacy.

Proposition 1.14
.

Let
\varphi,\psi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be two irreducible semi-conjugate actions. If
\varphi
is laminar, then so is
\psi
, and the type of each subgroup
H\subseteq G
is the same for
\varphi
and for
\psi
.
In particular, every focal laminar action is semi-conjugate to a minimal focal laminar action.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an invariant covering prelamination for
\varphi
, and

h\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
a semi-conjugacy from
\varphi
to
\psi
(in the sense that (
1
) hold n
h
is proper, as a consequence of irreducibility of
\varphi
and
\psi
. Thus for every sufficiently large interval
I\in\mathcal{L}
,
h(I)
is not a singleton. Hence, the collection of the interiors of the convex hulls of the images
h(I)
, where
I
runs through all the intervals
I\in\mathcal{L}
, gives a
\psi
-invariant covering prelamination. The statement on the type of subgroups follows easily from Proposition
1.10
since the properties that a subgroup be totally bounded, have compact set of fixed point, admit or not a minimal set, are all stable under semi-conjugacy. Finally, note that if
\varphi
is focal, then the existence of a non-discrete minimal invariant set implies that it is semi-conjugate to a minimal action (Corollary
1.15
), which must be focal by what we have just proved.
∎

Next, we observe that conversely, any
minimal
laminar action, is automatically focal. More precisely, we have the following result, which is an improvement of Remark
1.5
.

Proposition 1.15
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a minimal action which preserves a prelamination
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathcal{L}
is covering, thin, and the
\varphi
-orbit of every
I\in\mathcal{L}
is cofinal.
In particular,
\varphi
is focal and proximal.

Proof.

Fix
I\in\mathcal{L}
. The family
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\{g.I:g\in G\}
is also a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination. The union of elements of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is a non-empty
\varphi
-invariant open subset, so that by minimality of the action, it has to coincide with the whole real line. Remark
1.5
ensures that
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is a covering prelamination. Hence so is
\mathcal{L}
, and it also follows that the orbit of
I
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
. Since
I
is arbitrary, this shows that the orbit of every
I\in\mathcal{L}
is cofinal. Now, suppose that
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{L}
is a maximal totally ordered family, and consider the (possibly empty) open interval
J
defined as the interior of the intersection
\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{F}}I
. The
\varphi
-images of
J
are pairwise equal or disjoint, so their union is a proper
\varphi
-invariant open subset, hence by minimality we must have
J=\varnothing
. This shows that
\mathcal{L}
is thin. Finally, this fact combined with cofinality of the action of
G
on
\mathcal{L}
implies that arbitrarily large intervals can be mapped into arbitrarily small neighborhoods of a given point, so that
\varphi
is proximal.
∎

Example 1.16
.

The Plante-like actions in Example
1.8
are minimal and laminar, and so they are focal.

2.
Horogradings

We now introduce the notion of horograding, which will play a crucial role in our main theorem for actions of locally moving groups.

1.
Definitions

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a prelamination of an ordered set
(\Omega,\prec)
. A
horograding
of
\mathcal{L}
is a monotone map
\mathfrak{h}\colon(\mathcal{L},\subset)\to(\mathbb{R},<)
. We say that a horograding
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{R}
is
positive
(respectively,
negative
) if it is non-decreasing (respectively, non-increasing): if
I\subseteq J
, then
\mathfrak{h}(I)\leq\mathfrak{h}(J)
(respectively,
\mathfrak{h}(I)\geq\mathfrak{h}(J)
).

Definition 2.2

(Horogradings of actions)
.

Let
\psi\colon G\to\Aut(\Omega,\prec)
be an order-preserving action admitting an invariant covering prelamination. A (positive or negative)
prehorograding
of
\psi
by an action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(X)
, where
X\subset\mathbb{R}
is an open interval, is a pair
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
consisting of:

—

a
\psi
-invariant covering prelamination
\mathcal{L}
;

—

a (positive or negative) horograding
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathcal{L}\to X
, such that
\mathfrak{h}(\psi(g)(I))=\rho(g)(\mathfrak{h}(I))
for every
I\in\mathcal{L}
and every
g\in G
.

In the case that
\Omega=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{L}
is a lamination, we say that
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a
horograding
of
\psi
by
\rho
.

Note that although it is convenient to state this definition without any assumptions on
\varphi
and
\rho
(except laminarity of
\varphi)
, in practice we will often assume that
\varphi
is
focal
and that the horograding action
\rho
is irreducible.
In this section, in order to discuss general properties of horogradings, we restrict for simplicity to the case
\Omega=\mathbb{R}

When there is no ambiguity, we will use the shortcut notation
\mathfrak{h}(g.I)=g.\mathfrak{h}(I)
for
\mathfrak{h}(\psi(g)(I))=\rho(g)(\mathfrak{h}(I))
(and similar variations), assuming that it is clear that the action
\psi
is on the source of
\mathfrak{h}
(where points are denoted by Greek letters) and the action
\rho
is on the image of
\mathfrak{h}
(where points are denoted by Latin letters).

Remark 2.3
.

If an action
\psi\colon G\to\Aut(\Omega,\prec)
has a prehorograding
(\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathfrak{h})
by an action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, then the dynamical realization
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
of
\psi
also has a prehorograding by
\rho
. Indeed, we have seen in Remark
1.7
that
\varphi
preserves a covering prelamination
\mathcal{L}
, obtained by considering interiors of convex hulls of the images of leaves of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
under the associated good embedding
i\colon(\Omega,\prec)\to(\mathbb{R},<)
. Then
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h}\circ i^{-1})
gives a prehorograding of
\varphi
by
\rho
.

Remark 2.4
.

A positive (respectively, negative) prehorograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
of a laminar action
\varphi
by
\rho
can be extended to a positive (respectively, negative) horograding defined on the closure
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
, provided
\mathcal{L}
is thin (this is always true if
\varphi
is minimal, by Proposition
1.15
). Indeed, in this case, every
J\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}
contains some element of
\mathcal{L}
. It follows that the map
\mathfrak{h}
can be extended, in the positive case, by setting
\mathfrak{h}(J)=\sup\{\mathfrak{h}(I):I\in\mathcal{L},I\subset J\}
.

Example 2.5

(Self-horograding)
.

Every laminar action
\varphi
can be (positively) horograded by itself. Indeed, choose an invariant covering lamination
\mathcal{L}
, and define a horograding by
\mathfrak{h}(I)=\sup I
.

Example 2.6

(Horograding of Plante-like actions)
.

Consider our running example of a Plante-like action of a permutational wreath product
H\wr_{X}G
, described in Example
1.8
. Fix a
G
-invariant order
<_{X}
on
X
and
<_{H}\in\LO(H)
and, as in Example
1.8
, let
\Psi\colon H\wr_{X}G\to\Aut(\bigoplus_{X}H,\prec)
be the associated Plante-like product (where
\prec
is the order of lexicographic type on
\bigoplus_{X}H
associated with
<_{X}
and
<_{H}
).
As in Example
1.8
, we assume that
G
,
H
, and
X
are countable, and that the action of
G
on
(X,<_{X})
is cofinal, so that we can take the dynamical realization
\varphi\colon H\wr_{X}G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, with associated good embedding
\iota\colon(\bigoplus_{X}H,\prec)\to\mathbb{R}
. Let also
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be the dynamical realization of the
G
-action on
(X,<_{X}
), with associated embedding
\iota_{G}\colon(X,<_{X})\to\mathbb{R}
. We claim that the Plante-like action
\varphi
is horograded by
\rho
(seen as an action of
H\wr_{X}G
which factors through the projection to
G
). To see this, recall that an invariant prelamination for
\Psi
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\{C_{\s,y}:\s\in\bigoplus_{X}H,y\in X\}
, where
C_{\s,y}\subset\bigoplus_{X}H
is defined as in (
2
). From (
2
) it is evident that the correspondence

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathfrak{h}_{0}\colon&\mathcal{L}_{0}&\to&(X,<_{X})\\
&C_{\s,x}&\mapsto&x\end{array}

is monotone and
G
-equivariant. It follows that
(\mathcal{L}_{0},\iota_{G}\circ\mathfrak{h}_{0})
defines a prehorograding of
\Psi
by
\rho
. After Remark
2.3
, we obtain a prehorograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
of the dynamical realization
\varphi
of
\Psi
by the dynamical realization
\rho
; after Remark
2.4
, since
\varphi
is minimal (as shown in Example
1.8
), the prehorograding can be extended to a horograding of
\varphi
by
\rho
.

Let us point out the following simple consequence of Definition
2.2
, that will be often used implicitly.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\varphi,\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be irreducible actions, and
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
a positive prehorograding of
\varphi
by an irreducible action
\rho
. If
(I_{n})\subset\mathcal{L}
is any increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
, then
\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})\to+\infty
as
n\to+\infty
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{h}(\mathcal{L})
is
\rho
-invariant and
\rho
is irreducible, we must have
\sup\mathfrak{h}(\mathcal{L})=+\infty
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is non-decreasing and
(I_{n})
is a cofinal sequence in
\mathcal{L}
, the conclusion follows.
∎

In contrast, it is
not
true that
\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})
tends to
-\infty
whenever
(I_{n})
is a decreasing sequence in
\mathcal{L}
with diameter tending to
0
. Consider for example the self-horograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
of a focal laminar action by itself (Example
2.5
). Then for every decreasing sequence
(I_{n})
in
\mathcal{L}
, the sequence
\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})=\sup I_{n}
cannot converge to
-\infty
, since
\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})\geq\inf I_{0}
for every
n
. It is therefore convenient to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 2.8

(
\mathfrak{h}
-complete points)
.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
be a positive horograding of
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
by
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. A point
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
is
\mathfrak{h}
-
complete
if there exists a decreasing sequence
(I_{n})\subset\mathcal{L}
such that
\bigcap I_{n}=\{\xi\}
and
\lim\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})=-\infty
. The analogous definition can be given for negative horogradings.

(When the horograding action
\rho
is on an open interval
X\subsetneq\mathbb{R}
, this definition should be modified by requiring convergence to the appropriate endpoint of
X
.)

Remark 2.9
.

The set
\Xi_{\mathfrak{h}}
of
\mathfrak{h}
-complete points is always a
G_{\delta}
-subset of
\mathbb{R}
, i.e. a countable intersection of open sets. For instance, in the case of a positive horograding, we have

\Xi_{\mathfrak{h}}=\textstyle\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{L},\,\mathfrak{h}(I)\leq-n}I.

It is also clearly
\varphi
-invariant. It follows that when the action
\varphi
is minimal, the subset
\Xi_{\mathfrak{h}}
is either empty or residual, i.e. generic in the sense of Baire.

2.
Classification of elements in the horograded case

A particularly useful consequence of the existence of a horograding of a laminar action
\varphi
by an irreducible action
\rho
, is that the type of each element for
\varphi
is determined by
\rho
.

Proposition 2.10

(Dynamical classification of elements in the horograded case)
.

Assume that a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is positively horograded by an irreducible action
\rho:G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Then for every element
g\in G
, the following hold.

—

\varphi(g)
is totally bounded if and only if
\rho(g)
has fixed points accumulating on
+\infty
.

—

Otherwise,
\varphi(g)
is a pseudo-homothety, which is expanding if
\rho(g)(x)>x
for every sufficiently large
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and contracting otherwise. Moreover if
\rho(g)
has no fixed point, then
\varphi(g)
is a homothety, and its unique fixed point is
\mathfrak{h}
-complete.

Instead of proving Proposition
2.10
directly, we shall deduce it from the following two lemmas, that give similar conclusions for more general subgroups.

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is positively horograded by an irreducible action
\rho:G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, a finitely generated subgroup
H\subseteq G
is totally bounded for
\varphi
, if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\rho}(H)
accumulates on
+\infty
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
be a positive horograding of
\varphi
by
\rho
. By Proposition
1.10
,
H
is totally bounded for
\varphi
if and only if the subset
\mathcal{L}^{H}
of
H
invariant leaves is cofinal. If this is the case, then
\mathfrak{h}(\mathcal{L}^{H})
consists of
\rho(H)
-fixed points, which by Lemma
2.7
, accumulate on
+\infty
.
1
1

1

Note that this implication does not use that
H
is finitely generated.
For the converse, assume that
H
is not totally bounded. By finite generation, Proposition
1.10
gives that it is either pseudo-homothetic or focal. In either case, we can find
I\in\mathcal{L}
and a sequence
(h_{n})\subset H
such that
h_{n}.I
is an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
. Then by Lemma
2.7
, the images
h_{n}.\mathfrak{h}(I)=\mathfrak{h}(h_{n}.I)
must converge to
+\infty
, and so
\rho(H)
cannot have fixed points accumulating to
+\infty
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Assume that a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is positively horograded by an irreducible action
\rho:G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
H\subseteq G
be a subgroup which is pseudo-homothetic for
\varphi
, and denote by
J^{-}
and
J^{+}
the two unbounded connected components of
\suppphi(H)
(see Proposition
1.10
). Then the following hold.

—

\supp^{\rho}(H)
has a connected component adjacent to
+\infty
, denoted as
U
.

—

the
\varphi
-action of
H
on
J^{+}
(respectively,
J^{-})
is positively (respectively, negatively) semi-conjugate to its
\rho
-action on
U
.

Moreover, if
\supp^{\rho}(H)=\mathbb{R}
, then
\varphi(H)
is homothetic, and its unique fixed point is
\mathfrak{h}
-complete.

Proof.

The proof is similar to part
(

L

2)
of the proof of Proposition
1.10
. Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
be a positive horograding. Set
K=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(H)
, and consider the collection
\mathcal{L}_{K}=\{I\in\mathcal{L}:K\subset I\}
, which is totally ordered and
\varphi(H)
-invariant. Set
c=\inf\mathfrak{h}(\mathcal{L}_{K})
. It follows from Proposition
1.10
that every
I\in\mathcal{L}_{K}
has a cofinal orbit for
\varphi(H)
, and can also be mapped arbitrarily close to
K
. Hence the
\rho(H)
-orbit of
\mathfrak{h}(I)
accumulates on
c
and
+\infty
. It follows that
U=(c,+\infty)
is a connected component of
\supp^{\rho}(H)
. For
\xi\in J^{\pm}
, define
I_{\xi}=\inf\{I\in\mathcal{L}_{K}:\xi\in\overline{I}\}
. Then the maps
J^{\pm}\to U
,
\xi\mapsto\mathfrak{h}(I_{\xi})
, are the desired semi-conjugacies. Finally if
U=\mathbb{R}
, i.e. if
c=-\infty
, it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{K}
has no lower bound in
\mathcal{L}
, and this is possible only if
K
is reduced to a single point, which is
\mathfrak{h}
-complete.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.

Since every cyclic subgroup of
G
is either totally bounded or pseudo-homothetic (Proposition
1.13
), the proposition follows from Lemmas
2.11
and
2.12
.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

In general, the type of a subgroup
H\subseteq G
with respect to a laminar action
\varphi
, according to Proposition
1.10
, is not determined uniquely by its image in the horograding action
\rho
. Elaborating on the previous arguments, one can see the following.

—

If
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\rho}(H)
accumulates on
+\infty
, then
H
is either totally bounded or horocyclic. Both cases are possible if
H
is not finitely generated.
For instance in the case of a Plante-like action of
H\wr_{X}G
(see Example
1.8
), the subgroup
\bigoplus_{X}H
is horocyclic, but
\rho(\bigoplus_{X}H)=\{\mathsf{id}\}=\rho(\{\mathsf{e}\})
.

—

If
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\rho}(H)
does not accumulate on
+\infty
, then
H
can be either focal or pseudo-homothetic. Again, both cases are possible. In the case of a Plante-like action, we have
\rho(H\wr_{X}G)=\rho(G)
; the first is focal and the second is pseudo-homothetic.

In §
1
, we will describe a more global relation between the large-scale dynamics of a horograded laminar action and the horograding action, which will be important for the results in Part
3
.

3.
Finding invariant laminations

In this final section we describe a mechanism to get an invariant lamination for an action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, that will be used in the proof of the main results of this part.

1.
General criterion

The central notion of our method is the following.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an action and
H\subseteq G
a subgroup. We say that a non-empty interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an
irreducible wandering interval
for
\varphi(H)
if the following hold:

(
\mathcal{W}
1)

I
is bounded and open,

(
\mathcal{W}
2)

I
is wandering for
\varphi|_{H}
(that is, for every
h\in H
either
h.I=I
or
h.I\cap I=\varnothing
),

(
\mathcal{W}
3)

the action of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Stab}}^{\varphi}_{H}(I):=\stab^{\varphi|_{H}}_{H}(I)
on
I
has no fixed points.

We denote by
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)
the family of irreducible wandering intervals for
\varphi(H)
.

If
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(G)\neq\varnothing
, then
\varphi
cannot be minimal, as the union of all images of any
I\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(G)
is a
\varphi
-invariant proper open subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Conversely, we have the following criterion for finding irreducible wandering intervals.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an action with no minimal invariant set. Then every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
belongs to an irreducible wandering interval for
\varphi(G)
.

Proof.

Note that
\varphi
is irreducible, because any global fixed point defines a minimal invariant set. Then for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, we can find an element
g\in G
such that
g.\xi>\xi
. Since
\varphi
has no minimal invariant set, there exists a closed non-empty invariant subset
D
that does not intersect
[\xi,g.\xi]
(otherwise we could use Lemma
1.11
to find a minimal invariant set). Denote by
J
the component of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus D
containing
[\xi,g.\xi]
, and set
H:=\operatorname{\mathsf{Stab}}^{\varphi}(J)
. Note that
H
is non-trivial, as it contains the element
g
. Then, we can take the component
J_{0}\subseteq J
of
\suppphi(H)
that contains
\xi
. We claim that
J_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(G)
. We first check that condition
(

W

2)
is satisfied for
J_{0}
. To see this, take
h\in G
. Since
D
is
\varphi
-invariant and
J
is a component of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus D
, we have that either
h.J=J
or
h.J\cap J=\varnothing
. In the second case we have
h.J_{0}\cap J_{0}=\varnothing
. In the first case, we have that
h\in H
, and therefore
h
preserves
J_{0}
, since it is a component of the support of the action of
H
. Finally, condition
(

W

3)
holds because
J_{0}
is a component of the support of
H
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an action, and consider two subgroups
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
of
G
, and distinct wandering intervals
J_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H_{i})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, the following hold.

(i)

if
H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}
, then
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
do not cross.

(ii)

If
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
commute, then
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
do not cross.

In particular, any two distinct wandering intervals in
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(G)
do not cross.

Proof.

To prove
(i)
, set
J_{1}=(a,b)
and
J_{2}=(c,d)
. Suppose by contradiction that they are crossed, and assume without loss of generality that
a<c<b<d
. Applying condition
(

W

3)
to
J_{2}
, we find
g\in H_{1}
such that
g.J_{1}=J_{1}
and
g.c\neq c
. Since
g.b=b
and
g\in H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}
, condition
(

W

2)
applied to
J_{2}
implies that
g.J_{2}=J_{2}
. This contradicts the fact that
g.c\neq c
. Thus,
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
do not cross. To show
(ii)
, suppose by contradiction that
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are crossed. Let
\xi
be the endpoint of
J_{1}
contained in
J_{2}
; by condition
(

W

3)
, there exists
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Stab}}^{\varphi}_{H_{2}}(J_{2})
such that
g.\xi\in J_{1}
. However,
\xi
is fixed by
\operatorname{\mathsf{Stab}}^{\varphi}_{H_{1}}(J_{1})
, while
g.\xi
is not (again by
(

W

3)
), contradicting the assumption that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
commute.
∎

The combination of Lemma
3.3
and Proposition
1.15
immediately implies the following.

Proposition 3.4

(Criterion for laminarity)
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be a subgroup with the property that any two conjugates of
H
in
G
commute or are related by inclusion, and suppose that
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is an action such that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)\neq\varnothing
. Then
\mathcal{L}=\bigcup_{g\in G}\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}({gHg^{-1}})
is a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination. In particular if
\varphi
is minimal, then it is focal.

2.
The case of normal subgroups

It is worth pointing out the following special case of Proposition
3.4
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Consider a faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
N\triangleleft G
be a normal subgroup which is not a cyclic subgroup of the center of
G
. Assume that the
\varphi
-action of
N
is not minimal. Then
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
is a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination; in particular,
\varphi
is focal.

First we recall the following lemma. Its proof is well known but we include it for completeness.

Lemma 3.6
.

Consider a faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
N\triangleleft G
be a normal subgroup which is not a cyclic subgroup of the center of
G
. Then, either the image of
N
acts minimally, or it admits no minimal invariant set.

Proof.

Assume that there exists a minimal
\varphi(N)
-invariant set
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, and let us show that
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}
. Note that
\Lambda
cannot be a fixed point otherwise, as
N
is normal and
\varphi
is minimal, this would imply that
N
acts trivially, which is an absurd because we are assuming that
\varphi
is faithful (see Corollary
1.7
for details).
Then
\Lambda
is either the unique minimal
\varphi(N)
-invariant set, or a discrete orbit. In the first case, we have that
\Lambda
is preserved by the whole group
G
and thus
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}
by minimality. In the second case we have that the action of
N
must be semi-conjugate to a cyclic action coming from a homomorphism
\tau\colon N\to\mathbb{Z}
, and that
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\tau
acts trivially on
\Lambda
. Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\tau
is precisely the subset of
N
acting with fixed points, the subgroup
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\tau
is necessarily normal in the whole group
G
, and so, as before, we must have
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\tau=\{1\}
. Thus
N
is infinite cyclic and acts as a group of translations. Since
N
is normal in
G
, this implies that
N
is central in
G
, contradicting the assumption.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

By Lemma
3.6
, we have that
\varphi|_{N}
does not admit any minimal invariant set. Hence, by Lemma
3.2
, the set
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
is non-empty, and Proposition
3.4
gives that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
defines a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination, and that
\varphi
is focal.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The converse to Proposition
3.5
is not true, as we will see with an example described in §
4
that gives a minimal laminar action for the simple group
[F,F]
(where
F
is Thompson’s group). See however Proposition
3.3
for a partial converse to Proposition
3.5
.

3.
An application to solvable groups

We conclude this section with the following application of Proposition
3.5
, which shows that laminar actions appear naturally in the context of solvable groups. Its derivation from Proposition
3.5
uses ideas of Rivas and Tessera in
[
111
]
. By an affine action of
G
on
\mathbb{R}
we mean an action by affine transformations, i.e. transformations of the form
x\mapsto ax+b
with
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 3.8

(Alternative for solvable groups)
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an irreducible action of a finitely generated solvable group. Then, either
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to an affine action, or it is laminar and focal.

Proof.

If
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to a cyclic action, then the first case holds. So up to semi-conjugacy, we can assume that
\varphi
is minimal; moreover upon replacing
G
by a quotient we can suppose that it is faithful. Let
G^{(n)}
denote the derived series of
G
, and consider
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that
G^{(k)}\neq\{1\}
and
G^{(k+1)}=\{1\}
. Then
H:=G^{(k)}
is an abelian normal subgroup of
G
. As
H
is normal, we deduce that
\varphi(H)
cannot have fixed points (otherwise, as
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(H)
is a closed invariant subset, we would get that
H
acts trivially).

Assume first that
H
is a cyclic subgroup of the center of
G
. Then
\varphi(H)
is conjugate to the cyclic group generated by a translation and the action
\varphi
induces a minimal action on the circle
\mathbb{R}/\varphi(H)
. As
G
is solvable and thus amenable, it preserves a Borel probability measure on the circle, which must be of total support for the action is minimal; as it is well known (see for instance Navas
[
102
, Proposition 1.1.1]
), this gives that the action is conjugate to a minimal action by rotations (basically, this is the action defined by the rotation number homomorphism
\mathsf{rot}\colon G\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
). Therefore,
\varphi(G)
is the lift of a group of rotations of the circle, thus conjugate to a group of translations (and so of affine transformations).

Assume next that
H
acts minimally. Take a non-trivial
h\in H
and note as before that
\varphi(h)
has no fixed points (otherwise we would get that
H
acts trivially). Thus, we obtain a minimal action of
H
on the circle
\mathbb{R}/\langle\varphi(h)\rangle
, and the argument for the previous case leads to the conclusion that
\varphi(H)
is conjugate to a minimal group of translations. Then, the set of invariant Radon measures for
\varphi(H)
corresponds to the one-parameter family of positive multiples of the Lebesgue measure, and this family must be preserved by
\varphi(G)
, for
H
is normal in
G
. By a standard argument, we deduce that
\varphi(G)
is conjugate to a group of affine transformations (see for instance Plante
[
110
]
).

If neither of the previous cases hold, Proposition
3.5
gives that
\varphi
is laminar and focal, as desired.
∎

Since in a minimal laminar action every element has fixed points (Lemma
1.9
), we deduce the following result first obtained by Guelman and Rivas in
[
55
]
.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated solvable group, and let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an action such that some element acts without fixed points. Then, the action
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to an affine action.

Chapter 8
Laminations and micro-supported groups

This chapter contains the main results of Part
2
, which describe the dynamics of exotic actions of micro-supported and locally moving groups on the line, using invariant laminations and horogradings. We refer to Chapters
2
and
3
for basic definitions and results used throughout this chapter.
In a first result (Theorem
1.1
), we shall show that if
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
is a micro-supported group acting minimally on an interval
X
, then all faithful minimal actions of
G
on the line must be laminar, with at most one exception. Our main result (Theorem
2.1
) implies that if
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
is in addition finitely generated and fragmentable, then any faithful minimal action of
G
on the line is either conjugate to the standard action on
X
, or laminar and horograded by it.

1.
Most actions of micro-supported groups preserve laminations

Let
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be a micro-supported subgroup acting minimally on an open interval
X
(this is equivalent to the existence of one non-trivial element of relatively compact support, see Proposition
1.4
). When
G
is locally moving, the standard action of
G
cannot preserve any lamination. Indeed, for any bounded
1
1

1

Recall our convention that “bounded” stands for “relatively compact” here.
open interval
I=(a_{1},a_{2})
, one can choose two disjoint intervals
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, such that
a_{i}\in J_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. As
G
is locally moving, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
there exists
g_{i}\in G_{J_{i}}
such that
g_{i}(a_{i})>a_{i}
, so that
g_{1}g_{2}(I)
crosses
I
. Hence, no bounded open interval
I
can belong to an invariant lamination. We will show the following.

Theorem 1.1

(Laminar/locally moving alternative)
.

Let
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be a micro-supported subgroup acting minimally on an open interval
X
. Then the following hold.

(i)

The standard action of
G
on
X
is either laminar or locally moving.

(ii)

Every faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which is not conjugate to the standard action, is laminar.

We first need to discuss a couple of lemmas in preparation for the proof. The following result is essentially Proposition
2.1
, which we restate here in a more explicit form, for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an action of a direct product, and suppose that
\varphi|_{\Gamma_{1}}
is irreducible and admits a minimal invariant set
\Lambda
. Then, either

(i)

the action of
\Gamma_{1}
on
\Lambda
factors through a free action of an abelian quotient of
\Gamma_{1}
, or

(ii)

\Gamma_{2}
preserves
\Lambda
, its action on
\Lambda
is non-trivial and factors through a free action of an abelian quotient of
\Gamma_{2}
, or

(iii)

\Gamma_{2}
fixes
\Lambda
pointwise.

In particular, in either case there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
[\Gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i}]
fixes
\Lambda
pointwise.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Theorem
1.20
, the proof is identical to the proof of Proposition
2.1
(note that the finite generation of
\Gamma_{1}
was used there only to ensure the existence of a minimal invariant set).
∎

Lemma 1.3
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be a micro-supported subgroup whose action on
X
is minimal. Suppose that for any
x\in X
, the subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
and
G_{(x,b)}
act without fixed points on
(a,x)
and
(x,b)
, respectively. Then
G
is locally moving.

Proof.

Take a bounded open interval
I=(u,v)\Subset X
and
x\in I
. We need to show that
g(x)\neq x
for some
g\in G_{I}
. Since
G
is micro-supported, the subgroup
G_{c}
of compactly supported elements is non-trivial. Since the action of
G
is minimal and
G_{c}
is normal, we have that
G_{c}
has no fixed points, and thus there exists
h\in G_{c}
such that
h(u)>v
. Consider a bounded interval
(u_{0},v_{0})
containing
\supp(h)\Supset I
. From the assumption, we can find elements
k_{1}\in G_{(a,x)}
and
k_{2}\in G_{(x,b)}
such that
k_{1}(u_{0})\in(u,x)
and
k_{2}(v_{0})\in(x,v)
. Writing
k=k_{1}k_{2}
, we have
g=khk^{-1}\in G_{I}
and
g(x)\neq x
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We first prove
(i)
. Let us denote by
\iota\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(X)
the standard action, and suppose that
G
is not locally moving. After Lemma
1.3
, we can assume that there exists
x\in X
such that
G_{(a,x)}
has fixed points in
(a,x)
(the symmetric case, in which this holds for some
G_{(x,b)}
, can be treated similarly). This implies that
\supp(G_{(a,x)})
has a bounded connected component
J
, which in particular belongs to the set
\mathcal{W}_{\iota}(G_{(a,x)})
of irreducible wandering intervals. Since all conjugates of
G_{(a,x)}
are related by inclusion, we conclude by applying Proposition
3.4
.

We next prove
(ii)
under the assumption that the standard action of
G
on
X
is locally moving. We want to use Proposition
3.4
again. Note that the subgroups of the form
G_{(x,b)}
or
G_{(a,x)}
satisfy the condition of Proposition
3.4
(as their conjugates are related by inclusion), and so do their commutator subgroups. So it is enough to find one subgroup
H
of this type such that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)\neq\varnothing
. Fix
x\in X
. By Proposition
1.1
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(x,b)})=\varnothing
. If
G_{(x,b)}
does not admit any minimal invariant set, we conclude by Lemma
3.2
. Else, if
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
is a minimal invariant set for
G_{(x,b)}
, we deduce from Lemma
1.2
that
\Lambda
is fixed by
H=[G_{(x,b)},G_{(x,b)}]
or
H=[G_{(a,x)},G_{(a,x)}]
. In either case, any connected component
J
of
\suppphi(H)
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)
.

Finally, we prove
(ii)
under the assumption that the standard action on
X
is not locally moving. By part
(i)
, we can find a covering lamination
\mathcal{L}
, invariant under the standard action. For every
I\in\mathcal{L}
, the subgroup
G_{I}
satisfies the condition of Proposition
3.4
, and so does
[G_{I},G_{I}]
. Hence it is enough to find
I\in\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)\neq\varnothing
for
H=G_{I}
or
H=[G_{I},G_{I}]
. Fix
I\in\mathcal{L}
. If
\suppphi(G_{I})
has a bounded connected component, say
J
, then
J\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}({G_{I}})
. Else, assume that every connected component of
\suppphi(G_{I})
is unbounded above or below (and thus there are at most two of them). If the action of
G_{I}
on one such component
J
has no minimal invariant set, we can still conclude that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}({G_{I}})\neq\varnothing
by Lemma
3.2
. Finally, suppose that the action of
G_{I}
on an unbounded component
J
has a minimal invariant set
\Lambda
. Choose
g\in G
such that
g(I)\cap I=\varnothing
. Then
g.J
is a component of
\suppphi(G_{g(I)})
, which intersects
J
non-trivially (as it is unbounded). Since
[G_{g(I)},G_{I}]=\{\mathsf{id}\}
, we deduce that
g.J=J
, so that we can apply Lemma
1.2
to the action of
G_{I}\times G_{g(I)}
on
J
. We deduce that
H=[G_{J},G_{J}]
or
H=[G_{g(I)},G_{g(I)}]
fixes
\Lambda
pointwise. In either case
\suppphi(H)
has bounded connected components, so that
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(H)\neq\varnothing
. ∎

2.
Horograding by the standard action

1.
Statement of the main result

Recall (from §
1
) that for a subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
, where
X=(a,b)
, we denote by
G_{+}
(respectively,
G_{-}
) the normal subgroup of elements acting trivially on some neighborhood of
b
(respectively,
a
), and
G_{\mathsf{frag}}=G_{-}G_{+}
is the fragmentable subgroup of
G
. This subgroup plays an important role in the theorem below.

Theorem 2.1

(Horograding theorem)
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a subgroup acting minimally on
X
, with
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
non-trivial and finitely generated. Then
G
is locally moving, and every faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is either conjugate to the standard action on
X
, or laminar, horograded by the standard action on
X
.

The finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
allows to prove the following corollary, providing a classification for irreducible actions of
G
, up to semi-conjugacy.

Corollary 2.2
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a subgroup acting minimally on
X
, with
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
non-trivial and finitely generated. Then, every irreducible action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is semi-conjugate to an action in one of the following families.

—

(Induced from a quotient)
. An action induced from the largest quotient
G/[G_{c},G_{c}]
.

—

(Standard)
. The standard action on
X
.

—

(Horograded by standard)
. A minimal laminar action, horograded by the standard action on
X
.

Proof of Corollary
2.2
assuming Theorem
2.1
.

Some argument is needed because
G
itself is not supposed to be finitely generated, so that its actions need not be all semi-conjugate to a minimal or cyclic action. Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be an irreducible action. Suppose at first that
\varphi|_{G_{\mathsf{frag}}}
is irreducible. Since
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is finitely generated, there exists a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
intersecting every
\varphi(G_{\mathsf{frag}})
-orbit, and thus every
\varphi(G)
-closed invariant subset. Hence
\varphi
has a minimal invariant set (by Lemma
1.11
), and thus it is semi-conjugate to a minimal or cyclic action; we conclude using Theorem
2.1
. If
\varphi(G_{\mathsf{frag}})
has a fixed point, then
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to a non-faithful action (Corollary
1.7
). Finally, any non-faithful action must factor through
G/[G_{c},G_{c}]
by the normal subgroup structure of locally moving groups (Proposition
2.1
).
∎

Remark 2.3
.

A concrete consequence of Theorem
2.1
is that for all faithful minimal actions of
G
, the dynamics of individual elements is determined by the way they act in the standard action, via Proposition
2.10
. For this, consider the case where
\varphi
is laminar, positively horograded by the standard action of
G
on
X
(the other case is analogous). Then, we have the following:

—

if
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(g)
accumulates on
b
, then
\varphi(g)
is totally bounded;

—

if
g(x)>x
(respectively,
g(x)<x
) on a neighborhood of
b
, then
\varphi
is an expanding (respectively, contracting) pseudo-homothety;

—

if
g(x)>x
(respectively,
g(x)<x
) for every
x\in X
, then
\varphi(g)
is an expanding (respectively, contracting) homothety.

Remark 2.4
.

Let us comment on the assumption of finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
, which is crucial for Theorem
2.1
. A common reason why this assumption is satisfied is if
G
is itself finitely generated and fragmentable (that is,
G=G_{\mathsf{frag}}
), which boils down to say that
G
can be generated by finitely many elements supported in strict subintervals of
X
. However, if
G
is a locally moving group such that
G\neq G_{\mathsf{frag}}
, the assumption of finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
cannot be replaced by finite generation of
G
: in §
2
, we will provide an example of a minimal laminar action of a finitely generated locally moving group
G
(with
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
not finitely generated), which cannot be horograded by the standard action of
G
.

2.
Overall strategy

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.1
. We will divide the proof into steps, which individually yield some additional information. Before getting into details, let us provide an overview of the strategy. An application of Theorem
1.1
shows that a group
G
as in Theorem
2.1
is locally moving, and every faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which is not conjugate to the standard action must be laminar. To find a horograding, we will analyze the type of the subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
and
G_{(x,b)}
, according to the classification of laminar actions in Proposition
1.10
(this does not depend on
x\in X
). We shall use the assumption that
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is finitely generated to show that one of these two subgroups, say
G_{(a,x)}
, must be totally bounded. This allows to introduce a natural prelamination consisting of the union
\mathcal{L}=\bigcup_{x}\mathcal{L}_{x}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{x}
is the collection of components of
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
. The horograding is then constructed by mapping every
I\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
to the point
x
. Let us start.

3.
Type of subgroups

We begin with the following general result on laminar actions of locally moving groups (note that we do not need here the assumption that
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is finitely generated).

Proposition 2.5
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be locally moving, and
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
a laminar action which is not semi-conjugate to any action of
G/[G_{c},G_{c}]
. Then the type of the subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
and
G_{(x,b)}
(according to the classification of laminar actions in Proposition
1.10
) does not depend on
x\in X
. Moreover, one of the following holds.

(i)

(One-sided domination)
. One of the subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
,
G_{(x,b)}
acts without fixed points on
\mathbb{R}
(and thus it is either focal or horocyclic), and the other is totally bounded.

(ii)

(Balance between sides)
. Both subgroups act without fixed points; in this case they are both horocyclic.

Proof.

Note that since all subgroups in the family
\{G_{(a,x)}:x\in X\}
can be conjugated into each other, the property that one of them is totally bounded does not depend on
x
(for a subgroup of a totally bounded subgroup is still totally bounded), and so is the property that one of them is focal (for an overgroup of a focal subgroup is focal). Because of this, it is enough to show that either case
(i)
or
(ii)
hold for any given
x\in X
, and the independence of the type on
x
follows.

Fix
x\in X
. Since
\varphi
is laminar, it is not semi-conjugate to the standard action (as the latter is locally moving). By assumption, it cannot be conjugate to an action of a proper quotient. Hence, by Proposition
1.1
, at least one of the two subgroups
G_{(a,x)},G_{(x,b)}
must act without fixed points on
\mathbb{R}
.

Suppose first that one of them has fixed points and the other does not. Let us assume that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(a,x)})\neq\varnothing
. Then Proposition
1.10
implies that
G_{(a,x)}
is either totally bounded, or pseudo-homothetic. In the second case,
\varphi(G_{(x,b)})
would preserve the compact set
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(a,x)})
and thus fix its largest point, contradicting the assumption
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(x,b)})=\varnothing
. Thus
G_{(a,x)}
can only be totally bounded, and we are in case
(i)
.

Suppose now that both
G_{(a,x)}
and
G_{(x,b)}
act without fixed points, and thus are either horocyclic or focal. Assume by contradiction that one of them, say
G_{(a,x)}
, is focal. Then it admits a non-discrete minimal invariant set
\Lambda
. We can therefore apply Lemma
1.2
to
G_{(a,x)}\times G_{(x,b)}
. Note that cases
(i)
and
(ii)
in Lemma
1.2
imply the existence of elements without fixed points, which do not exist in a laminar action (Lemma
1.9
). It follows that
G_{(x,b)}
pointwise fixes
\Lambda
, contradicting that it has no fixed points. Thus both subgroups are necessarily horocyclic.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

Recall from Proposition
1.10
that all finitely generated subgroups of a horocyclic group are totally bounded. Thus Proposition
2.5
gives in either case that one of the two subgroups
G_{+}=\bigcup_{x}G_{(a,x)}
and
G_{-}=\bigcup_{x}G_{(x,b)}
must have the property that all of its finitely generated subgroups are totally bounded. The reader might compare this with Proposition
2.5
, which provides the same conclusion for the smallest subgroup
[G_{c},G_{c}]
, and played an important role in Part
1
. Proposition
2.5
provides a strengthening and a more precise explanation of that statement.

In view of Proposition
2.5
, for the rest of this section we shall say that a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo(X)
has one-sided domination if case
(i)
holds. When needed, we will specify the dominating side by saying, for instance, that
\varphi
has
right-hand side domination
if
G_{(x,b)}
acts without fixed points.

The next lemma is where finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is crucially used (in both conclusions).

Lemma 2.7

(Key lemma)
.

Let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a subgroup acting minimally on an open interval
X=(a,b)
, with
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
non-trivial and finitely generated. Then the following hold.

(i)

The standard action of
G
on
X
is locally moving.

(ii)

Every faithful minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
has one-sided domination (in the sense of Proposition
2.5
).

Proof.

Recalling that
G_{\mathsf{frag}}=G_{-}G_{+}
, let us fix a finite symmetric generating set
S
of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
of the form
S=S_{-}\cup S_{+}
, with
S_{\pm}\subset G_{\pm}
, and then choose
x_{+},x_{-}\in X
such that
S_{+}\subset G_{(a,x_{+})}
and
S_{-}\subset G_{(x_{-},b)}
.

To prove
(i)
, note first that as
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is non-trivial, at least one of the two subgroups
G_{-}
and
G_{+}
is non-trivial, say
G_{-}
. Irreducibility of the action of
G
easily implies that
G_{-}
is not finitely generated, so finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
gives that
G_{\mathsf{frag}}\neq G_{-}
. So also
G_{+}
is non-trivial, and not finitely generated. This easily implies that we can choose
g_{1}\in G_{+}
and
g_{2}\in G_{-}
that do not commute, so that
[g_{1},g_{2}]
is a non-trivial element of
G_{-}\cap G_{+}=G_{c}
. By Proposition
1.4
,
G
is micro-supported. By Theorem
1.1
, if
G
is not locally moving, its standard action must be laminar. Now, as the standard action of
G
is minimal and faithful, the normal subgroup
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
acts without fixed points. As
G_{\mathsf{frag}}=\langle S_{-},S_{+}\rangle
, we deduce that
\langle S_{+}\rangle
must act without fixed points on a neighborhood of
a
, and trivially on a neighborhood of
b
(and
viceversa
for
\langle S_{-}\rangle
). After Proposition
1.10
, this behavior is not possible in a laminar action. Hence
G
is locally moving.

We next show
(ii)
. Fix a
\varphi
-invariant covering lamination
\mathcal{L}
. By Proposition
2.5
, we can suppose, without loss of generality, that the subgroups of the form
G_{(x,b)}
act without fixed points. If so, Proposition
2.5
already tells us that the subgroups
G_{(x,b)}
are either focal or horocyclic. Our goal is to show that we are in the first case.

Claim 1
.

The
\varphi
-action of
{G_{\mathsf{frag}}}
is focal.

Proof of claim.

Since
\varphi
is faithful and minimal, and
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is normal, the restriction
\varphi|_{G_{\mathsf{frag}}}
must be irreducible. Hence, finite generation of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
implies that it must be focal (see Proposition
1.10
). ∎

Claim 2
.

The
\varphi
-action of
{G_{-}}
is focal.

Proof of claim.

Suppose by contradiction that
G_{-}
is horocyclic. By Proposition
1.10
, the subset
\mathcal{L}_{0}\subset\mathcal{L}
of wandering intervals for
G_{-}
is cofinal and closed in
\mathcal{L}
; moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is
\varphi
-invariant, by normality of
G_{-}
. By Claim
1
and Proposition
1.15
, the
\varphi(G_{\mathsf{frag}})
-orbit of every
I\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
is cofinal. Choose
I
large enough so that the image of
I
under every element of
S
intersects
I
(and hence is related to
I
by inclusion). Then we can find a sequence
(s_{i})\subset S=S_{+}\cup S_{-}
such that
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.I\subsetneq s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}.I
and
\bigcup_{n}s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.I=\mathbb{R}
. But since all intervals
{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.I}
belong to
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, no element of
S_{-}
can send any of them to a strictly larger interval, so the sequence
(s_{i})
must actually be contained in
S_{+}
. It follows that the
\varphi(G_{(a,x_{+})})
-orbit of
I
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
. This is absurd, since
G_{(a,x_{+})}
is either totally bounded or horocyclic (by Proposition
2.5
).
∎

Claim 3
.

The
\varphi
-action of
{G_{(x,b)}}
is focal.

Proof of claim.

For
I\in\mathcal{L}
and
x\in X
, let us set

\alpha(I,x)=\bigcup_{h\in G_{(x,b)},\,I\subset h.I}h.I.

(1)

Observe that
\alpha(I,x)\subset\alpha(I,y)
for
y<x
, and that the function
\alpha(\cdot,\cdot)
is
G
-equivariant:

g.\alpha(I,x)=\alpha(g.I,g(x)).

Note also that the definition of
\alpha(I,x)
and the cross-free property of
\mathcal{L}
easily give that if
I,J\in\mathcal{L}
and
x\in X
are such that
J\subset\alpha(I,x)
, then
\alpha(J,x)\subset\alpha(I,x)
; in particular we have that

if
\alpha(I,x)\supset J
and
\alpha(J,x)\supset I
, then
\alpha(I,x)=\alpha(J,x)
.

(2)

Suppose by contradiction that
G_{(x,b)}
is not focal, and so horocyclic. Then
\alpha(I,x)\neq\mathbb{R}
for every
I\in\mathcal{L}
and
x\in X
. In contrast, since
G_{-}
is focal by Claim
2
, we can choose
I\in\mathcal{L}
whose
\varphi(G_{-})
-orbit is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
.
Since
G_{-}=\bigcup_{x}G_{(x,b)}
, this means exactly that
\bigcup_{x}\alpha(I,x)=\mathbb{R}
. It follows that we can choose
x_{0}<x_{-}
such that for every
s\in S
, we have
\alpha(I,x_{0})\supset s.I
. Since the standard action of
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
on
X
has no global fixed point, we can choose a sequence
(s_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset S
such that the sequence
x_{n}=s_{n}\cdots s_{1}(x_{0})
is strictly decreasing and converges to the endpoint
a
. Note that such a sequence
(s_{n})
will be automatically contained in
S_{+}
, since we chose
x_{0}<x_{-}
. For every
n\geq 1
, we have

\alpha(I,x_{n})\supset\alpha(I,x_{0})\supset s_{n}.I,

and (recall that we have chosen
S
symmetric)

\alpha(s_{n}.I,x_{n})=s_{n}.\alpha(I,x_{n-1})\supset s_{n}.\alpha(I,x_{0})\supset s_{n}s_{n}^{-1}.I=I.

Hence, from (
2
) and induction, we conclude that for every
n\geq 1
we have

\alpha(I,x_{n})=\alpha(s_{n}.I,x_{n})=s_{n}.\alpha(I,x_{n-1})=s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.\alpha(I,x_{0}).

Since
\alpha(I,x_{n})
is an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
and all elements
s_{i}
are contained in
S_{+}
, this implies that the
\varphi(G_{(a,x_{+})})
-orbit of
\alpha(I,x_{0})
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
; this contradicts the fact from Proposition
2.5
that
G_{(a,x_{+})}
is either totally bounded or horocyclic. ∎

By Proposition
2.5
, focality of
G_{(x,b)}
implies that
\varphi
has right-hand side domination.
∎

4.
End of the proof of Theorem
2.1
: construction of the horograding

Strictly speaking, the next lemma could be avoided for the proof of Theorem
2.1
, but it makes the construction more natural, and it shows some good properties of the horograding constructed in the proof.

Lemma 2.8
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a locally moving subgroup. Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a faithful minimal laminar action, and assume that
G_{(a,x)}
is totally bounded. Then
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
is dense for every
x\in X
.

Proof.

For
x\in X
, set
C_{x}:=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(a,x)})
; note that
C_{x}\neq\mathbb{R}
because we are assuming that the action
\varphi
is faithful. Assume by contradiction that there exists
x\in X
such that
C_{x}
has non-empty interior. Fix also an arbitrary
y\in X
and write
H=G_{(a,y)}
. Let
I
be a connected component of
\suppphi(H)=\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus C_{y}\neq\varnothing
. Since
\varphi
is proximal (Proposition
1.15
), there exists
g\in G
such that
I\subset g.C_{x}=C_{g(x)}
. Thus
\varphi(G_{(a,g(x))})
acts trivially on
I
, and since
G_{(a,g(x))}\cap H\neq\{\mathsf{id}\}
, we deduce that the action of
H
on
I
is not faithful. Since
H
is a locally moving subgroup of
\homeo_{0}((a,y))
, from Proposition
2.1
we have that the kernel of this action must contain
[H_{c},H_{c}]
. Since
I
was an arbitrary connected component of
\suppphi(H)
, this implies that
[H_{c},H_{c}]
acts trivially everywhere on
\suppphi(H)
, and thus the
\varphi
-image of
[H_{c},H_{c}]
is trivial, contradicting the assumption that
\varphi
is faithful.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

By the first part of Lemma
2.7
, we have that the group
G
is locally moving, so by Theorem
1.1
, every faithful minimal action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which is not conjugate to the standard action must be laminar.
After the second part of Lemma
2.7
, every such an action has one-sided domination. We take such a
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
with right-hand side domination, and see how to construct a positive horograding by the standard action. The case of left-hand side domination is symmetric, and can be treated by conjugating the standard action by
x\mapsto-x
, thus obtaining a negative horograding.

For the construction, consider the intersection of supports

\Xi:=\textstyle\bigcap_{x\in X}\suppphi(G_{(a,x)}),

which defines a
\varphi
-invariant subset. By Lemma
2.8
and Baire’s theorem, we have that
\Xi
is a
G_{\delta}
-dense subset of
\mathbb{R}
, and in particular it is non-empty.
For every
\xi\in\Xi
and
x\in X
, we denote by
\Iphi(x,\xi)
the connected component of
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
which contains
\xi
. This is well defined for every
x\in X
, by definition of
\Xi
, and is a bounded interval because
G_{(a,x)}
is totally bounded.

Claim 1
.

The collection
\mathcal{L}=\{\Iphi(x,\xi):x\in X,\xi\in\Xi\}
defines a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination, which is covering and thin.

Proof of claim.

By definition, we have
g.\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(g(x),g.\xi)
for every
g\in G
,
x\in X
, and
\xi\in\Xi
. So
\mathcal{L}
is
\varphi
-invariant. Next, if
x,y\in X
are such that
x<y
, then for any
\xi\in\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
we have
\xi\in\suppphi(G_{(a,y)})
and
\Iphi(x,\xi)\subset\Iphi(y,\xi)
. Moreover, for
x\in X
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
, we have either
\Iphi(x,\xi_{1})=\Iphi(x,\xi_{2})
, or
\Iphi(x,\xi_{1})\cap\Iphi(x,\xi_{2})=\varnothing
. This gives that
\mathcal{L}
is a prelamination. Finally, Proposition
1.15
gives that
\mathcal{L}
is covering and thin.
∎

Claim 2
.

The function

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathfrak{h}\colon&\mathcal{L}&\to&X\\
&\Iphi(x,\xi)&\mapsto&x\end{array}

is well defined, and
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a positive prehorograding of
\varphi
by the standard action of
G
on
X
.

Proof of claim.

In order to check that
\mathfrak{h}
is well defined, we need to prove that if
\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(y,\eta)
, then
x=y
. Note first that by Claim
1
, the prelamination
\mathcal{L}
is thin, so for any fixed
\xi\in\Xi
, the length of
\Iphi(x,\xi)
tends to 0 as
x
tends to
a
.
Now, assume by contradiction that
\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(y,\eta)
for some
x<y
. Since
G_{(a,y)}
preserves the interval
\Iphi(y,\eta)
, for every
g\in G_{(a,y)}
we have

\Iphi(y,\eta)=g.\Iphi(y,\eta)=g.\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(g(x),g.\xi)=\Iphi(g(x),\eta).

(The last equality follows from the fact that
\eta
belongs to the first interval in the chain of equalities.)
Since
G_{(a,y)}
acts without fixed points on
(a,y)
, we can choose
g
such that
g(x)
is arbitrarily close to
a
, so the size of
\Iphi(g(x),\eta)
can be made arbitrarily small. This contradicts the previous equality.

The fact that
\mathfrak{h}
is non-decreasing follows from the fact that
x\leq y
if and only if
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})\subseteq\suppphi(G_{(a,y)})
. Finally, equivariance follows from equivariance of the intervals
\Iphi(x,\xi)
: for any
g\in G
,
x\in X
, and
\xi\in\Xi
, we have

\mathfrak{h}(g.\Iphi(x,\xi))=\mathfrak{h}(\Iphi(g(x),g.\xi))=g(x)=g(\mathfrak{h}(\Iphi(x,\xi))),

as desired.
∎

Finally, the positive prehorograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
from Claim
2
can be extended to a positive horograding
(\overline{\mathcal{L}},\mathfrak{h})
as explained in Remark
2.4
, since by Claim
1
we have that
\mathcal{L}
is thin. This concludes the proof. ∎

Remark 2.9
.

For further reference, let us write explicitly what we have proved in this subsection.

For
X=(a,b)
, consider a locally moving subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
and a faithful minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
for which the subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
are totally bounded. Set

\Xi=\textstyle\bigcap_{x\in X}\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})

which is a
G_{\delta}
-dense subset. Consider the following.

—

For
x\in X
and
\xi\in\Xi
, let
\Iphi(x,\xi)
be the connected component of
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
containing
\xi
.

—

Define
\mathcal{L}=\{\Iphi(x,\xi):x\in X,\xi\in\Xi\}
.

—

Define

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathfrak{h}\colon&\mathcal{L}&\to&X\\
&\Iphi(x,\xi)&\mapsto&x.\end{array}

Then,
\mathfrak{h}
is well defined, and
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a positive prehorograding of
\varphi
by the standard action of
G
on
X
, which extends to a positive horograding
(\overline{\mathcal{L}},\mathfrak{h})
.

Although unnecessary for the proof of Theorem
2.1
, let us mention that one can check that the set
\Xi
coincides with the set of
\mathfrak{h}
-complete points (see Definition
2.8
).

3.
Examples of minimal laminar actions: orders of germ type revisited

We have seen in Theorems
1.1
and
2.1
that laminations and horogradings can be used to understand exotic actions of many micro-supported groups. In this section, we show that many such groups (including most groups of piecewise linear or projective homeomorphisms) actually do admit minimal laminar actions. More constructions of laminar actions will be found in the subsequent chapters.

Set
X=(a,b)
and let
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be a (countable) micro-supported group acting minimally on
X
. In order to run our construction, we require
G
to satisfy the following condition.

(
\mathcal{G}
1)

The group
\Germ(G,b)
admits a
section
inside
\homeo_{0}(X)
, namely a subgroup
\Gamma\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
such that
\Germ(\Gamma,b)=\Germ(G,b)
and which projects bijectively to
\Germ(G,b)
.
2
2

2

The problem of when a group of germs admits a section as a group of homeomorphisms is very interesting. We refer the reader to Mann
[
82
]
for an example of a finitely generated group of germs which does not admit any such section.

Under assumption
(

G

1)
, let
\Gamma\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
be a section of
\Germ(G,b)
, and consider the overgroup
\widehat{G}=\langle G,\Gamma\rangle
. We will proceed by describing an action of
\widehat{G}
and then restricting it to
G
. Note that
\Germ(\widehat{G},b)=\Germ(G,b)
, namely
\widehat{G}
induces the same group of germs at
b
as
G
. The advantage of passing to
\widehat{G}
is that it splits as a semidirect product

\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}_{+}\rtimes\Gamma,

where as usual
\widehat{G}_{+}\subseteq\widehat{G}
is the subgroup of elements whose germ at
b
is trivial.
Using this splitting, we can let the group
\widehat{G}
act “affinely” on
\widehat{G}_{+}
: the subgroup
\widehat{G}_{+}
acts on itself by left-multiplication, and
\Gamma
acts on it by conjugation. Explicitly, if
g\in\widehat{G}
and
h\in\widehat{G}_{+}
, writing
g=g_{+}\gamma_{g}
with
g_{+}\in\widehat{G}_{+}
and
\gamma_{g}\in\Gamma
, we set

g\cdot h=gh\gamma_{g}^{-1}.

(1)

Note that we actually have
g\cdot h=g_{+}(\gamma_{g}h\gamma_{g}^{-1})
, from which it is straightforward to check that this defines indeed an action on
\widehat{G}_{+}
.
We want to find an order
\prec
on
\widehat{G}_{+}
which is invariant under the action of
\widehat{G}
, and then consider the dynamical realization of the action of
\widehat{G}
on
(\widehat{G}_{+},\prec)
. For this we look for a left-invariant order
\prec
on
\widehat{G}_{+}
which is also invariant under the conjugation action of
\Gamma
. We will say for short that such an order is
\Gamma
-invariant
.

Good candidates are the orders of germ type on
\widehat{G}_{+}
described in §
1
. Recall that an order of germ type is determined by a family of left-invariant orders
\{<^{(x)}:x\in X\}
, where for every
x\in X
,
<^{(x)}
is a left-invariant order on the group of germs
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
: the associated order of germ type on
\widehat{G}_{+}
is the order
\prec
whose positive cone is the subset

P=\left\{g\in\widehat{G}_{+}:\mathcal{G}_{p_{g}}(g)\succ^{(p_{g})}\mathsf{id}\right\},

where
p_{g}=\sup\{x\in X:g(x)\neq x\}
. However, not every order of germ type is
\Gamma
-invariant, and this is because for every
x\in X
the stabilizer
\stab_{\widehat{G}}(x)
of
x
acts on
\widehat{G}_{(a,x)}
by conjugation, and this action descends to an action on
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
. In light of this, we are able to produce a
\Gamma
-invariant order of germ type on
\widehat{G}_{+}
if and only if the following condition is satisfied.

(
\mathcal{G}
2)

For every
x\in X
, the group
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
admits a left-invariant order
<^{(x)}
which is invariant under the induced conjugation action of
\stab_{\Gamma}(x)
.

Indeed, suppose that
\{<^{(x)}:x\in X\}
is a family such that the associated order of germ type is
\Gamma
-invariant, then each
\prec^{(x)}
is as in
(

G

2)
. Conversely if we choose such an order
<^{(x)}
as in
(

G

2)
, for
x
in a system of representatives of the
\Gamma
-orbits in
X
, then we can extend it uniquely by
\Gamma
-equivariance to a family
\{<^{(x)}:x\in X\}
which defines a
\Gamma
-invariant order of germ type.

Remark 3.1
.

Here are two simple sufficient conditions for
(

G

2)
.

(
\mathcal{G}
2a)

The group
\Gamma
acts freely on
X
.

(
\mathcal{G}
2b)

For every
x\in X
, every non-trivial germ in
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
has no sequence of fixed points accumulating on
x
from the left (this does not depend on the choice of the element representing the germ).

The fact that
(

G

2a)
implies
(

G

2)
is clear because in this case
\stab_{\Gamma}(x)
is trivial. In contrast, when
(

G

2b)
holds, we can define an order
<^{(x)}
on
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
by setting
\mathcal{G}_{x}(g)>^{(x)}\mathsf{id}
if and only if
g(y)>y
for every
y\neq x
in some left-neighborhood of
x
. Then this is a left-order on
\Germ\left(\widehat{G}_{(a,x)},x\right)
, invariant under conjugation by the whole stabilizer of
x
in
\homeo_{0}(X)
.

Summing up, under conditions
(

G

1)
and
(

G

2)
, we can consider a
\Gamma
-invariant order of germ type
\prec
on
\widehat{G}_{+}
and let
\widehat{G}
act on
(\widehat{G}_{+},\prec)
by (
1
). Passing to the dynamical realization, we obtain an action of
\widehat{G}
, and thus of
G
, on the real line.
This construction yields the following criterion for the existence of exotic actions.

Proposition 3.2
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a finitely generated, micro-supported group acting minimally on
X
. Assume that
\Germ(G,b)
admits a section
\Gamma\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
(that is, condition
(

G

1)
holds) such that
\widehat{G}=\langle G,\Gamma\rangle
satisfies
(

G

2)
.

Then there exists a faithful minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which is not conjugate to the standard action of
G
on
X
.

Proof.

Choose a
\Gamma
-invariant order of germ type
\prec
on
\widehat{G}_{+}
, defined from a family of orders
\{<^{(x)}:x\in X\}
. We let
\psi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be the dynamical realization of the action of
G
on
(\widehat{G}_{+},\prec)
. Set
N=[G_{c},G_{c}]
, which by Proposition
2.1
is the smallest non-trivial normal subgroup of
G
.

Claim
.

For every
x\in X
the group
\psi(N_{(x,b)})
acts on
\mathbb{R}
without fixed points.

Proof of claim.

Let
\iota\colon(\widehat{G}_{+},\prec)\to(\mathbb{R},<)
be an equivariant good embedding associated with
\psi
(Definition
2.11
). Observe that the subgroups
\widehat{G}_{(a,y)}
, for
y\in X
, are bounded convex subgroups of
(\widehat{G}_{+},\prec)
which form an increasing exhaustion of
\widehat{G}_{+}
, thus the interior of the convex hull of every
\iota(\widehat{G}_{(a,y)})
is a bounded open interval
I_{y}\subset\mathbb{R}
, giving an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
. Now given any
x,y\in X
, every
g\in N_{(x,b)}
with
p_{g}>y
satisfies
g\widehat{G}_{(a,y)}\neq\widehat{G}_{(a,y)}
, which in turn implies that
\psi(g)(I_{y})\cap I_{y}=\varnothing
. Since
y
is arbitrary, and the intervals
I_{y}
exhaust
\mathbb{R}
, this implies that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\psi}(N_{(x,b)})=\varnothing
.
∎

As
G
is finitely generated, we can consider a canonical model
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
of
\psi
, which is thus either minimal or cyclic.
Since
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to
\psi
, the claim gives
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(N_{(x,b)})=\varnothing
. Using Proposition
2.1
, we deduce that
\varphi
is faithful, and thus minimal. Moreover, we see that it cannot be conjugate to the standard action of
X
, since
N_{(x,b)}
does have fixed points on
X
.

Finally, arguing as in the proof of the claim, it is not difficult to see that the collection

\mathcal{L}:=\{\psi(g)(I_{y}):g\in G,y\in X\}

is an invariant covering prelamination for
\psi
(thus
\psi
is laminar), see also Remark
1.7
. Since
\varphi
is semi-conjugate to
\psi
, Proposition
1.14
implies that
\varphi
is also laminar.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Working as in §
4
, one can show that
\varphi
can be positively horograded by the natural action of
G
on
X
. In particular, the dynamics of elements in the image of
\varphi
is determined by their dynamics on
X
(see Remark
2.3
).

The criterion given by Proposition
3.2
applies to several classes of examples of micro-supported groups. Let us give a first illustration.
We say that the group of germs
\Germ(G,b)
acts
freely near

b
if every non-trivial germ has no sequence of fixed points accumulating on
b
(this condition does not depend on the choice of the representative; cf.
(

G

2b)
).

Corollary 3.4
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a finitely generated, micro-supported group acting minimally on
X
. Assume that
\Germ(G,b)
is abelian and acts freely near
b
. Then there exists a faithful minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(X)
, which is not conjugate to the standard action of
G
on
X
.

Proof.

We first check that if
\Germ(G,b)
is abelian and acts freely near
b
, then it admits a section
\Gamma\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
which acts freely on
X
. To see this, using that
G
is finitely generated, we can take finitely many elements
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
in
G
whose germs at
b
are non-trivial and generate
\Germ(G,b)
. Up to taking inverses, the assumption that
\Germ(G,b)
is abelian and acts freely near
b
allows to find
z\in X
such that
g_{i}(x)>x
and
g_{i}g_{j}(x)=g_{j}g_{i}(x)
for every
x\in(z,b)
and
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. Choose an element
\gamma_{1}\in\homeo_{0}(X)
which coincides with
g_{1}
on
(z,b)
and has no fixed points in
X
. Take
x_{0}\in(z,b)
and consider the fundamental domain
I=[x_{0},\gamma_{1}(x_{0}))
. As the elements
g_{2},\ldots,g_{r}
commute with
g_{1}=\gamma_{1}
on
I
, they induce an action of
\mathbb{Z}^{r-1}
on the circle
X/\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle=[x_{0},\gamma_{1}(x_{0})]/_{x_{0}\sim\gamma_{1}(x_{0})}
, which can be lifted to an action of
\mathbb{Z}^{r-1}
on
X
commuting with
\gamma_{1}
. In simpler terms, writing
I_{n}=\gamma_{1}^{n}(I)
, so that
X=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}I_{n}
, for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,r\}
we can consider the homemorphism
\gamma_{i}\in\homeo_{0}(X)
defined by

\gamma_{i}(x)=\gamma_{1}^{n}g_{i}\gamma_{1}^{-n}(x)\quad\text{for }x\in I_{n}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(2)

The elements
\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{r}
define exactly the action of
\mathbb{Z}^{r-1}
on
X
which commutes with
\gamma_{1}
, as discussed above.
From the definition (
2
), we see that every
\gamma_{i}
coincides with
g_{i}
on
[x_{0},b)
, and in particular we have
\mathcal{G}_{b}(\gamma_{i})=\mathcal{G}_{b}(g_{i})
. This gives that
\Gamma=\langle\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\rangle
is a section of
\Germ(G,b)
acting freely on
X
. Thus conditions
(

G

1)
and
(

G

2a)
are satisfied, so that the conclusion follows from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

A case in which the previous criterion applies is when
b<\infty
and the group of germs
\Germ(G,b)
coincides with a group of germs of linear homeomorphisms
x\mapsto\lambda(x-b)+b
. This is for instance the case whenever
G
is a subgroup of the group
\PL(X)
of finitary piecewise linear homeomorphisms of
X
. This case can be generalized as follows.

Given an interval
X=(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\operatorname{\mathsf{PDiff}}_{0}^{\omega}(X)
the group of all locally piecewise analytic, orientation-preserving homeomorphisms of
X
, with a finite set of breakpoints in
X
. We also let
\PP(X)
be the subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{PDiff}}_{0}^{\omega}(X)
of piecewise projective homeomorphisms of
X
with finitely many breakpoints, namely those that are locally of the form
x\mapsto\frac{px+q}{rx+s}
, with
ps-qr=1
.

Corollary 3.5
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{PDiff}}_{0}^{\omega}(X)
be a finitely generated micro-supported group acting minimally on
X
. Assume that one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(i)

G
is contained in the group
\PP(X)
of piecewise projective homeomorphisms;

(ii)

the group of germs
\Germ(G,b)
admits a section
\Gamma
contained in
\operatorname{\mathsf{PDiff}}_{0}^{\omega}(X)
.

Then there exists a faithful minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, which is not conjugate to the action of
G
on
X
.

Proof.

First of all observe that
(i)
implies
(ii)
. To see this, assume first that
X=\mathbb{R}
; then
\Germ(G,+\infty)
is a subgroup of the group of germs of the affine group
\Aff(\mathbb{R})=\{x\mapsto ax+b\}
, and thus admits a section inside
\Aff(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\PP(\mathbb{R})
. For general
X
, observe that if we fix
x_{0}\in X
, we can find
A,B\in\PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
which fix
x_{0}
and such that
A
maps the interval
(a,x_{0})
to
(-\infty,x_{0})
and
B
maps
(x_{0},b)
to
(x_{0},+\infty)
. Then the map
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
, given by

H(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}A(x)&\text{if }x\leq x_{0},\\
B(x)&\text{if }x>x_{0},\end{array}\right.

conjugates
\PP(X)
to
\PP(\mathbb{R})
, so that the we can conclude from the previous case.

Now assume that
(ii)
holds, and choose a section
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{PDiff}}_{0}^{\omega}(X)
of
\Germ(G,b)
. Then since non-trivial analytic maps have isolated fixed points, we see that
\widehat{G}
satisfies
(

G

2b)
, thus
(

G

2)
, and we can apply Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The conditions in Proposition
3.2
cannot be dropped: in §
4
we will construct an example of a finitely generated locally moving group
G\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which admits no exotic action. Moreover, this example satisfies
(

G

1)
(but not
(

G

2)
) and its standard action is by piecewise linear homeomorphisms with a countable set of singularities with finitely many accumulation points inside
X
. This shows the sensitivity of Corollary
3.5
to its assumptions.

Chapter 9
A test family of examples: Bieri–Strebel groups on the line

In this chapter we discuss how the previous results apply to a concrete family of examples: Bieri–Strebel groups of the form
G(\mathbb{R},A,\Lambda)
.
Recall from §
3
that we denote by
G(X;A,\Lambda)
the Bieri–Strebel group, consisting of all finitary PL homeomorphisms of an interval
X
, with slopes in the multiplicative subgroup
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and breakpoints and constant terms in a non-trivial
\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda]
-submodule
A\subset\mathbb{R}
(see Definition
3.1
). Our focus here is on the case
X=\mathbb{R}
. The groups
G(\mathbb{R};A,\Lambda)
turn out to be an illuminating test case for both the assumptions and the conclusion of our results, in particular of Theorem
2.1
.

Let us summarize the results of this chapter in a special case. For
\lambda>1
, consider the group

G(\lambda):=G\left(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{Z}[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}],\langle\lambda\rangle_{\ast}\right),

where
\langle\lambda\rangle_{\ast}
denotes the cyclic multiplicative subgroup of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
generated by
\lambda
.
We will begin by revisiting, in §
1
, the concrete construction of faithful minimal exotic actions of Bieri–Strebel groups from
jump preorders
(introduced in §
2
). We shall show that this construction yields actions that are laminar and horograded by the standard action.

The group
G(\lambda)
is finitely generated for every
\lambda
(this follows from the result of Bieri and Strebel
[
11
, Theorem B.7.1]
), but it is not always fragmentable. Its fragmentable subgroup
G(\lambda)_{\mathsf{frag}}
turns out to be finitely generated if and only if
\lambda
is an algebraic number (see Lemma
2.1
). Thus, the assumption of Theorem
2.1
is satisfied by
G(\lambda)
only in this case. We will show the following.

—

When
\lambda
is transcendental, there exists a faithful minimal laminar action on
\mathbb{R}
that cannot be horograded by the standard action (see Proposition
2.3
). This shows that the hypothesis of finite generation of the fragmentable subgroup
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
in Theorem
2.1
is not redundant, and cannot be replaced by finite generation of the group
G
itself.

—

If
\lambda
is algebraic, we will use Theorem
2.1
to show that
G(\lambda)
has exactly three faithful minimal actions on
\mathbb{R}
up to conjugacy: the standard action, and two laminar actions arising from jump cocycles preorders, which are horograded by the standard action (this is a special case of Theorem
3.1
). This provides an application of Theorem
2.1
, for which the conclusion is particularly restrictive.

—

Building on the previous result, we will construct an example of a finitely generated, fragmentable locally moving subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, with the property that all its faithful minimal actions on the line are conjugate to its standard action (Theorem
4.6
). This example is a suitable modification of the group
G(\lambda)
for
\lambda=2
, obtained by allowing PL maps with a countable set of breakpoints (with some control on them). This shows that there exist groups which fall in the scope of Theorem
2.1
, but do not admit exotic actions at all. This should be compared with the results in Chapter
5
(and other constructions in this paper), which imply that exotic actions always exist for many natural classes of micro-supported groups.

1.
Jump preorders revisited

Let us briefly recall the definition of jump preorders on Bieri–Strebel groups, discussed in §
2
. Let
G=G(X;A,\Lambda)
be a Bieri–Strebel group, that we always assume to be countable. The jump cocycle associated with an element
g\in G
is the map
j_{g}
defined in (
1
), namely

\begin{array}[]{cccc}j_{g}\colon&X&\to&\Lambda\\
&x&\mapsto&\dfrac{D^{+}g^{-1}(x)}{D^{-}g^{-1}(x)}.\end{array}

Recall that each
j_{g}
is a finitely supported function, whose support coincides with the set
\BP(g^{-1})
of breakpoints of
g^{-1}
. Recall also the cocycle relation (
2
):

j_{gh}(x)=j_{g}(x)j_{h}(g^{-1}(x)).

We denote by
\mathsf{S}:=\{j_{g}:g\in G\}
the set of jump cocycles. The group
G
acts on
\mathsf{S}
by
g\cdot j_{h}:=j_{gh}
.
The following is a slight generalization of the definition of jump preorders given in §
2
.

Definition 1.1
.

Consider a non-trivial
\Lambda
-invariant preorder
\leq_{\Lambda}\in\LPO(\Lambda)
, with residue

\Lambda_{0}=[1]_{\leq_{\Lambda}}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:1\leq_{\Lambda}\lambda\leq_{\Lambda}1\},

write
\Lambda^{*}=\Lambda/\Lambda_{0}
, and
\lambda\equiv_{\Lambda}\mu
when
\lambda/\mu\in\Lambda_{0}
.
The
positive jump preorder
on
G
associated with
\leq_{\Lambda}
is defined by setting
g\preceq h
if and only if either

—

j_{g}(x)\equiv_{\Lambda}j_{h}(x)
for every
x\in X
, or

—

j_{g}(\overline{x}_{g,h})\lneq_{\Lambda}j_{h}(\overline{x}_{g,h})
, where
\overline{x}_{g,h}:=\max\{x\in X:j_{g}(x)\not\equiv_{\Lambda}j_{h}(x)\}
.

The
negative jump preorder
associated with
\leq_{\Lambda}
is defined analogously, by replacing
\overline{x}_{g,h}
with
\underline{x}_{g,h}:=\min\{x\in X:j_{g}(x)\not\equiv_{\Lambda}j_{h}(x)\}
.

Recall also from §
3
(more specifically, from what mentioned in Remark
2.10
) that given a left-invariant preorder
\preceq
on
G
, its dynamical realization
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is defined as the dynamical realization of the action of
G
on the ordered space
(G/[\mathsf{id}]_{\preceq},\prec)
, where
[\mathsf{id}]_{\preceq}=\{g\in G:\mathsf{id}\leq g\leq\mathsf{id}\}
is the residue of
\preceq
.

Definition 1.2
.

We denote by
\varphi_{+,\leq_{\Lambda}}\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
(respectively,
\varphi_{-,\leq_{\Lambda}}
) the dynamical realization of the positive (respectively, negative) jump preorder associated with
\leq_{\Lambda}
.

Remark 1.3
.

In §
2
we only defined and studied positive jump preorders (simply called jump preorders there), but all proofs given there extend to the negative case by symmetry. In particular, any positive or negative jump preorder is left invariant (Lemma
2.2
), and its dynamical realization is a minimal faithful action, not semi-conjugate to the standard action, whose positive conjugacy class is uniquely determined by the preorder
\leq_{\Lambda}
(Proposition
2.3
).

Remark 1.4
.

Given a preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
on a group
G
, we can consider its opposite preorder, namely the preorder
\leq^{op}\in\LPO(G)
with positive cone
P_{\leq^{op}}=P_{\leq}^{-1}
. Note that when
\leq_{1},\leq_{2}\in\LPO(\Lambda)
are one opposite of the other, their associated positive (respectively, negative) jump preorders are also opposite to each other. Therefore, their dynamical realizations are conjugate (although not positively conjugate).

With the language of horogradings at hand, we can complement the results in §
2
by the following.

Proposition 1.5
.
