are positively conjugate. For clarity, let us denote by
\mathbb{R}_{i}
the line on which
\varphi_{\xi_{i}}
is defined.
First, define a map
h\colon\iota_{\xi_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{\xi_{1}})\to\mathbb{R}_{2}
, by setting
h(\iota_{\xi_{1}}(\xi))=\sup\{\iota_{\xi_{2}}(\eta):\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{\xi_{2}},\eta\preceq\xi\}
, for
\xi\in\mathcal{O}_{\xi_{1}}
. Then,
h
is non-decreasing,
G
-equivariant, so by Lemma
1.8
it extends to semi-conjugacy
h\colon\mathbb{R}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}_{2}
.
Since both actions
\varphi_{\xi_{i}}
are minimal, the map
h
must be a conjugacy.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

After Proposition
2.1
, we say that the action
\varphi_{\xi}\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is the
dynamical realization
of the focal action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
. Given an action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, we will say that
\varphi
is
represented
by a focal action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
if
\varphi
is positively conjugate to the dynamical realization of
\Phi
.

Conversely, we have the following.

Proposition 2.3

(From minimal laminar actions to planar directed trees)
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a minimal laminar action of a countable group. Then there exists a focal action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
on a planar directed tree, representing
\varphi
. If moreover
\varphi
can be horograded by an action
\rho
, then one can choose
\Phi
which can be horograded by
\rho
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the proposition in the horograded case, since every focal laminar action can be horograded by itself (Example
2.5
). Let therefore
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
be a horograding of
\varphi
by an action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Then for every
I,J\in\mathcal{L}
, there is a smallest leaf containing
I\cup J
, that we denote by
I\curlywedgeuparrow J
. The proof proceeds as follows: for every
I\in\mathcal{L}
, take a copy of the ray
[\mathfrak{h}(I),+\infty)
, and consider the quotient space
\mathbb{T}
obtained by gluing pairwise the rays corresponding to
I,J
along the subray
[\mathfrak{h}(I\curlywedgeuparrow J),+\infty)
. Then
\mathbb{T}
is naturally a planar directed tree, and inherits a group action and a horograding. Formalizing this idea and performing all the necessary verification is routine; we include below some further detail for the interested reader.

First note the map
\mathfrak{h}
need not be (strictly) increasing along totally ordered subsets of
\mathcal{L}
: this is not really a problem, but it is a source of more case-by-case analysis. We avoid this possibility with a trick. First, replace
\mathfrak{h}
with the map
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
, defined by
\mathfrak{h}_{1}(I)=\sup_{J\subsetneq I}\mathfrak{h}_{1}(J)
if
I
is non-trivially accumulated by leaves
J\subsetneq I
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{1}(I)=I
otherwise. The map
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
is still non-decreasing and equivariant, moreover it is now continuous from below. Next, consider the subset

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\{I\in\mathcal{L}:\mathfrak{h}_{1}(J)>\mathfrak{h}_{1}(I)\text{ for every }J\supsetneq I\}.

Then
\mathcal{L}_{1}\neq\varnothing
, as for every
I_{0}\in\mathcal{L}
the subset
\{I\in\mathcal{L}:I\supseteq I_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{1}(I)=\mathfrak{h}_{1}(I_{0})\}
has a maximum, by continuity from below of
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
, which belongs to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Further,
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is easily seen to be
\varphi
-invariant and, although not necessarily closed, it is still closed from above, namely the limit of a decreasing sequence in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
. As a consequence, every
I,J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
still have a smallest upper bound in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, which we still denote by
I\curlywedgeuparrow J
. Note also that the restriction of
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is increasing. We shall work with the prehorograding
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{1})
, that we rename
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
from now on.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be the quotient of
\{(I,t)\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathbb{R}:t\geq\mathfrak{h}(I)\}
by the equivalence relation that identifies
(I_{1},t_{1})
and
(I_{2},t_{2})
if
t_{1}=t_{2}\geq\mathfrak{h}(I\curlywedgeuparrow J)
(transitivity of this relation follows easily from the cross-free property of
\mathcal{L}
). We denote by
[I,t]
the equivalence class of
(I,t)
. The order
\triangleleft
on
\mathbb{T}
is given by
v_{1}\trianglelefteq v_{2}
if for some (equivalently, every) choice of representatives
v_{i}=[I_{i},t_{i}]
, we have
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
and
t_{2}\leq\mathfrak{h}(I_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow I_{2})
. It is not difficult to verify, using again the cross-free property of
\mathcal{L}
, that
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
satisfies
(

T

1)
, and that
(

T

2)
is satisfied with

[I_{1},t_{1}]\curlywedgeuparrow[I_{2},t_{2}]=[I_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow I_{2},\max\{t_{1},t_{2},\mathfrak{h}(I_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow I_{2})\}].

Note that the map
(\mathcal{L},\subseteq)\to(\mathbb{T},\trianglelefteq),I\mapsto[I,\mathfrak{h}(I)]
is order preserving and injective (its injectivity uses that
h
is increasing). Since
\mathcal{L}
is separable (for its natural topology), the image
\Sigma
of any countable dense subset satisfies
(

T

3)
. The action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
is given by
g.[I,t]=[\varphi(g).I,\rho(g).t]
, and the map
[I,t]\mapsto t
is a
G
-equivariant horograding, that we still denote by
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
. Note that since every
\varphi
-orbit in
\mathcal{L}
is cofinal (by Proposition
1.15
), and since every point in
\mathbb{T}
has lower and upper bounds in the image of
\mathcal{L}
, it follows that
\Phi
is focal.
To define a planar order on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
, choose
v\in\Br(\mathbb{T})
. Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in E_{v}^{-}
be distinct directions, and choose points of the form
w_{i}=[J_{i},\mathfrak{h}(J_{i})]\in e_{i}
. Then the
J_{i}
must be disjoint (since the points
w_{i}
are necessarily not comparable, as they belong to distinct directions
e_{i}
). We declare that
e_{1}<^{(v)}e_{2}
if
\sup J_{1}<\inf J_{2}
. For a different choice
w_{i}^{\prime}=[J_{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}(J_{i}^{\prime})]\in e_{i}
, we have
w_{i}\curlywedgeuparrow w_{i}^{\prime}=[J_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow J_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}(J_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow J_{i}^{\prime})]
, and again
J_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow J_{1}^{\prime}
and
J_{2}\curlywedgeuparrow J_{2}^{\prime}
are disjoint. This implies that
\sup J_{1}<\inf J_{2}
if and only if
\sup J_{1}^{\prime}<\inf J_{2}^{\prime}
, and hence
<^{(v)}
is a well-defined total order. The planar order
\prec
defined by the orders
<^{(v)}
is clearly
\Phi
-invariant.

Now, let
(I_{n})\subset\mathcal{L}
be a decreasing sequence such that
\bigcap_{n}\overline{I_{n}}=\{x\}
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}
(such a sequence exists by Proposition
1.15
). Then
[I_{n},\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})]
is a decreasing sequence in
\mathbb{T}
, and we claim that it converges to a point
\xi\in\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
. Suppose by contradiction that
[J,t]
is a lower bound of
[I_{n},\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})]
for every
n
. By definition of
\triangleleft
, this implies that
\mathfrak{h}(I_{n})\geq\mathfrak{h}(I_{n}\curlywedgeuparrow J)
, and thus it is equal, since
I_{n}\subset I_{n}\curlywedgeuparrow J
. But since
\mathfrak{h}
is increasing, this is possible only if
I_{n}=I_{n}\curlywedgeuparrow J\supseteq J
for every
n
, contradicting that
\overline{I_{n}}
is decreasing to a point. Now, from the construction of the planar order
\prec
, it follows that the
G
-orbits of
\xi
and
x
, with the respective induced orders, can be
G
-equivariantly identified. Since
\varphi
is minimal, it follows that it is conjugate to the dynamical realization of
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
.
∎

Example 2.4

(Planar directed trees for Plante-like actions)
.

Let us illustrate how to get a focal action on a planar directed tree in our running example of a Plante-like action of a permutational wreath product. As in Example
1.8
, we consider two countable groups
G
and
H
, with an action of
G
on a countable set
X
, and we fix a left-invariant order
<_{H}\in\LO(H)
, and a
G
-invariant order
<_{X}
on
X
such that the
G
-action on
(X,<_{X})
is cofinal. We also take a good embedding
\iota\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
. For every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the collection of functions

\mathsf{S}_{x}=\left\{s\colon(x,+\infty)\to H:s(y)=1_{H}\text{ for all $y>x$ but finitely many exceptions in $\iota(X)$}\right\}.

As a set, we define
\mathbb{T}
as the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathsf{S}_{x}
. We declare that
s\trianglelefteq t
if
s\in\mathsf{S}_{x}
,
t\in\mathsf{S}_{y}
for some
x\leq y
, and
s\restriction_{(y,+\infty)}=t
. With this definition, it is immediate to see that for every
s\in\mathsf{S}_{x}
, the subset
\{u:s\trianglelefteq u\}=\{s\restriction_{(y,+\infty)}:y\geq x\}
is order-isomorphic to
[x,+\infty)
, giving
(

T

1)
. To verify
(

T

2)
, given
s\in\mathsf{S}_{x}
and
t\in\mathsf{S}_{y}
, the restriction
s\restriction_{(x_{*},+\infty)}
, where
x_{*}=\max\{z:s(z)\neq t(z)\}
, gives the desired smallest common upper bound. Finally, we have that the collection
\Sigma=\bigsqcup_{x\in\iota(X)}\mathsf{S}_{x}
is countable, as
X
and
H
are countable, and this gives
(

T

3)
. Note that
\Sigma
coincides with the collection of branching points
\Br(\mathbb{T})
. For
x\in\iota(X)
and
s\in\mathsf{S}_{x}\subset\Br(\mathbb{T})
, we note that
E^{-}_{s}
is in one-to-one correspondence with
H
, the identification being given by the value of
s
at
x
; this allows to put on
E^{-}_{s}
the total order coming from
<_{H}
, defining a planar order
\prec
on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
. A horograding
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
is simply given by
\mathfrak{h}(s)=x
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}
is such that
s\in\mathsf{S}_{x}
.

In order to describe the boundaries
\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}
and
\partial^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathbb{T}
, set

\mathsf{S}=\{s\colon(x,+\infty)\to H:x\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\text{ and }s\upharpoonright_{(y,+\infty)}\in\mathsf{S}_{y}\text{ for every }y>x\},

and notice that the partial order
\triangleleft
naturally extends to
\mathsf{S}
. Denote by
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
the subset of the functions which are minimal for the relation
\triangleleft
. That is, an element
s\in\mathsf{S}
is in
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}
if and only if, either
s
is defined over
\mathbb{R}
or the support of
s
is infinite. There is a correspondence between
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}
and
\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
so that the function
s\colon(x,+\infty)\to H
corresponds to the equivalence class of the ray
y\mapsto s\upharpoonright_{(y,+\infty)}
. Under this correspondence, the
\mathfrak{h}
-complete boundary
\partial^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathbb{T}
is identified with those functions in
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}
whose domain is
\mathbb{R}
. In particular, we can see
\bigoplus_{X}H
as a subset of
\partial^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathbb{T}
(a function
\s\colon X\to H
can be seen as a function
\s\colon\mathbb{R}\to H
with support in
\iota(X)
). Notice that the order induced on
\bigoplus_{X}H
by
\prec
under this identification coincides with that introduced in Example
1.8
.

The permutational wreath product
H\wr_{X}G
acts on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
. Indeed, every
g\in G
sends
\mathsf{S}_{x}
to
\mathsf{S}_{g.x}
by pre-composition with
g^{-1}
, where the action
j\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is the dynamical realization of the
G
-action on
(X,<_{X})
corresponding to the good embedding
\iota
. If
\s\in\bigoplus_{X}H
, then we can see it as a function defined on
\mathbb{R}
as before, and thus
\s
acts on
\mathbb{T}
by pointwise multiplication of functions. It is direct to check that this action preserves the poset structure
\triangleleft
and the planar order
\prec
.

Note that this action naturally extends to
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}
, giving the action of
H\wr_{X}G
on
\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
through the correspondence between
\mathsf{S}^{\ast}
and
\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
. Moreover, the
\mathfrak{h}
-complete boundary
\partial^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathbb{T}
and (the copy of)
\bigoplus_{X}H
are invariant subsets for this action. Indeed, the restriction to
\bigoplus_{X}H
coincides with the action
\Psi
(discussed in Example
1.8
). In other terms, we constructed an order-preserving and equivariant “embedding” of the Plante-like product
\Psi\colon H\wr_{X}G\to\Aut\left(\bigoplus_{X}H,\prec\right)
defined in Example
1.8
, into the restriction to the boundary of an action on a planar directed tree.

Finally, note that the horograding
\mathfrak{h}
satisfies
\mathfrak{h}(g.s)=g.\mathfrak{h}(s)
for every
g\in G
and
s\in\mathbb{T}
, while it is constant on
\bigoplus_{X}H
-orbits.
See Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
The directed tree for the Plante-like action (Example
2.4
).

3.
Simplicial trees and metric
\mathbb{R}
-trees

In this section we clarify how all the notions discussed above relate to the more familiar setting of isometric group actions on simplicial or real trees.

1.
Horogradings and metrics on
\mathbb{R}
-trees

A (metric)
real tree
, or
\mathbb{R}
-tree
, is a metric space
(\mathbb{T},d)
such that every two points
u,v\in\mathbb{T}
can be connected by a unique injective continuous arc
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{T}
, and this arc can be chosen to be geodesic (i.e. an isometric embedding). This notion is well studied; see for instance the survey of Bestvina
[
10
]
. Let us clarify the connection between
\mathbb{R}
-trees and directed trees, in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

Assume that
(\mathbb{T},d)
is a separable metric
\mathbb{R}
-tree. Let
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{T}}
be the
Gromov
(or
visual
)
boundary
of
\mathbb{T}
, namely
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{T}
is the set of equivalence classes of geodesic rays in
\mathbb{T}
, where a geodesic ray is a subset
r\subset\mathbb{T}
isometric to
[0,+\infty)
, and two rays
r_{1},r_{2}
are equivalent if
r_{1}\cap r_{2}
is a geodesic ray. Assume that
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{T}
is non-empty, and choose
\omega\in\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{T}
. The choice of
\omega
naturally defines a partial order
\triangleleft
on
\mathbb{T}
, by the condition that
v\triangleleft u
when
u\in[v,\omega[
, where
[v,\omega[
is the ray from
v
to
\omega
.
Moreover, the choice of a point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
allows to define a
horofunction

\mathfrak{h}_{\omega,z_{0}}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
centered at
\omega
(normalized to vanish on
z_{0}
), by the formula

\mathfrak{h}_{\omega,z_{0}}(v)=d(z_{0},v\curlywedgeuparrow z_{0})-d(v,v\curlywedgeuparrow z_{0}).

(1)

This function is a horograding in the sense of Definition
1.10
, and commonly the level sets
\{v\in\mathbb{T}:\mathfrak{h}_{\omega,z_{0}}(v)=t\}
are called
horospheres
, see for instance the book of Bridson and Haefliger
[
18
, Chapter II.8]
.
Conversely, a directed tree
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
as in Definition
1.1
can always be endowed with a metric that makes it a separable
\mathbb{R}
-tree. Indeed, choose a horograding
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
(see Proposition
1.12
). We can then define a distance on
\mathbb{T}
by the formula

d_{\mathfrak{h}}(u,v)=|\mathfrak{h}(u\curlywedgeuparrow v)-\mathfrak{h}(u)|+|\mathfrak{h}(u\curlywedgeuparrow v)-\mathfrak{h}(v)|.

(2)

This distance turns
\mathbb{T}
into an
\mathbb{R}
-tree; see Favre and Jonsson
[
45
, Proposition 3.10]
. We call a distance of this form a
compatible
\mathbb{R}
-tree metric
on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
, associated with the horograding
\mathfrak{h}
. The focus
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
defines a point in the visual boundary
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{T}
, and
\mathfrak{h}
is a horofunction with respect to
d_{\mathfrak{h}}
. Thus, these two constructions are inverse to each other.

(Note however that the visual boundary
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{T}
does not correspond to the whole boundary
\partial\mathbb{T}
in the sense of Definition
1.3
, but rather to the subset
\{\omega\}\cup\partial_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
, where
\partial_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\ast}\mathbb{T}
is the set of
\mathfrak{h}
-complete boundary points (Definition
1.11
). To recover the whole of
\partial\mathbb{T}
, consider the metric completion
\hat{\mathbb{T}}
of
\mathbb{T}
. Then
\partial\mathbb{T}
identifies with
\partial_{\infty}\hat{\mathbb{T}}\cup(\hat{\mathbb{T}}\smallsetminus\mathbb{T}
).)

Suppose now that
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
is an action horograded by an action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, with associated horograding
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, and let
d_{\mathfrak{h}}
be the associated
\mathbb{R}
-tree metric as in (
1
). Equivariance of
\mathfrak{h}
simply means that
\Phi
must preserve the partition of
\mathbb{T}
into horospheres associated with the focus
\omega
. It is evident from (
1
) that the action on
\mathbb{T}
preserves the metric if and only if the horograding action
\rho
takes values in the group of translations
(\mathbb{R},+)
. More explicitly, if the action is isometric, then every
g\in G
must eventually coincide with a translation along any ray
[v,\omega)
, and
\rho(g)
is equal to its signed translation length (with the convention that
\rho(g)>0
if
g
pushes points towards
\omega
).
This, together with Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
, implies the following.

Proposition 3.1

(Horogradings by translations and actions on
\mathbb{R}
-trees)
.

A minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
can be horograded by an action by translations
\rho\colon G\to(\mathbb{R},+)
, if and only if it is represented by a focal action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
preserving a compatible
\mathbb{R}
-tree metric on
\mathbb{T}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that when a laminar action can be horograded by an action by translations, Proposition
2.10
gives that any pseudo-homothety is is actually a homothety. This fact can also be recovered from Proposition
3.1
, using the classification of isometries of
\mathbb{R}
-trees.

2.
Case of simplicial trees

The simplest type of real trees are simplicial trees. A simplicial tree is a connected graph
\mathbb{T}
without cycles. We identify
\mathbb{T}
with its geometric realization, so that each edge is a subset of
\mathbb{T}
isometric to
[0,1]
. We will say that a directed tree
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
is
simplicial
if
\mathbb{T}
is a simplicial tree of countable degree (that is, the degree at every point is at most countable), and
\triangleleft
is the partial order associated with the choice of an end
\omega\in\partial\mathbb{T}
, where
u\triangleleft v
if
v
lies on the ray from
u
to
\omega
. Note that in this case the set of directions
E_{v}^{-}
below a vertex
v
can be identified with the set of edges at
v
which are in the opposite side of
v
with respect to the focus. Thus, a planar order corresponds to the choice of a linear order of such edges, for every vertex
v
.

As it turns out, there is a particularly explicit characterization of the laminar actions that can be represented by an action on a simplicial planar directed tree. This characterization is given by item
(iv)
in the following result (which can be seen as a partial converse to Proposition
3.5
). We will elaborate on this result in §
1
, when studying examples of laminar actions of Thompson’s group
F
.

Proposition 3.3

(Simplicial laminar actions)
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a minimal action whose image is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

The action
\varphi
preserves a discrete lamination.

(ii)

The action
\varphi
is represented by a focal action on a planar directed tree
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
, such that
\mathbb{T}
is a simplicial tree of countable degree, and the action of
G
on
\mathbb{T}
is by simplicial automorphisms.

(iii)

The action
\varphi
is laminar, horograded by a cyclic action
\rho\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
.

(iv)

There exists a non-trivial normal subgroup
N\unlhd G
such that
G/N\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and
\varphi(N)
admits no minimal invariant set.

Definition 3.4
.

We shall say that a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is
simplicial
if it satisfies one of the equivalent conditions in Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Too see that
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
, note that a discrete lamination
\mathcal{L}
is naturally the vertex set of a simplicial tree. Namely, for every
I\in\mathcal{L}
, the set
\{J\in\mathcal{L}:I\subsetneq J\}
does not accumulate on
I
, and thus has a smallest element
\hat{I}
. The tree
\mathbb{T}
is obtained by connecting each
I
to
\hat{I}
by an edge. It can be naturally endowed with a
G
-invariant order
\triangleleft
and a planar order
\prec=\{\prec^{J}:J\in\mathcal{L}\}
on
\mathbb{T}
, where two edges
(I_{1},J)
and
(I_{2},J)
with
I_{1},I_{2}\subsetneq J
are ordered according to the order in which
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
appear in
J
. By construction,
\varphi
is conjugate to the dynamical realization of the action on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
.

The implication
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
is a particular case of the discussion before Proposition
3.1
. Indeed, the action of
G
on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
is horograded by the homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to(\mathbb{R},+)
given by the signed translation length in the direction of
\omega
, which has integer values when
\mathbb{T}
is simplicial.

To see that
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
, assume that
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a horograding of
\varphi
by a cyclic action
\rho\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
, and let
N=\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\rho
. Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(N)
is a closed
\varphi
-invariant subset and thus, by minimality,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(N)=\varnothing
(as otherwise the
\varphi
-action of
N
would be trivial, which is impossible since
G/N\cong\mathbb{Z}
cannot act minimally on
\mathbb{R}
). Also, no
I\in\mathcal{L}
can have a cofinal orbit under
\varphi(N)
, since
\rho(N)
is trivial. It follows from Proposition
1.10
that
\varphi(N)
has no minimal invariant set.

We now show that
(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i)
. Assume that
(iv)
holds, and choose
f\in G
which projects to a generator of
G/N\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so that
G=N\rtimes\langle f\rangle
. By Proposition
3.5
, the set
\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
of irreducible wandering intervals for
\varphi|_{N}
is a
\varphi
-invariant covering prelamination. Choose
J\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
such that
f.J\cap J\neq\varnothing
(e.g. containing
\xi
and
f.\xi
for some arbitrary
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.) First note that it is not possible that
f.J=J
. Indeed, this would imply that for every
g\in G
, writing
g=hf^{n}
with
h\in N
, we would have
g.J=h.J
for some
h\in N
, and thus
g.J=J
or
g.J\cap J=\varnothing
. But this contradicts the fact that the
\varphi
-orbit of
J
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
(Proposition
1.15
). Therefore
f.J\neq J
, and upon replacing
f
by
f^{-1}
, we may assume that
J\subsetneq f.J
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, write
\mathcal{L}_{n}=\{hf^{n}.J:h\in N\}
, so that
\mathcal{L}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{L}_{n}
is the
\varphi
-orbit of
J
. Since
f^{n}.J\in\mathcal{W}_{\varphi}(N)
, any two elements of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
are either equal or disjoint. Moreover, every
I\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
is contained in a unique element of
\mathcal{L}_{n+1}
: indeed,
I=hf^{n}.J\Subset hf^{n+1}.J
. It follows that
\mathcal{L}
is a discrete
\varphi
-invariant lamination.
∎

3.
Examples of simplicial trees: groups with cyclic germs at infinity

Let us revisit the construction of exotic actions in §
3
, and show that it actually gives rise to a simplicial laminar action. As in in §
3
, we consider a locally moving subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
, where
X=(a,b)
, with cyclic germs at
b
. We fix the homomorphism
\tau\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}\cong\Germ(G,b)
and the preferred element
f_{0}\in G
such that
\tau(f_{0})=1
, in such a way that
f_{0}(x)>x
on a neighborhood of
b
.
We then fix a bi-infinite sequence
\s
as in (
1
): we fix
s_{0}
sufficiently close to
b
, such that
s_{n}=f_{0}^{n}(s_{0})
tends to
b
as
n\to\infty
, and set
\s=(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in X^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Recall that in (
2
) we have defined an action of
G
on
X^{\mathbb{Z}}
, by the expression

g\cdot\mathsf{t}=\left(g(t_{n-\tau(g)})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},

and considered the
G
-orbit of
\s
, denoted as
\mathsf{S}
. The relation
\mathsf{t}\prec\mathsf{t}^{\prime}
if and only if
t_{m}<t^{\prime}_{m}
, with
m=m(\mathsf{t},\mathsf{t}^{\prime})=\max\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:t_{n}\neq t_{n}^{\prime}\}
, defines a
G
-invariant order on
\mathsf{S}
(Lemma
3.3
), and we observed that the dynamical realization
\varphi_{\s}\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
of the action of
G
on
(\mathsf{S},\prec)
is faithful and minimal (Proposition
3.4
).
Here we want to see that
\varphi_{\s}
is in fact a simplicial laminar action.

Remark 3.5
.

After Remark
2.9
, we have that the dynamical realization
\varphi_{\s}
can also be positively horograded by the action of
G
on
X
. This is an illustration of the fact that a planar directed tree encoding a laminar action is not unique, and identifying a tree with good properties may be important for some purposes.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, denote by
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}
the set of integers
j\geq n
. We let
\mathsf{S}_{\geq n}\subset X^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}}
be the subset of sequences indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}
obtained by restricting sequences in
\mathsf{S}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}
:

\mathsf{S}_{n}=\{(t_{j})_{j\geq n}:(t_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{S}\}.

We will call
truncation
this operation. Given a sequence
(t_{j})_{j\geq n}\in\mathsf{S}_{n}
, we say that
(t_{j})_{j\geq n+1}\in\mathsf{S}_{n+1}
is its
successor
.
The disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{S}_{n}
is naturally the vertex set of a simplicial tree
\mathbb{T}
, obtained by connecting each element to its successor. Indeed, it is clear that the graph obtained in this way has no cycles; moreover it is connected, because all elements of
\mathsf{S}
eventually coincide with the sequence
\s
(Lemma
3.2
).

If we endow all edges of
\mathbb{T}
with the orientation from a point to its successor, then all edges point to a common end
\omega\in\partial\mathbb{T}
, hence we get a directed tree
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
with focus
\omega
.
Note that every
\mathsf{t}=(t_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{S}
defines a bi-infinite ray of
\mathbb{T}
, whose vertices are the successive truncations of
\mathsf{t}
. For every
\mathsf{t}\in\mathsf{S}
, this sequence converges to
\omega
as
n\to+\infty
. As
n\to-\infty
, it converges to some end
\alpha_{\mathsf{t}}\in\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}=\partial\mathbb{T}\smallsetminus\{\omega\}
. The map
\mathsf{t}\mapsto\alpha_{\mathsf{t}}
is clearly injective, and thus allows to identify
\mathsf{S}
with a subset of
\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}
.

The group
G
has a natural action on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
: for every vertex
v=(t_{j})_{j\geq n}
of
\mathbb{T}
and
g\in G
, we set

g\cdot(t_{j})_{j\geq n}=\left(g(t_{j-\tau(g)})\right)_{j\geq n+\tau(g)}.

Note in particular that if
v\in\mathsf{S}_{n}
, then
g\cdot v\in\mathsf{S}_{n+\tau(g)}
. This action is by simplicial automorphism and fixes the end
\omega
. Observe that if
g\in G
is such that
\tau(g)\neq 0
, then
g
has no fixed point on
\mathbb{T}
, and thus acts as a hyperbolic isometry. On the other hand, if
\tau(g)=0
, then
g
preserves each of the sets
\mathsf{S_{n}}
and acts as an elliptic isometry (indeed, since
g
acts trivially on some neighborhood of
b
in
x
, it must fix all vertices
(s_{j})_{j\geq n}
for
n
large enough).

Let us now define a planar order on
(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft)
. In this case, this just means an order
<^{v}
for every
v=(t_{j})_{j\geq n}\in\mathbb{T}
on the set of edges
E^{-}_{v}
which lie below
v
(i.e. opposite to
\omega
). Fix
v=(t_{j})_{j\geq n}
, and consider two distinct edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in E_{v}^{-}
. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
e_{i}=(w,v)
for some
w=(t^{(i)}_{j})_{j\geq{n-1}}
with
t^{(1)}_{j}=t^{(2)}_{j}=t_{j}
for
j\geq n
, and
t^{(1)}_{n-1}\neq t^{(2)}_{n-1}
. Thus we set
e_{1}<^{v}e_{2}
if and only if
t^{(1)}_{n-1}<t^{(2)}_{n-1}
. The collection
\{<^{v}:v\in\mathbb{T}\}
defines a planar order
\prec
on
(\mathbb{T},\omega)
which is invariant under the action of
G
.

The map
\mathsf{t}\mapsto\alpha_{\mathsf{t}}
is
G
-equivariant and order preserving, with respect to the order on
\mathsf{S}
and the order on
\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}
induced from the planar order.
Thus the
G
-action on
(\mathsf{S},\prec)
can be identified with an action on an orbit in
\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}
. It follows that
\varphi_{\s}
is represented by the action on the planar directed tree
(\mathbb{T},\prec,\omega)
.

Chapter 11
Groups with many micro-supported actions

The goal of this chapter is to present a generalization of the Brin–Navas group defined in Example
2.3
. The new examples, called
generalized Brin–Navas groups
, serve to show that some results in this article concerning locally moving groups do not extend to the general context of micro-supported groups (notably Corollary
1.2
and Theorem
3.2
fail).

Recall from Theorem
1.1
that if
G\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is a micro-supported subgroup whose action on
\mathbb{R}
is minimal, then either
G
is locally moving or its standard action is laminar.
In this subsection we give a general construction of micro-supported subgroups of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
whose action is minimal and laminar. We will use this construction to illustrate that the class of micro-supported subgroups of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is much more flexible than the class of locally moving groups: we can find groups that admit uncountably many, pairwise non-semi-conjugate, faithful micro-supported actions (in contrast with Rubin’s theorem for locally moving groups, see Corollary
1.2
). Moreover, many of these examples can even be chosen to be of class
C^{1}
(in contrast with Theorem
3.2
). After Theorem
1.1
, these actions are all laminar. In fact, these groups are directly described as groups of automorphisms of directed (simplicial) trees, by adapting the classical construction of Burger and Mozes
[
26
]
, and the related groups defined by Le Boudec
[
70
]
. The crucial point is that the directed trees admit many planar orders which are preserved by our groups.

1.
Construction of laminar actions

We say that a pair
(A,a_{0})
is a
marked alphabet
if
A
is a set and
a_{0}\in A
. Then, we denote by
\mathsf{S}\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
the set of sequences with values in
A
which take the constant value
a_{0}
in all but finitely many terms. Following the notation as in §
3
, we denote by
\mathsf{S}_{n}
the truncations to
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}
of the elements in
\mathsf{S}
. Also, given a sequence
(t_{j})_{j\geq n}
, we say that
(t_{j})_{j\geq n+1}
is its successor. This defines a simplicial directed tree
(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
whose focus
\omega
is defined by the successive truncations of the constant path
\s
with value
a_{0}
.
By abuse of notation, we will identify
\mathbb{T}_{A}
with its set of vertices
\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{S}_{n}
.
Recall that for a vertex
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
, we denote by
E^{-}_{v}
the set of edges below
v
(opposite to
\omega
). For
v=(t_{j})_{j\geq n}
, the set
E^{-}_{v}
is naturally identified with the alphabet
A
, considering the labeling

\begin{array}[]{cccc}j_{v}\colon&E^{-}_{v}&\to&A\\
&[(t_{j})_{j\geq{n-1}},(t_{j})_{j\geq n}]&\mapsto&t_{n-1}.\end{array}

Note also that every
g\in\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
induces a bijection between
E^{-}_{v}
and
E^{-}_{g.v}
. We write
\sigma_{g,v}:=j_{g(v)}\circ g\circ j_{v}^{-1}
for the induced permutation of
A
. Observe that we have the cocycle relation

\sigma_{g,h(v)}\,\sigma_{h,v}=\sigma_{gh,v}.

(1)

Definition 1.1
.

Let
(A,a_{0})
be a marked alphabet,
G\subseteq\Bij(A)
a group of permutations of
A
, and
(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
the directed tree defined above together with the labelings
j_{v}
,
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
. We define the
generalized Brin–Navas group

\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
as the group of all elements
g\in\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
such that
\sigma_{g,v}\in G
for all
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
and
\sigma_{g,v}=\mathsf{id}
for all but finitely many
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
.

Remark 1.2
.

The name comes from the fact that the Brin–Navas group considered in Example
2.3
is isomorphic to the group
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z};G)
where
a_{0}=0\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
G\subset\Bij(\mathbb{Z})
is the group of translations of the integers.

Remark 1.3
.

Note that we omit the marked element
a_{0}
in the notation
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
. This is because we will only consider transitive subgroups
G\subseteq\Bij(A)
, and in this case the definition of
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
does not depend on the choice of the marked element, up to a group isomorphism induced by an isomorphism between the corresponding trees.

We need to define a suitable generating set. For this, given a vertex
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
, denote by
G_{v}\subset\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
the subgroup of all
g\in\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
which fix
v
and such that
\sigma_{g,w}=\mathsf{id}
for
w\neq v
(clearly
G_{v}\cong G
). We then choose an extra generator defined as follows. Note that the shift map
\sigma\colon\mathsf{S}\to\mathsf{S}
, which sends a bi-infinite sequence
(t_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
to
(t_{j-1})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
naturally acts on the set of truncated sequences
\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{S}_{n}
preserving the successor relation. Thus it defines an automorphism

f_{0}\in\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
. It is direct to check that
f_{0}
is a hyperbolic element in
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
, whose axis consists of the geodesic
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
w_{n}=(a_{0})_{j\geq n}
, and that moreover
\sigma_{f_{0},v}=\mathsf{id}
for every vertex
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
.
With this notation set, we have the following.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
(A,a_{0})
be a marked alphabet, and assume that
G\subset\Bij(A)
acts transitively on
A
. Then the group
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
is generated by
G_{w_{0}}
and
f_{0}
. In particular, it is finitely generated as soon as
G
is so.

Proof.

Write
\Gamma=\langle G_{w_{0}},f_{0}\rangle
for the subgroup of
\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
generated by
G_{w_{0}}
and
f_{0}
. We first observe the following.

Claim
.

For every vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
, there exists
g\in\Gamma
such that
g.v_{1}=v_{2}
and
\sigma_{g,v}=\mathsf{id}
for every vertex
v\triangleleft v_{1}
.

Proof of claim.

First notice that, by composing with powers of
f_{0}
and using the cocycle relation (
1
), we can assume that both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
belong to
\mathsf{S}_{0}
.
Similarly, we can also assume that
v_{1}=w_{0}=(a_{0})_{n\geq 0}
; write
v_{2}=(t_{n})_{n\geq 0}
. Since
G
acts transitively on
A
, there exists a sequence
(h_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
G
such that
h_{n}(a_{0})=t_{n}
. Moreover, since
t_{n}=a_{0}
for
n
large enough, we can take
h_{n}
to be the identity for
n
large enough. With abuse of notation, denote by
h_{n}\in G_{w_{0}}
the element with
\sigma_{h_{n},w_{0}}=h_{n}
. Then, the product
g:=\prod_{n\geq 0}(f_{0}^{n}h_{n}f_{0}^{-n})
is actually a finite product and thus defines an element of
\Gamma
, which moreover satisfies
\sigma_{g,v}=\mathsf{id}
for every
v\in\bigsqcup_{n<0}\mathsf{S}_{n}
. It follows directly from the choices that
g.w_{0}=v_{2}
. This proves the claim.
∎

Take a vertex
v_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
. By the previous claim, we can take
g\in\Gamma
so that
g.v_{0}=w_{0}
and
\sigma_{g,v}=\mathsf{id}
for every
v\triangleleft v_{0}
. Then it is direct to check that
g^{-1}G_{w_{0}}g=G_{v_{0}}
. This shows that
G_{v_{0}}\subseteq\Gamma
for every vertex
v_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
. Finally, given
g\in\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
, write

C(g):=|\{v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}:\sigma_{g,v}\neq\mathsf{id}\}|.

Notice that if
C(g)=0
, then
g
is a power of
f_{0}
, hence it belongs to
\Gamma
. Take an element
g\in\mathsf{BN}(G)
with
C(g)>0
, and a vertex
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
so that
\sigma_{g,v}\neq\mathsf{id}
. Then we can find
h\in G_{v}
satisfying
\sigma_{h,v}=\sigma_{g,v}
, and we have
C(gh^{-1})=C(g)-1
. By repeating this procedure finitely many times, we can find
h^{\prime}\in\Gamma
so that
C(gh^{\prime})=0
. We deduce that
g
belongs to the group
\Gamma
.
∎

Notice that for every total order
<
on the alphabet
A
, there exists a unique planar order
\prec
on
(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft)
for which the maps
j_{v}
are order preserving. If in addition the subgroup
G\subset\Bij(A)
preserves
<
, the group
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
preserves the associated planar order
\prec
. We call this action the induced
planar directed tree representation
associated with
G
and
<
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
(A,a_{0})
be a marked alphabet. Assume that
G\subset\Bij(A)
acts transitively on
A
, and let
<
be a
G
-invariant total order on
A
. Then, the dynamical realization of the planar directed tree representation associated with
G
and
<
, is a faithful minimal laminar action of
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
, which is moreover micro-supported.

Moreover, for two different
G
-invariant orders on
A
, the dynamical realizations of their corresponding planar directed tree representations are not positively semi-conjugate.

Proof.

Let
\prec
be the planar order associated with
<
, and let
\Phi\colon\mathsf{BN}(A;G)\to\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{A},\triangleleft,\prec)
be the corresponding action. Since
\Phi
acts transitively on the vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{A}
and
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)
contains a hyperbolic element, focality of
\Phi
follows.

Let
\xi\in\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{A}
be the end defined by the sequence
(w_{n})_{n\leq 0}
, and let
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
be its orbit under the induced
\Phi
-action on
\partial^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{A}
. Since
f_{0}
is a hyperbolic element with axis
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, it acts on
(\mathcal{O}_{\xi},\prec)
as a homothety fixing
\xi
. This allows us to apply Lemma
2.15
to deduce that the dynamical realization of
\mathsf{BN}(A;G)\to\Aut(\mathcal{O}_{\xi},\prec)
is minimal. In other words, the dynamical realization of
\Phi
, that we denote by
\varphi
, is minimal. On the other hand, since
G_{v}
is supported on the set of points below
v
, its associated action on
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
is supported on the shadow of
v
. This implies that the support of
\varphi(G_{v})
is relatively compact, and thus by Proposition
1.4
we deduce that
\varphi
is micro-supported. Finally, faithfulness of
\varphi
follows from that of
\Phi
.

Given an element
h\in G
and a vertex
v\in\mathbb{T}_{A}
we denote by
h_{v}
the element of
G_{v}
satisfying
\sigma_{h_{v},v}=h
. Also, let
p\in\mathbb{R}
be the fixed point of
\varphi(f_{0})
. Then, given
h\in G
, we have

\varphi(h_{w_{0}})(p)>p\quad\text{if and only if}\quad h(a_{0})>a_{0}.

Since the action of
G
on
A
is transitive, this implies that we can read the total order
<
from the action
\varphi
. In particular, dynamical realizations corresponding to different
G
-invariant orders on
A
give rise to non-positively-conjugate actions. Finally, since these dynamical realizations are minimal and not positively conjugate, they are not positively semi-conjugate.
∎

Note that by the transitivity assumption in Proposition
1.5
, the planar directed tree representation is in fact determined by a choice of a left-invariant preorder on
G
, as an abstract group: write the identification
A\cong G/H
, with
H=\stab_{G}(a_{0})
, so that a
G
-invariant order on
A
corresponds to a preorder
\leq
on
G
whose residue (that is, the subgroup of elements which are
\leq
-equivalent to
1_{G}
; see §
2
) is
H
. Hence,
the second part in the statement says that two different left-invariant preorders on
G
with same residue yield non-positively-semi-conjugate actions. As a particular case, one can consider a left-invariant order on
G
, in which case we can identify the marked alphabet with
(G,1_{G})
, and the subgroup
G\subset\Bij(G)
is the group of left translations. In such a case, we will simply write
\mathsf{BN}(G)
for the group
\mathsf{BN}(G;G)
.

Recall that if
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is a locally moving group, then every faithful locally moving action of
G
on
\mathbb{R}
is conjugate to its standard action (by Rubin’s theorem, or by Corollary
1.2
). The groups
\mathsf{BN}(G)
show that the this is far from being true for micro-supported subgroups of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group admitting uncountably many left-invariant orders. Then
\mathsf{BN}(G)
has uncountably many, pairwise non-conjugate, faithful minimal micro-supported actions on the line.

Proof.

After the previous discussion, we can apply Proposition
1.5
to each left-invariant order in
\LO(G)
, which gives the desired result.
∎

2.
Groups with many differentiable micro-supported actions

Here we extend the result given by Corollary
1.6
by showing that for some finitely generated groups
G
, we can actually get many faithful micro-supported actions of class
C^{1}
of the group
\mathsf{BN}(G)
(compare with Theorem
3.2
).

Theorem 2.1
.

There exists a finitely generated group admitting uncountably many, faithful minimal micro-supported actions, which are pairwise not conjugate (and not conjugate to any non-faithful action), and each of which is semi-conjugate to a
C^{1}
action on
\mathbb{R}
.More precisely, the group
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
satisfies such properties.

Remark 2.2
.

In fact, it seems also possible to prove that the group
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
admits faithful minimal micro-supported actions which are
conjugate
to a
C^{1}
action and pairwise not conjugate, but since this is more technical, we will content ourselves of the previous statement (which suffices to disprove the analogue of Theorem
3.2
for micro-supported groups).

Let us also remark that from the point of view of regularity, our construction for
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
relies on the Pixton–Tsuboi examples (see Tsuboi
[
120
]
) and naturally gives actions by diffeomorphisms of class
C^{\alpha}
, for any
\alpha<3/2
. On the other hand, a result of Deroin, Kleptsyn, and Navas (see
[
39
, Théorème C]
) tells that our construction cannot give more regular actions. We are not aware of any method that could give uncountably many, pairwise non-semi-conjugate, micro-supported actions by
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms on
\mathbb{R}
, morally because actions by
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms on
[0,1]
with an exceptional minimal set in
(0,1)
are expected to satisfy many constraints. However, an elementary construction gives the following.

Proposition 2.3
.

For any
n\geq 1
, there exists a finitely generated group admitting (at least)
2^{n}
pairwise non-positively-conjugate, faithful minimal micro-supported actions by
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms on
\mathbb{R}
.

Sketch of proof.

For any integer
n\geq 1
, consider the subgroup
H_{n}
of
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z})
generated by the power
f_{0}^{n}
and the subgroups
G_{w_{k}}=f_{0}^{k}G_{w_{0}}f_{0}^{-k}
, for
k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. The action of
H_{n}
on the directed tree
(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Z}},\triangleleft)
has
n
distinct orbits of vertices (the orbits of
w_{k}
, for
k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
), so it admits at least
2^{n}
distinct planar orders, obtained by taking all possible switching of signs of the standard orders on the sets of edges
E_{w_{k}}^{-}\cong\mathbb{Z}
, for
k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. It is not difficult to see (elaborating on the arguments described in this section) that the dynamical realizations of the actions of
H_{n}
on these planar ordered trees are conjugate to
C^{\infty}
actions on
\mathbb{R}
, and that different choices of orders on the
E_{w_{k}}^{-}
lead to pairwise non-positively-conjugate actions.
∎

We now discuss Theorem
2.1
. It will be obtained as a consequence of Proposition
2.7
, which is
a criterion to recognize faithful actions of
\mathsf{BN}(G)
on the line. As a preliminary result, which may also help to follow the rather technical proof of Proposition
2.7
, we work out a presentation of
\mathsf{BN}(G)
, which is analogous to the one for
\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z})=B
appearing in Example
2.3
.
So let
G
be a finitely generated group, and fix a presentation

G=\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\mid r_{\nu}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\,(\nu\in\mathbb{N})\rangle.

We will assume for simplicity that the generating set is symmetric and that for every distinct
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the generators
g_{i}
and
g_{j}
represent distinct non-trivial elements in
G
. The free product
G\ast\mathbb{Z}
admits the presentation

G\ast\mathbb{Z}=\langle f,g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\mid r_{\nu}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\,(\nu\in\mathbb{N})\rangle.

It is convenient to introduce the following more redundant presentation of
G\ast\mathbb{Z}
, which corresponds to applying a (multiple) Tietze transformation to the previous one. For
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, write
\mathcal{G}_{m}=\{g_{i,m}\}_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
, and consider the union
\mathcal{G}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}_{m}
.
Then, we get the presentation

G\ast\mathbb{Z}=\left\langle f_{0},\mathcal{G}\,\middle|\,fg_{i,m}f^{-1}=g_{i,m+1}\text{ }(i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},m\in\mathbb{Z}),\text{ }r_{\nu}(g_{1,0},\ldots,g_{n,0})\,(\nu\in\mathbb{N})\right\rangle.

(1)

Write
\mathcal{R}_{0}
for the set of relators in the previous presentation. As in the proof of Lemma
1.5
, given
g\in G\subset\Bij(G)
and
v\in\mathbb{T}_{G}
, we denote by
g_{v}
the element in
\mathsf{BN}(G)
fixing
v
such that
\sigma_{g,v}=g
, and
\sigma_{g,w}=\mathsf{id}
for every
w\neq v
. Then we denote by
\Psi_{0}\colon G\ast\mathbb{Z}\to\mathsf{BN}(G)
the morphism such that
\Psi_{0}(f)=f_{0}
and
\Psi_{0}(g_{i,m})=f_{0}^{m}(g_{i})_{w_{0}}f_{0}^{-m}
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
(as before, we write
w_{0}=(1_{G})_{n\geq 0}
).

In order to complete the set of relations for the desired presentation of
\mathsf{BN}(G)
, we need to study the support of some elements.

Lemma 2.4
.

With notation as above, fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for every
g\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{m}\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\bigcup_{q<m}\mathcal{G}_{q}
we have that the commutator
[g_{1},gg_{2}g^{-1}]
is in the kernel of
\Psi_{0}
.

Proof.

We will show that for any such choices of elements,
g_{1}
and
gg_{2}g^{-1}
have disjoint support in
\mathbb{T}_{G}
and hence commute. On the one hand, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
h\in\mathcal{G}_{k}
, the support of
\Psi_{0}(h)
is contained in
\mathbb{T}_{G}^{w_{k}}
, where
w_{k}=(1_{G})_{n\geq k}
, and
\mathbb{T}_{G}^{w_{k}}=\{u\in\mathbb{T}_{G}:u\trianglelefteq w_{k}\}
denotes the subtree of vertices below
w_{k}
. On the other hand, for any
h\in\bigcup_{q<m}\mathcal{G}_{q}
, we have that the support of
\Psi_{0}(ghg^{-1})
is contained in the subtree
\mathbb{T}_{G}^{v_{g,m-1}}
, where
v_{g,m-1}\in\mathbb{T}_{G}
is the vertex corresponding to the sequence
(t_{n})_{n\geq m-1}
such that
t_{0}=1_{G}
for
n\geq m
and
t_{m-1}=g
. In particular, these two remarks apply respectively to the elements
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
from the statement, so that the images
\Psi_{0}(g_{1})
and
\Psi_{0}(gg_{2}g^{-1})
have disjoint supports.
∎

Write
\mathcal{R}_{1}
for the set of all the commutation relators from Lemma
2.4
and set

\Gamma=\langle f,\mathcal{G}\mid\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{1}\rangle.

(2)

We are ready for the following statement.

Proposition 2.5
.

The map
\Psi\colon\Gamma\to\mathsf{BN}(G)
induced by
\Psi_{0}
is an isomorphism.

Before proving Proposition
2.5
we need to fix some notation and state a technical lemma that will also be used later for Proposition
2.7
, and whose proof is postponed. As in the proof of Lemma
2.4
, given
g\in G
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
denote by
v_{g,m}\in\mathbb{T}_{G}
the vertex
(t_{n})_{n\geq m}
such that
t_{n}=1_{G}
for
n>m
and
t_{m}=g
; in particular
w_{m}=v_{1,m}
.
Note that after the conjugation relations in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, the subgroup
H:=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
is the normal closure of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
in
\Gamma
, and the quotient
\Gamma/H
is generated by the image of
f
. Given
\gamma\in H
, we denote by
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{G}}
its word-length with respect to the generating system
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Take a non-trivial element
\gamma\in H
, written as
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
with
\gamma_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{m_{j}}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, and write
M=\max\{m_{j}:j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\}
. Assume that
\Psi(\gamma)(w_{m})=w_{m}
for some
m<M
. Then, there exist
h_{1},\ldots,h_{l}\in\left\langle\bigcup_{m<M}\mathcal{G}_{m}\right\rangle
and pairwise distinct elements
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{M}\rangle
, such that

\gamma=(f_{1}h_{1}f_{1}^{-1})\cdots(f_{l}h_{l}f_{l}^{-1})

(3)

and
\|h_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}<k
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

First notice that, by Lemma
1.4
,
\Psi
is surjective. To prove injectivity of
\Psi
, suppose by contradiction that
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\Psi
is non-trivial. As the tree
\mathbb{T}_{G}
is simplicial, we have a morphism
\rho\colon\mathsf{BN}(G)\to\mathbb{Z}
given by the translation length in the direction of
\omega
(see Proposition
3.3
). Notice that
\rho
vanishes at
G_{w_{0}}=\Psi(\mathcal{G}_{0})
and that
\rho(f_{0})
is non-trivial. As the quotient of
\Gamma
by the normal closure
H
of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is generated by the image of
f
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\Psi\subseteq H
.
In particular, the word-length
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}
is defined on the kernel of
\Psi
, so that we can consider a non-trivial element
\gamma\in\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\Psi
of minimal word-length.
We write
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
with
k=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\gamma_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{m_{j}}
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. As
\Phi(\gamma)
acts trivially, in particular it fixes every vertex of the form
w_{m}
, we can apply Lemma
2.6
to the factorization

\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
, and obtain a decomposition as in (
3
) with
\|h_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}<\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{G}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
. Keeping the same notation as in Lemma
2.6
, we observe that the support of
\Psi(f_{i}h_{i}f_{i}^{-1})
is contained in the subtree
\mathbb{T}_{G}^{v_{f_{i},M-1}}
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
. Thus, since
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
for
i\neq j
, we get that different factors in the factorization of
\gamma
above have disjoint support. We deduce that every such factor is in the kernel of
\Psi
, and therefore also every element
h_{i}
is. However, as the word-length of every such element is less than
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{G}}
, the minimality assumption on
\gamma
implies that every
h_{i}
is trivial, contradicting the choice of
\gamma
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

In order to simplify notation, from here and until the end of the proof, we will write
\prod_{j\in E}\alpha(j):=\alpha(i_{1})\cdots\alpha(i_{k})
, for any function
\alpha\colon E\to\Gamma
, with
E=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
. We will also write
\sigma_{g}
instead of
\sigma_{\Psi(g),w_{M}}
, for every
g\in\Gamma
. Note that
\sigma_{g}=\mathsf{id}
for every
g\in\langle\bigcup_{m<M}\mathcal{G}_{m}\rangle
.
For given
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, we let
i_{j}
be the index such that
\gamma_{j}=g_{i_{j},m_{j}}
.
Notice that
\Psi(\gamma_{j})(w_{M})=w_{M}
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, and in particular
\Psi(\gamma)(w_{M})=w_{M}
. Using the cocycle relation (
1
), we have

\sigma_{\gamma}=\textstyle\prod_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{\gamma_{j}}.

(4)

On the other hand, since
\Psi(\gamma)(w_{s})=w_{s}
for some
s<M
and the action of
G\subset\Bij(G)
on
G
is free, we conclude that
\sigma_{\gamma}=\mathsf{id}
. Since only factors in
\mathcal{G}_{M}
give non-trivial factors in the product (
4
), writing
P=\{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}:\gamma_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{M}\}
, we get the equality

\mathsf{id}=\textstyle\prod_{j\in P}\sigma_{\gamma_{j}}.

Note that for
j\in P
, the permutation
\sigma_{j}
corresponds to the left translation by
g_{i_{j}}
, whence we get
\prod_{j\in P}g_{i_{j}}=1_{G}
. This gives

\textstyle\prod_{j\in P}\gamma_{j}=1_{\Gamma}.

(5)

Next, for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, consider the product in
\langle\mathcal{G}_{M}\rangle
given by

f_{j}:=\textstyle\prod_{l\in P,\,l\leq j}\gamma_{l}.

Set
Q:=\{1,\ldots,k\}\smallsetminus P
, and notice that after the relation (
5
) we can write

\gamma=\textstyle\prod_{j\in Q}\left(f_{j}\gamma_{j}f_{j}^{-1}\right).

(6)

Note that as
f_{j}\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{M}\rangle
, and
\gamma_{j}\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{m_{j}}\rangle
with
m_{j}<M
for every
j\in Q
, we deduce from Lemma
2.4
that we can make any two factors
f_{i}\gamma_{i}f_{i}^{-1}
and
f_{j}\gamma_{j}f_{j}^{-1}
in the product in (
6
) commute, provided
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
. Rearranging factors in this way, we can write

\gamma=\textstyle\prod_{j\in Q_{0}}\left(f_{j}\left(\prod_{l\in Q_{0}^{j}}\gamma_{l}\right)f_{j}^{-1}\right),

(7)

where
Q_{0}\subseteq Q
is a section of the map
Q\to\Gamma
given by
j\mapsto f_{j}
, and for
j\in Q_{0}
we set
Q_{0}^{j}:=\{l\in Q_{0}:f_{l}=f_{j}\}
. We claim that the decomposition in (
7
) is the one that we are looking for. First notice that, by the choice of
Q_{0}
,
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
whenever
i,j
are different indices in
Q_{0}
. Secondly for every
j\in Q_{0}
, by definition of
Q_{0}
and
Q_{0}^{j}
, the element
h_{j}:=\prod_{l\in Q_{0}^{j}}\gamma_{l}
belongs to
\left\langle\bigcup_{m<M}\mathcal{G}_{m}\right\rangle
and clearly satisfies
\|h_{j}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq|Q_{0}^{j}|<k
.
∎

Consider now, for a left-invariant order
<
on a group
G
, the directed tree representation
\Phi\colon\mathsf{BN}(G)\to\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{G},\triangleleft,\prec)
associated with
G
and
<
; let
\varphi\colon\mathsf{BN}(G)\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be its corresponding dynamical realization. Pursuing the discussion from the proof of Proposition
1.5
, we extract some properties of
\varphi
, which characterize it up to (positive) semi-conjugacy.

First recall that
G_{w_{0}}\subset\mathsf{BN}(G)
is a subgroup supported on the subset of points below the vertex
w_{0}
. This implies that
J_{0}:=\suppphi(G_{w_{0}})
is a relatively compact interval. Since
\Phi(f_{0})
is a hyperbolic element, the homeomorphism
\varphi(f_{0})
is a homothety (see for instance Remark
3.2
).
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, write
J_{n}:=f_{0}^{n}.J_{0}
; since
f_{0}^{-1}.w_{0}\triangleleft w_{0}
, we have the inclusion
J_{-1}=f_{0}^{-1}.J_{0}\Subset J_{0}
. Finally, since the action of
G
on itself by left translations is free, we get that
G_{w_{0}}
acts freely on
E_{w_{0}}^{-}
, which implies
g.J_{-1}\cap J_{-1}=\varnothing
for every
g\in G_{w_{0}}\smallsetminus\{1\}
. Moreover, the total order
<
on
G
(which coincides with the planar order
\prec^{w_{0}}
on
E_{w_{0}}^{-}
) can be read from the action:

g>1

\Leftrightarrow

g.x>x
for some
x\in J_{-1}
(equivalently, for any
x\in J_{-1}
).

Summarizing we have the following:

(a)

\suppphi(G_{w_{0}})=:J_{0}
consists of a relatively compact interval;

(b)

\varphi(f_{0})
is a homotethy satisfying
J_{-1}\Subset J_{0}
, with
J_{-1}:=f_{0}^{-1}.J_{0}
;

(c)

g.J_{-1}\cap J_{-1}=\varnothing
for every
g\in G_{w_{0}}\smallsetminus\{1\}
;

(d)

given
x\in J_{-1}
and
g\in G_{w_{0}}
, it holds that
g.x>x
if and only if
g>1
.

For the next statement, recall that
\mathsf{BN}(G)
is isomorphic to the group
\Gamma
which, in turn, can be written as the quotient
\Gamma=G\ast\mathbb{Z}/\langle\langle\mathcal{R}_{1}\rangle\rangle
(following the notation in (
1
) and (
2
), see Proposition
2.5
). We denote by
\pi\colon G\ast\mathbb{Z}\to\Gamma
the corresponding projection. We also define the
height
of an element
\gamma\in H=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
as

\mathsf{ht}(\gamma):=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:\gamma\in\left\langle\textstyle\bigcup_{m\leq n}\mathcal{G}_{m}\right\rangle\right\}.

(8)

Notice that
\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)>-\infty
for every
\gamma\in H\smallsetminus\{1\}
. Indeed, if it were not the case, its image under the isomorphism
\Psi
in Proposition
2.5
would be trivial, which is not the case.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
(G,<)
be a finitely generated left-ordered group. Consider the free product
G\ast\mathbb{Z}
, and denote by
f
a generator of its cyclic factor. Consider also an action
\varphi_{0}\colon G\ast\mathbb{Z}\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
and assume that it satisfies conditions
(a)
to
(d)
above, with
\langle\mathcal{G}_{0}\rangle
and
f
instead of
G_{w_{0}}
and
f_{0}
, respectively. Then,
\varphi_{0}
factors through the quotient
\pi\colon G\ast\mathbb{Z}\to\Gamma
, inducing an action
\varphi_{1}\colon\Gamma\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which is (positively) semi-conjugate to the dynamical realization of the directed tree representation associated with
G\subset\Bij(G)
and
<
.

Proof.

After Proposition
2.5
, in order to show that
\varphi_{0}
factors through the projection
\pi
we need to check that the elements in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
belong to the kernel of
\varphi_{0}
. That is, we need to check that the elements of the form
[g_{1},gg_{2}g^{-1}]
are in the kernel of
\varphi_{0}
, whenever
g\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{m}\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\bigcup_{q<m}\mathcal{G}_{q}
. We closely follow the proof of Lemma
2.4
. On the one hand, for every
q<m
and
h\in\mathcal{G}_{q}
, there exists
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
such that
h=f^{q}h^{\prime}f^{-q}
, so that by condition
(a)
, we have that the support of
\varphi_{0}(h)
is contained in
J_{q}:=f_{0}^{q}.J_{0}
, and thus in
J_{m}
after condition
(b)
(and the same argument for
q=m
).
On the other hand, for any
g\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{m}\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
, condition
(c)
gives that
g.J_{m-1}\cap J_{m-1}=\varnothing
. Thus, the support of
\varphi_{0}(gg_{2}g^{-1})
is disjoint from
J_{m}
. Putting this all together, we get that the support of
\varphi_{0}(g_{1})
and that of
\varphi_{0}(gg_{2}g^{-1})
are disjoint, as desired.

As in the statement, denote by
\varphi_{1}\colon\Gamma\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
the action induced by
\varphi_{0}
, and let
\mathcal{I}
be the orbit of
J_{0}
under
\varphi_{1}(\Gamma)
.
Let also
\Phi\colon\Gamma\to\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{G},\triangleleft,\prec)
be the directed tree representation associated with
G
and
<
, and denote by
\varphi
the dynamical realization of
\Phi
. Our goal is to show that
\varphi
and
\varphi_{1}
are semi-conjugate. This will be a direct consequence of the following statement.

Claim
.

The family of intervals
\mathcal{I}
is a covering prelamination, which determines a simplicial planar directed tree, order-isomorphic to
(\mathbb{T}_{G},\triangleleft,\prec)
via a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism.

Proof of claim.

We consider the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}F\colon&\mathbb{T}_{G}&\to&\mathcal{I}\\
&g.w_{0}&\mapsto&g.J_{0}\end{array}

and then prove that it gives the desired
\Gamma
-equivariant order-isomorphism.
To simplify notation, given
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
, we write
I_{1}<I_{2}
if
\sup I_{1}\leq\inf I_{2}
. Also, given two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{G}
, we write
v_{1}\prec v_{2}
if for every points
\xi_{1}\in\partial U_{v_{1}}
and
\xi_{2}\in\partial U_{v_{2}}
in the shadows, we have
\xi_{1}\prec\xi_{2}
.
We want to show that the map
F
is well defined,
\Gamma
-equivariant, and satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

v_{1}\triangleleft v_{2}
implies
F(v_{1})\subset F(v_{2})
,

(ii)

v_{1}\prec v_{2}
implies
F(v_{1})<F(v_{2})

To see that
F
is well defined, we need to check that
\mathsf{Stab}^{\Phi}(w_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{Stab}^{\varphi_{1}}(J_{0})
.
Given
\gamma\in\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
consider its height
\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)
defined as in (
8
). Notice that if
\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)\leq 0
, then the support of
\gamma
is contained in
\bigcup_{r\leq 0}J_{r}
, and therefore
\gamma\in\mathsf{Stab}^{\varphi_{1}}(J_{0})
. In order to show the inclusion between the stabilizers, we claim that every
\gamma\in\mathsf{Stab}^{\Phi}(w_{0})
can be written as

\gamma=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\quad\text{with }\gamma_{1}\in\mathsf{Stab}^{\varphi_{1}}(J_{0})\text{ and }\mathsf{ht}({\gamma_{2}})<\mathsf{ht}({\gamma}).

(9)

To obtain a decomposition as in (
9
), first note that in the case
\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)\leq 0
we are done. Suppose it is not the case, and write
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
with

\max\{\mathsf{ht}(\gamma_{j}):j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\}=\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)>0.

Then, since
\gamma.w_{0}=w_{0}
and
0<\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)
, we are in condition to apply Lemma
2.6
to the decomposition
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
. Thus, we can write
\gamma=(f_{1}h_{1}f_{1}^{-1})\cdots(f_{l}h_{l}f_{l}^{-1})
with
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\in\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)}\rangle
such that
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
for
i\neq j
, and
\mathsf{ht}({h_{i}})<\mathsf{ht}({\gamma})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
. As discussed above, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
such that
f_{i}\neq 1_{\Gamma}
, we have that the support of
\varphi_{1}(f_{i}h_{i}f_{i}^{-1})
is disjoint from
J_{\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)-1}
and, as a consequence, disjoint from
J_{0}
. Thus, if
f_{i}\neq 1_{\Gamma}
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
we are done. Suppose it is not the case, and that for some
i
we have
f_{i}=1_{\Gamma}
. In this case, by applying the commutation relations in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
from Lemma
2.4
, we can assume
f_{l}=1_{\Gamma}
and we set
\gamma_{1}=(f_{1}h_{1}f_{1}^{-1})\cdots(f_{l-1}h_{l-1}f_{l-1}^{-1})
and
\gamma_{2}=h_{l}
.
Notice that, as we argued in the previous case, the element
\gamma_{1}
fixes
J_{0}
. Finally notice that, by the choice from Lemma
2.6
, we have
\mathsf{ht}({h_{l}})<\mathsf{ht}(\gamma)
. This gives the desired decomposition as in (
9
).

By applying the decomposition as in (
9
) finitely many times, we get a factorization
\gamma=\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{r}
where
\delta_{i}
is in the stabilizer of
J_{0}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
, and
\mathsf{ht}({\delta_{r}})\leq 0
. Finally, since
\mathsf{ht}({\delta_{r}})\leq 0
, we also have that
\delta_{r}
is in the stabilizer of
J_{0}
, and therefore the inclusion between the stabilizers follows. This gives that the map
F
is well defined, as wanted. Moreover, by definition of
F
, we also have that it is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

In order to prove condition
(i)
, first recall that
\mathsf{BN}(G)
acts transitively on the vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{G}
(see the claim in the proof of Lemma
1.4
). Thus, for every vertices
v_{1}\triangleleft v_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}_{G}
, there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma.v_{1}=w_{s}
for some
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, and thus
\gamma.v_{2}=w_{r}
for some
r>s
. Since the partial order
\triangleleft
is preserved by the action
\Phi
, and the inclusion relation is preserved by the action induced by
\varphi_{1}
on
\mathcal{I}
, using
\Gamma
-equivariance of
F
we only need to check that condition
(i)
holds when we take
v_{1},v_{2}
in the subset
\{w_{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. For this, consider
w_{i}\triangleleft w_{j}
; then
F(w_{i})=J_{i}\subset J_{j}=F(w_{j})
, as desired. To prove condition
(ii)
, first notice that, since condition
(i)
holds, it is enough to check the condition taking
v_{1}\prec v_{2}
adjacent to
v_{1}\curlywedgeuparrow v_{2}
. Notice that the relation
\prec
on
\mathbb{T}_{G}
is invariant under
\Phi
, and the relation
<
on
\mathcal{I}
is invariant under the action induced by
\varphi_{1}
. Following the same reasoning as for condition
(i)
, it is enough to check condition
(ii)
taking
v_{1},v_{2}
adjacent to
w_{0}
. In that case, condition
(i)
follows from condition
(d)
in the statement. Summarizing, conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
are satisfied by the map
F
, as wanted.
∎

After the claim, we have that the directed tree representation
\Phi\colon\Gamma\to\Aut(\mathbb{T}_{G},\triangleleft,\prec)
is conjugate to a focal action representing
\varphi_{1}
(technically speaking, its positive semi-conjugacy class, as
\varphi_{1}
need not be minimal and we may be forced to consider a minimal action semi-conjugate to
\varphi_{1}
). This implies that
\varphi
and
\varphi_{1}
are positively semi-conjugate.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Given any irrational
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\mathbb{Q}
, denote by
\tau^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
the action by translations so that
\tau^{\alpha}((1,0))(x)=x+1
and
\tau^{\alpha}((0,1))(x)=x+\alpha
. By the Pixton–Tsuboi examples (see Tsuboi
[
120
]
), for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\mathbb{Q}
, there exists an action
\varphi^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\Diff^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
such that:

—

\varphi^{\alpha}
is supported on
(0,1)
,

—

the restriction
\varphi^{\alpha}\restriction_{(0,1)}
is semi-conjugate to
\tau^{\alpha}
, and

—

\varphi^{\alpha}\restriction_{(0,1)}
has an exceptional minimal set
\Lambda_{\alpha}\subset(0,1)
.

Then, for any irrational
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\mathbb{Q}
, consider an affine expanding homothety
f_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with fixed point in
(0,1)\smallsetminus\Lambda_{\alpha}
so that
f_{\alpha}^{-1}((0,1))\cap\Lambda_{\alpha}=\varnothing
. Consider the free product
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ast\mathbb{Z}
, and denote by
f_{0}
a generator of the cyclic factor. Then, we define the action
\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ast\mathbb{Z}\to\Diff^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
so that
\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}
coincides with
\varphi^{\alpha}
on the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-factor and
\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}(f_{0})=f_{\alpha}
. It is direct to check that the action
\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}
satisfies conditions
(a)
to
(d)
in the statement of Proposition
2.7
, with
G=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and with
<
being the left-invariant order
<_{\alpha}
induced by
\tau^{\alpha}
. Then, applying Proposition
2.7
we conclude that
\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}
induces an action
\Psi^{\alpha}\colon\mathsf{BN}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})\to\Diff^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, semi-conjugate to the dynamical realization of the planar directed tree representation associated with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subset\Bij(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
and
<_{\alpha}
, which is faithful, minimal, and micro-supported (in particular, any action semi-conjugate to
\Psi^{\alpha}
must be faithful). On the other hand, by Proposition
1.5
, different orders
<_{\alpha_{1}}
and
<_{\alpha_{2}}
give rise to planar directed tree representations with non-conjugate dynamical realizations. Therefore,
\Psi^{\alpha_{1}}
and
\Psi^{\alpha_{2}}
are not semi-conjugate.
∎

Chapter 12
A plethora of laminar actions of Thompson’s group
F

Perhaps the most basic example of a finitely generated locally moving group is Thompson’s group
F
(cf. Proposition
3.1
).
Recall that we have defined
F
in §
3
as the Bieri–Strebel group
G((0,1);\mathbb{Z}[1/2],\langle 2\rangle_{*})
.
Some examples of laminar actions of
F
can be obtained by the constructions in §
2
(jump cocycles), §
3
(orders of germ type revisited), and §
3
(groups with cyclic germs at infinity), which already show that
F
admits an uncountable family of non-conjugate such actions. In this chapter, we illustrate the abundance of laminar actions of the group
F
by providing various other constructions, and describing some subtle differences in their dynamical behavior. While we will focus more on some significant examples, the reader will notice that these constructions admit various variations involving several choices, which depend on the data and are not always compatible between them. Trying to take them all into account simultaneously would result in an obscure treatment.
This abundance of laminar actions of
F
may appear surprising when compared with the results in Chapter
9
, where we have seen that the Bieri–Strebel group
G(2)
, whose definition is very close to that of
F
, admits exactly
two
faithful minimal exotic actions up to conjugacy.

As we shall see, an interesting feature shared by many of the constructions that we will provide is that they yield laminar actions of
F
that are
simplicial
in the sense of Definition
3.4
(that is, preserve a discrete lamination). We will see in §
1
various equivalent characterizations of this property, one of them being that the commutator subgroup
[F,F]
does not admit any minimal invariant set for the action. However, we will also construct in §
2
a family of actions which remain minimal in restriction of
[F,F]
(and thus are not simplicial).

1.
Structure of laminar actions of
F

We begin by restating Corollary
2.2
in the special case of
F
. Note that
F
is well known to be fragmentable (for instance, the generators
a
and
b
in the presentation (
1
) can be taken in the subgroups
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
, respectively). Furthermore, the largest quotient of
F
is its abelianization
F^{ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Thus Corollary
2.2
applies and gives the following structure theorem for actions of
F
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Every irreducible action
\varphi\colon F\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is semi-conjugate to one of the following.

—

(Non-faithful)
An action by translations of
F^{ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

—

(Standard)
The standard piecewise linear action of
F
on
(0,1)
.

—

(Exotic)
A minimal laminar action, horograded by the standard action of
F
on
(0,1)
.

Remark 1.2
.

Note that minimal laminar actions of
F
are always faithful, as any proper quotient of
F
is abelian, and minimal laminar actions cannot be conjugate to any action by translations (for instance, because every element has fixed points, see Lemma
1.9
).

Theorem
1.1
gives many constraints on the structure of actions of
F
on
\mathbb{R}
, in terms of the standard action. In particular, it implies that for all exotic actions, the type of all individual elements of
F
satisfy a dynamical classification that can be read from the standard action on
(0,1)
(see Proposition
2.10
). For ease of reference, let us restate this dynamical classification in this special case. For this, recall that given
g\in F
, we denote by
D^{\pm}g(x)
the (right or left) derivative of
g
at a point
x\in[0,1]
, with respect to the standard piecewise linear action.

Proposition 1.3

(Dynamical classification of elements)
.

Let
\varphi\colon F\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a minimal laminar action, positively horograded by the standard action on
(0,1)
. Then the following hold.

—

For every
x\in(0,1)
, the image
\varphi(F_{(0,x)})
is totally bounded. In particular, the
\varphi
-image of every element
g\in F
with
D^{-}g(1)=1
is totally bounded.

—

For every
g\in F
such that
D^{-}g(1)\neq 1
the
\varphi
-image of
g
is a pseudo-homothety, which is expanding if
D^{-}g(1)<1
and contracting otherwise. If moreover
g\in F
has no fixed points in
(0,1)
, then its image is a homothety.

Let us also fix some notation that will be used throughout the chapter. Recall that the commutator subgroup
[F,F]
is simple and coincides with the subgroup
F_{c}
of compactly supported elements, so that the largest quotient
F/[F_{c},F_{c}]
coincides with the abelianization
F^{ab}\cong\Germ(F,0)\times\Germ(F,1)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We choose the identification

(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\colon F^{ab}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Germ(F,0)\times\Germ(F,1)

obtained
by identifying the groups of germs
\Germ(F,0)
and
\Germ(F,1)
with
\mathbb{Z}
, with the convention that
\tau_{x}(g)>0
if and only if the corresponding endpoint
x\in\{0,1\}
is an attracting fixed point of
g
. Explicitly,

\tau_{0}(g)=-\log_{2}D^{+}g(0)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{1}(g)=-\log_{2}D^{-}g(1).

(1)

In addition, we will denote by
f
the element of the generating pair of
F
given by

f(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}2x&x\in[0,\frac{1}{4}],\\[5.0pt]
x+\frac{1}{4}&x\in[\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}],\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}x&x\in[\frac{1}{2},1].\end{array}\right.

(2)

We will also write
1_{F}
for the trivial element of
F
(and we will simply denote it by
1
when there is no risk of confusion).
Since
\tau_{1}(f)
is a generator of
\Germ(F,1)
, we have a splitting

F=F_{+}\rtimes\langle f\rangle.

Remark 1.4
.

A common feature of all our constructions of laminar actions of
F
is the choice of a closed subset
K\subseteq(0,1)
, which is invariant under the
element
f
defined in (
2
).
These sets appear quite naturally with the point of view of focal actions. To understand this, take a minimal laminar action
\varphi\colon F\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. By Theorem
1.1
, we know that it can be horograded by the standard action on
(0,1)
, meaning that one can find a focal action
\Phi\colon G\to\Aut(\mathbb{T},\triangleleft,\prec)
on a directed planar tree, horograded by the standard action, and whose dynamical realization is (conjugate to)
\varphi
.
By Proposition
1.3
, the element
f
fixes a unique end
\xi_{0}\in\partial^{*}\mathbb{T}
, so that it preserves the axis
]\xi_{0},\omega[\subset\mathbb{T}
, which is naturally identified with the interval
(0,1)
via the horograding map
\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{T}\to(0,1)
. In particular, the
\mathfrak{h}
-image of the closure
\overline{]\xi_{0},\omega[\cap\Br(\mathbb{T})}
of the subset of branching points on this axis defines an
\varphi(f)
-invariant closed subset
K\subseteq(0,1)
. Although for some choices of the action
\Phi
, the subset
K
can be the whole interval
(0,1)
, it is not the case in most examples with correct choice of
\Phi
.

2.
A plethora of laminar actions, I: restriction preorders

Starting from now, we will present various constructions of laminar actions of the group
F
and study some of their properties.

1.
A reinterpretation in terms of preorders

Recall that we write
\tau_{1}\colon F\to\mathbb{Z}\cong\Germ(F,1)
for the germ homomorphism given by (
1
), and consider the element
f
of the standard generating pair of
F
given by (
2
).
Recall also the splitting
F=F_{+}\rtimes\langle f\rangle
.
As in §
3
, we can make
F
act on
F_{+}
“affinely”, by letting
F_{+}
act on itself by left translations, and
f
act on
F_{+}
by conjugation. As in (
1
), for
g=hf^{n}\in F
, with
h\in F_{+}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for
r\in F_{+}
, this action is given by
g\cdot r=hf^{n}rf^{-n}
.

Assume that
\preceq
is an
f
-invariant preorder
on
F_{+}
, that is, a left-invariant preorder on
F_{+}
which is also invariant under conjugation by
f
. In particular its residue
H=[1]_{\preceq}
is normalized by
f
, so that the action of
F
on
F_{+}
descends to an order-preserving action on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
, where
\prec
is the total order induced by
\preceq
. Then we can consider the dynamical realization
\varphi\colon F\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
of this action. We have the following equivalence.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon F\to\homeo_{+}(\mathbb{R})
be an action. The following are equivalent.

(i)

\varphi
is a minimal laminar action, horograded by the standard action on
(0,1)
.

(ii)

There exists an
f
-invariant preorder
\preceq
on
F_{+}
such that, writing
H=[1]_{\preceq}
, the map
f
acts as a homothety on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
, and
\varphi
is conjugate to the dynamical realization of the action of
F
on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
.

Moreover, two distinct preorders as in
(ii)
give rise to (positively) non-conjugate minimal laminar actions.

Proof.

Let us prove that
(ii)
implies
(i)
. Assume that
\preceq
verifies the conditions, and let
\varphi
be the dynamical realization of the action of
F
on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
. Since
f
is a homothety on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
, Proposition
2.17
implies that
\varphi
is minimal. Furthermore, the fact that
f
is a homothety on
(F_{+}/H,\prec)
implies that
\varphi(f)
is a homothety. As
\varphi
must be described by one of the cases of Theorem
1.1
, the only possibility is that
\varphi
is laminar, horograded by the standard action on
(0,1)
.

For the converse, let
\varphi
be as in
(i)
. If so, then Proposition
1.3
gives that
\varphi(f)
is a homothety; let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
be its unique fixed point, and consider the preorder
\preceq
on
F_{+}
associated with this point:
g\precneq h
if and only if
g.\xi<h.\xi
. Using that
\xi
is fixed by
f
, we see that
\preceq
is invariant under conjugation by
f
, and that the natural action of
F
on
(F_{+}/[1]_{\preceq},\prec)
can be identified with the action of
F
on the orbit of
\xi
, showing the claim.

Finally note that these two constructions are inverse to each other, and since
\xi
is the unique fixed point of
f
, the preorder
\prec
is uniquely determined by the (positive) conjugacy class of the action.
∎

2.
Restriction preorders on
F_{+}

Let us describe a concrete construction of preorders on
F_{+}
satisfying
(ii)
in Proposition
2.1
. This yields a family of laminar actions of
F
which contains as special cases the constructions in §
3
and §
3
.

Let
K\subseteq(0,1)
be a closed subset. We consider a preorder
\preceq^{K}
on
F_{+}
which is obtained by looking at the restriction of elements of
F_{+}
to
K
, as follows.
We first consider the subgroup
H=\{g\in F_{+}:g(x)=x\text{ for every }x\in K\}
, and for
g\in F_{+}
define

x_{g}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}0&\text{if }g\in H,\\
\sup\{x\in K:g(x)\neq x\}&\text{if }g\in F_{+}\smallsetminus H.\end{array}\right.

We immediately observe that
x_{g}=x_{g^{-1}}
for every
g\in F_{+}
. Moreover, we have the following behavior when considering compositions.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
K\subseteq(0,1)
be a non-empty closed subset, and take
g,h\in F_{+}
. Then we have the inequality
x_{gh}\leq\max\{x_{g},x_{h}\}
, and when
x_{h}\neq x_{g}
the equality
x_{gh}=\max\{x_{g},x_{h}\}
holds.

Proof.

Note that if
x\in K
is such that
x>\max\{x_{g},x_{h}\}
, then
gh(x)=g(x)=x
. This gives the inequality
x_{gh}\leq\max\{x_{g},x_{h}\}
.

Assume now
x_{h}\neq x_{g}
. Since
x_{gh}=x_{h^{-1}g^{-1}}
, upon replacing the pair
(g,h)
with
(h^{-1},g^{-1})
, we can assume that
x_{h}<x_{g}
. Assume first we are in the case
g(x_{g})\neq x_{g}
. Then
gh(x_{g})=g(x_{g})\neq x_{g}
, proving that
x_{g}\leq x_{gh}
, hence
x_{gh}=x_{g}
(using the previous inequality). When

g(x_{g})=x_{g}
, then
x_{g}
is accumulated from the left by points of
K
which are moved by
g
; in particular for every such point
x
with
x_{h}<x<x_{g}
, we have
gh(x)=g(x)\neq x
, giving
x\leq x_{gh}
. Taking the supremum we obtain the desired equality
x_{g}=x_{gh}
.
Note also that the same assumption
x_{h}<x_{g}
(which is equivalent to
x_{h^{-1}}<x_{g^{-1}}
) gives
x_{g^{-1}h^{-1}}=x_{g^{-1}}=x_{g}
. As
x_{hg}=x_{g^{-1}h^{-1}}
, we deduce from the previous case that
x_{hg}=x_{g}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We next introduce the subset

P_{K}=\left\{g\in F_{+}\smallsetminus H:\text{either }g(x_{g})>x_{g},\text{ or }g(x_{g})=x_{g}\text{ and }D^{-}g(x_{g})>1\right\}

(1)

and observe the following.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any non-empty closed subset
K\subseteq(0,1)
, the subset
P_{K}
defines a positive cone in
F_{+}
.

Proof.

We have to verify the conditions in Remark
2.8
. Let us first prove that
F_{+}=P_{K}\sqcup H\sqcup P_{K}^{-1}
. For this notice that, since
x_{g}=x_{g^{-1}}
, we have

P_{K}^{-1}=\{g\in F_{+}\smallsetminus H:\text{either }g(x_{g})<x_{g},\text{ or }g(x_{g})=x_{g}\text{ and }D^{-}g(x_{g})<1\}.

Thus, we automatically get that
H\cap\big(P_{K}\cup P_{K}^{-1}\big)=\varnothing
and
P_{K}\cap P_{K}^{-1}=\varnothing
. It only remains to show that
F_{+}\subseteq P_{K}\sqcup H\sqcup P_{K}^{-1}
. For this, take
g\in F_{+}\smallsetminus H
, so that
x_{g}>0
. If
x_{g}\neq g(x_{g})
we are done. In the complementary case,
x_{g}
must be accumulated from the left by points that are moved by
g
. Since
g
is piecewise linear we must have
D^{-}g(x_{g})\neq 1
, showing that
g\in P_{K}\sqcup P_{K}^{-1}
.
Next, let us check that
P_{K}
is a semigroup and
HP_{K}H\subseteq P_{K}
.

Take
g,h\in P_{K}
, and assume first
x_{h}<x_{g}
. Then Lemma
2.2
gives
x_{gh}=x_{g}
and

gh(x_{gh})=g(x_{g})
. If
g(x_{g})>x_{g}
, we deduce immediately
gh\in P_{K}
; otherwise
x_{g}
is accumulated from the left by points of
K
, which must be fixed by
h
, so that
D^{-}h(x_{g})=1
. Then
D^{-}(gh)(x_{gh})=D^{-}g(x_{g})\,D^{-}h(x_{g})>1
, and we conclude that
gh\in P_{K}
.

Assume now that
x_{g}<x_{h}
, so that
x_{gh}=x_{h}
by Lemma
2.2
.
Consider first the case
h(x_{h})=x_{h}
. Then
gh(x_{gh})=g(x_{h})=x_{h}=x_{gh}
, and as in the previous case
we see that
D^{-}g(x_{h})=1
, so that

D^{-}(gh)(x_{gh})=D^{-}h(x_{h})>1
. If
h(x_{h})>x_{h}
, then

gh(x_{gh})>g(x_{h})=x_{h}
. In both cases we have
gh\in P_{K}
.

Note that the previous argument works also when one of the two elements is in the residue
H
, proving that
HP_{K}H\subseteq P_{K}
.

Finally, consider the case
x_{g}=x_{h}
. As
h(x_{h})\geq x_{h}
and
g(x_{g})\geq x_{g}
, then if any of the two inequalities is strict, we deduce
gh(x_{g})>x_{g}
, and thus
x_{gh}=x_{g}
(by the inequality of Lemma
2.2
) and
gh\in P_{K}
. Otherwise, assume that both
g
and
h
fix
x_{g}=x_{h}
. Then we have the relation
D^{-}(gh)(x_{gh})=D^{-}g(x_{g})\,D^{-}h(x_{h})>1
, showing that
x_{gh}=x_{g}
