r\colon\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))\to\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})

which preserves positive semi-conjugacy classes.

Definition 2.2
.

The map
r
from Theorem
2.1
will be called the
harmonic retraction
of the representation space
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
.

This has an application to the study of local rigidity of representations.
The following notion is classical in dynamical systems.

Definition 2.3
.

An irreducible action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is
locally rigid
if there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\varphi
in
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
consisting of representations all positively semi-conjugate to
\varphi
.

In other terms, every sufficiently small perturbation of the action
\varphi
gives a positively semi-conjugate action. Theorem
2.1
has the following consequence.

Corollary 2.4

(Local rigidity criterion)
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group, and consider a symmetric probability measure
\mu
whose support is finite and generates
G
. For
\varphi\in\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
, let
\psi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
be a normalized
\mu
-harmonic action which is positively semi-conjugate to
\varphi
. If the orbit along the translation flow of
\psi
is open in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, then
\varphi
is locally rigid. The converse holds provided
\varphi
is minimal.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\{\Phi^{t}(\psi):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be the orbit of
\psi
in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
and let
r

be the harmonic retraction (Theorem
2.1
). Then
r(\varphi)
is an element of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
which is positively semi-conjugate to
\psi
and thus belongs to
\mathcal{I}
, i.e.
\varphi\in r^{-1}(\mathcal{I})
. If
\mathcal{I}
is open, then
r^{-1}(\mathcal{I})
is an open neighborhood of
\varphi
consisting only of actions positively semi-conjugate to
\psi
, so the claim follows.
Conversely, assume that
\varphi
is minimal. Since local rigidity is preserved under conjugacy, we can assume that
\varphi=\psi
. Thus if
\mathcal{I}
is not open, using the translation flow we find that
\varphi
can be approximated by
\mu
-harmonic actions in different
\Phi
-orbits. Since actions in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
belonging to different
\Phi
-orbits are not positively semi-conjugate (
(ii)
in Corollary
1.7
), this implies that
\varphi
is not locally rigid.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

The requirement that
\varphi
is minimal in the second part of Corollary
2.4
is essential to ensure that the orbit under the translation flow of its semi-conjugate representative
\psi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
is open.
To see this, consider the action
\varphi\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
obtained as the lift of the ping-pong action
\varphi_{0}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\to\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, given by the action of the fundamental group of a hyperbolic
one-holed
torus on the boundary at infinity of the hyperbolic plane; to make this more concrete, one can consider the subgroup of
\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by the two homographies
\begin{bmatrix}5&3\\
3&2\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&2\end{bmatrix}
.
The action
\varphi_{0}
is then non-minimal and locally rigid, so the same holds for
\varphi
. However, the canonical model of
\varphi_{0}
corresponds to the action of the fundamental group of a hyperbolic
one-punctured
torus on the boundary at infinity of the hyperbolic plane, and so this canonical model has a parabolic element (the commutator of the generators). This can be used to show that this minimal action and its corresponding lift to the line are not locally rigid. Thus the representative of
\varphi
in
\Der_{\mu}(\mathbb{F}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{R})
has non-isolated orbit along the translation flow.

The proof of Theorem
2.1
is postponed to the end of the next section.

3.
Preorders and harmonic actions

Recall from §
2
that given a finitely generated group
G
, we denote by
\LPO(G)
the space of non-trivial left-invariant preorders on
G
. To every preorder, we may associate an action on the line given by its dynamical realization (after Lemma
2.14
, this is well defined up to positive conjugacy). In §
1
, we introduce an appropriate equivalence relation on the set of preorders, such that equivalent preorders give rise to positively semi-conjugate actions (through a semi-conjugacy preserving a marked point). In §
2
, we make explicit the relations between properties of preorders and their dynamical realizations. In §
3
, we investigate the topology of the corresponding quotient space. Finally, in §
4
we identify such a quotient space with the space of normalized harmonic actions, and provide the proof of Theorem
2.1
.
As a by-product of this, we will get that the topology of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
does not depend on the choice of the probability measure
\mu
.

1.
Equivalence of preorders

Part of our discussion in this subsection is close to the exposition of Decaup and Rond
[
35
]
. Before going on, let us recall from Remark
2.8
that a preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
corresponds to a partition

G=P_{\leq}\sqcup[1]_{\leq}\sqcup P^{-1}_{\leq}
,
where
P_{\leq}=\{g\in G:g\gneq 1\}
is positive cone of
\leq
(which is a semigroup), and
[1]_{\leq}=\{g\in G:g\leq 1\leq g\}
is the residue (which is a subgroup of
G
). For further basic terminology, we refer more generally to §
2
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\leq,\preceq\in\LPO(G)
be two preorders on a group
G
. We say that
\leq

dominates

\preceq
if the identity map
\mathsf{id}\colon(G,\preceq)\to(G,\leq)
is order-preserving.

Remark 3.2
.

The direction in the definition of domination could appear counterintuitive, but in fact it is justified when thinking in terms of preorders (it is even clearer in terms of dynamical realization). Indeed, take an element
g\in G
, and suppose that we want to know whether
g\succneq 1
. Then we first check whether
g\gneq 1
(or
g\lneq 1
), and only in the case
g\in[1]_{\leq}
we take the preorder
\preceq
into consideration.

Recall that given a preorder
\leq
on
G
and a
\leq-
convex subgroup
H
, the preorder
\leq
descends to a preorder on
G/H
, denoted as
\leq_{G/H}
, and defines the quotient preorder
\leq_{H}
on
G
by pull-back via the projection
p\colon G\to G/H
(or more concretely, defined by the positive cone
P_{\leq_{H}}=P_{\leq}\smallsetminus H
).

Remark 3.3
.

After Remark
2.4
, the identity map
\mathsf{id}\colon(G,\leq)\to(G,\leq_{H})
is order-preserving and equivariant with respect to the actions of
G
by left multiplication.

Note that when
\leq
dominates
\preceq
, the subgroup
H=[1]_{\leq}
is
\preceq
-convex, thus the quotient preorder
\preceq_{H}
is well defined.
The following lemma characterizes domination in terms of positive cones.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\leq,\preceq\in\LPO(G)
be two preoders on a group
G
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\leq
dominates
\preceq
.

(ii)

The subgroup
H=[1]_{\leq}
is
\preceq
-convex, and
P_{\leq}=P_{\preceq}\smallsetminus H
(so that
\leq
coincides with the quotient preorder
\preceq_{H}
).

(iii)

P_{\leq}\subseteq P_{\preceq}
.

Proof.

We prove that
(i)
implies
(ii)
. As
\mathsf{id}\colon(G,\preceq)\to(G,\leq)
is order preserving, the subgroup
H=[1]_{\leq}
, which coincides with its preimage, is
\preceq
-convex.
Moreover, we have the following inclusions:

P_{\preceq}\subseteq P_{\leq}\sqcup[1]_{\leq},\quad[1]_{\preceq}\subseteq[1]_{\leq},\quad P^{-1}_{\preceq}\subseteq[1]_{\leq}\sqcup P^{-1}_{\leq}.

As
G=P_{\preceq}\sqcup[1]_{\preceq}\sqcup P^{-1}_{\preceq}
, we deduce
P_{\preceq}=P_{\leq}\smallsetminus[1]_{\leq}
. Clearly
(ii)
implies
(iii)
.
Finally, by left invariance, we have
g\preceq h
if and only if
h^{-1}g\in G\smallsetminus P_{\preceq}
, and clearly the same holds for the preorder
\leq
. This gives that
(i)
and
(iii)
are equivalent.
∎

For a given a preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
on a group
G
,
the collection of proper
\leq
-convex subgroups is totally ordered by inclusion. The subgroup
[1]_{\leq}
is always the least such subgroup, however a maximal proper
\leq
-convex subgroup may not exist.
This issue is the order-theoretic analogue of the problem of the existence of a minimal invariant set (Remark
1.10
). To simplify the discussion, we will systematically assume finite generation in the rest of the section, in which case there exists a maximal proper
\leq
-convex subset, that we will denote as
H_{\leq}
.
This leads to the notion of minimal model of a preorder which is an order-theoretic analogue of canonical models for actions on the line.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group. The
minimal model
of a preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
is the quotient preorder
\leq_{H}
, where
H=H_{\leq}
is the maximal proper
\leq
-convex subgroup. The minimal model will be denoted as
\leq^{\ast}
. When the preorder
\leq
coincides with
\leq^{\ast}
, we say that
\leq\in\LPO(G)
is a minimal model.

Remark 3.6
.

Minimal model preorders are exactly those whose structure of convex subgroups is the simplest possible.
Indeed, after Lemma
3.4
, the minimal model dominates the original preorder. Therefore,
H_{\leq}
is the unique proper
\leq^{*}
-convex subgroup of
(G,\leq^{*})
, and thus
H_{\leq}=[1]_{\leq^{*}}
.
Reversely, if
[1]_{\leq}=H_{\leq}
is the unique proper
\leq
-convex subgroup, then
\leq
is a minimal model.

It should be not surprising that the minimal model is unique, in the following sense.

Lemma 3.7
.

Consider preorders
\leq,\preceq\in\LPO(G)
on a finitely generated group
G
, such that
\leq
is a minimal model and dominates
\preceq
. Then
\leq
is the minimal model of
\preceq
.

Proof.

As
\leq
dominates
\preceq
, the subgroup
H=[1]_{\leq}
is
\preceq
-convex, so
H\subseteq H_{\preceq}
. This gives
P_{\leq}\supseteq P_{\preceq^{*}}
, so by Lemma
3.4
, the map

\mathsf{id}\colon(G,\leq)\to(G,\preceq^{*})

is order preserving. Using Remark
3.6
, as
H_{\preceq}=[1]_{\preceq^{*}}
, we deduce that
H_{\preceq}
is
\leq
-convex, and thus that
H_{\preceq}=H
.
∎

We next introduce the following equivalence relation on preorders.

Definition 3.8
.

We say that two preorders on a (finitely generated) group
G
are
equivalent
if they have the same minimal model. We denote by
[\leq]
the equivalence class of
\leq\in\LPO(G)
and we write
[\LPO](G)=\{[\leq]:\leq\in\LPO(G)\}
for the corresponding quotient.

It is immediate to verify that this is indeed an equivalence relation.
We have the following result.

Proposition 3.9
.

Consider preorders
\leq_{1},\leq_{2}\in\LPO(G)
on a finitely generated group
G
. Then, there exists a preorder
\leq
that dominates
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
if and only if they are equivalent.

Proof.

We have that the minimal order
\leq^{*}
dominates
\leq
and thus both
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
. From Lemma
3.7
, we conclude that
\leq^{*}
is the minimal model of both
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
. The converse statement follows directly from Remark
3.6
. ∎

2.
Relations between preorders and actions on the real line

We can now make explicit the relation between minimal models and their dynamical counterpart. For this, note that if
\leq\in\LPO(G)
is a preorder on a finitely generated group
G
, writing
H=[1]_{\leq}
, we can consider a
dynamical realization

\varphi_{\leq}
for the action of
G
on the totally ordered set
(G/H,<_{G/H})
; see Lemma
2.14
.
Conversely, given an irreducible action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
\leq_{\varphi}\in\LPO(G)
the preorder induced by the
\varphi
-orbit of
0
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group and
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
a canonical model. Then the preorder
\leq_{\varphi}
is a minimal model.

Proof.

Suppose by way of contradiction that there exists a proper
\leq_{\varphi}
-convex subgroup
H\neq[1]_{\leq_{\varphi}}
. Then, for each
gH\in G/H
, denote by
I_{gH}\subset\mathbb{R}
the interior of the convex hull of
\varphi(gH)(0)
. Since
H
is
\leq_{\varphi}
-convex, we get that
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\bigcup_{g\in G}I_{gH}
is a proper closed
\varphi
-invariant subset. On the other hand, since
H
strictly contains
[1]_{\leq_{\varphi}}
, the stabilizer in
\varphi(G)
of each
I_{g}
acts non-trivially on
I_{g}
, and this implies that the action
\varphi
is not cyclic, a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

Consider a preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
on a finitely generated group
G
. Then, the dynamical realization
\varphi_{\leq}
is a canonical model if and only if
\leq
is a minimal model.

Moreover, the dynamical realization
\varphi_{\leq^{\ast}}
is a canonical model for
\varphi_{\leq}
.

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
3.11
. We will need a preliminary result.

Lemma 3.12
.

Consider preorders
\leq_{1},\leq_{2}\in\LPO(G)
on a group
G
, with
\leq_{2}
dominating
\leq_{1}
. Then the dynamical realizations
\varphi_{\leq_{1}}
and
\varphi_{\leq_{2}}
are positively semi-conjugate.

Proof.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
we write
H_{i}:=[1]_{\leq_{i}}
and
\iota_{i}\colon G/H_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
for the corresponding good embeddings (see Definition
2.13
). As
\mathsf{id}\colon(G,\leq_{1})\to(G,\leq_{2})
is order preserving, we have
H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}
and the quotient map

i_{0}\colon(G/H_{1},<_{1})\to(G/H_{2},<_{2})

is order preserving and equivariant (here
<_{i}
denotes the total ordered induced by
\leq_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
). Write
\Omega=\iota_{1}(G/H_{1})
, and define
j_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
as

j_{0}(\iota_{1}(gH_{1}))=\iota_{2}(i_{0}(gH_{1}))=\iota_{2}(gH_{2}).

It follows directly from the definitions that
\Omega
is
\varphi_{\leq_{1}}
-invariant and that
j_{0}
is order preserving and equivariant. We conclude using Lemma
1.8
.
∎

Let us immediately point out the following conclusion.

Corollary 3.13
.

For finitely generated groups, equivalent preorders have positively semi-conjugate dynamical realizations.

Proof.

After Lemma
3.12
, the dynamical realizations of equivalent preorders are positively semi-conjugate to the dynamical realization of their minimal model. As positive semi-conjugacy is an equivalence relation, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

Suppose first that
\varphi_{\leq}
is not a canonical model. Then
\varphi_{\leq}
is neither minimal nor cyclic, and so either
\varphi_{\leq}
has an exceptional minimal invariant set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
,
or
\varphi_{\leq}
has a discrete orbit but its image is not cyclic.
We write
\Omega=\iota(G/[1]_{\leq})
for the image of the good embedding
\iota\colon G/[1]_{\leq}\to\mathbb{R}
associated with
\varphi_{\leq}
, and remark that
\Omega
consists of a single
\varphi_{\leq}
-orbit.

Case I
.

\varphi_{\leq}
has an exceptional minimal invariant set.

We first show the following.

Claim
.

\Omega
is contained in
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\Lambda
.

Proof of claim.

We assume by contradiction that
\Omega\cap\Lambda\neq\varnothing
. As
\Omega
consists of a single orbit, by invariance of
\Lambda
we get that
\Omega\subset\Lambda
, and thus
\Lambda=\overline{\Omega}
by minimality of
\Lambda
.
Consider a connected component
I=(\xi,\eta)
of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\Lambda
and note that, by Definition
2.11
of a good embedding, we have
\{\xi,\eta\}\subseteq\Omega
and thus the points
\xi
and
\eta
are in the same orbit. This shows that
\Lambda
is discrete, which is a contradiction.
∎

Consider now the connected component
U
of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\Lambda
that contains
\xi_{0}=\iota([1]_{\leq})
, and write
H=\stab^{\varphi_{\leq}}_{G}(U)
. Note that
H=\iota^{-1}(U)
is a
\leq
-convex subgroup. We will show that
H
strictly contains
[1]_{\leq}
, which by Remark
3.6
implies that
\leq
is not a minimal model. Equivalently, we need to show that
U\cap\Omega
strictly contains
\xi_{0}
. Assume that
\xi_{0}
is an isolated point of
\Omega
, otherwise there is nothing to prove.
Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
be the rightmost point of
U
. Note that
\overline{\Omega}
is
\varphi_{\leq}
-invariant, so we must have
\overline{\Omega}\supseteq\Lambda
. Thus
\lambda\in\overline{\Omega}
. If
\Omega\cap(\xi_{0},\lambda)=\varnothing
, then
(\xi_{0},\lambda)
is a connected component of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\overline{\Omega}
. As
\iota
is a good embedding, this gives
\lambda\in\Omega
, contradicting the claim.

Case II
.

\varphi_{\leq}
has a closed discrete orbit but its image is not cyclic.

Take a point
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
with closed discrete orbit and write
H=\stab^{\varphi_{\leq}}_{G}(\xi)
and
F=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi_{\leq}}(H)
. Note that
H
is the kernel of the morphism
G\to\mathbb{Z}
induced from the orbit of
\xi
.
Since
\varphi_{\leq}
is assumed to be non-cyclic, we have that
F
is a proper closed subset, and it is
\varphi_{\leq}
-invariant, for
H
is normal.
If
\Omega\cap F\neq\varnothing
, then the fact that
\Omega
is a single
\varphi_{\leq}
-orbit gives
\Omega\subseteq F=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi_{\leq}}(H)
; as
\varphi_{\leq}
is a dynamical realization of
\leq
, this implies
H=\varphi_{\leq}([1]_{\leq})
, so the image
\varphi_{\leq}(G)
is cyclic. A contradiction.
Thus
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus F
. More precisely, we have the following.

Claim
.

F=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(H)
is contained in
\overline{\Omega}\smallsetminus\Omega
.

Proof of claim.

Assume by way of contradiction that there is a point of
F
in the complement of
\overline{\Omega}
, and let
I
be corresponding connected component of the complement. As the action
\varphi_{\leq}
is a dynamical realization (Definition
2.13
) and
F=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi_{\leq}}(H)
, this gives that the closure
\overline{I}
is fixed by
H
, so that
\overline{I}\subseteq F
. However,
\iota
is a good embedding, so
\partial I\in\Omega
, which contradicts the fact that
\Omega\cap F=\varnothing
.
∎

We can now argue analogously as in Case
I
to find a proper
\leq
-convex subgroup strictly containing
[1]_{\leq}
.

For the converse, assume that
\leq
is not a minimal model and take a proper
\leq
-convex subgroup
H\neq[1]_{\leq}
. Denote by
U
the interior of the convex hull of
\iota(H)\subset\mathbb{R}
and notice that the orbit
\varphi_{\leq}(G)(U)
is a proper open
\varphi_{\leq}
-invariant subset. Also notice that the stabilizer
\stab_{G}^{\varphi_{\leq}}(U)
acts non-trivially on
U
, which implies that
\varphi_{\leq}
is not a cyclic action. The last two facts together imply that
\varphi_{\leq}
is not a canonical model.

It remains to prove that
\varphi_{\leq^{\ast}}
is a canonical model for
\varphi_{\leq}
. After Remark
3.6
and Lemma
3.12
, the actions
\varphi_{\leq^{\ast}}
and
\varphi_{\leq}
are positively semi-conjugate. On the other hand, by the first part of this proposition, the action
\varphi_{\leq^{\ast}}
is a canonical model.
∎

3.
Topology of the space of equivalence classes of preorders

Recall that we consider
\LPO(G)
as a subspace of
\{\leq,\gneq\}^{G\times G}
endowed with the product topology, and that this makes
\LPO(G)
a metrizable and totally disconnected topological space, which is compact when
G
is finitely generated.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group. The subset

\mathcal{E}=\{(\leq_{1},\leq_{2})\in\LPO(G)\times\LPO(G):[\leq_{1}]=[\leq_{2}]\}

is closed in
\LPO(G)\times\LPO(G)
. Therefore,
[\LPO](G)
considered with the quotient topology is a Hausdorff topological space.

Proof.

Consider a convergent sequence
(\preceq_{n},\preceq_{n}^{\prime})\to(\preceq_{\infty},\preceq_{\infty}^{\prime})
so that
(\preceq_{n},\preceq_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We want to show that
[\preceq_{\infty}]=[\preceq^{\prime}_{\infty}]
and this amounts to show, after Proposition
3.9
, that there exists a preorder that dominates both
\preceq_{\infty}
and
\preceq_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, using Proposition
3.9
we find a preorder
\leq_{n}
that dominates both
\preceq_{n}
and
\preceq_{n}^{\prime}
. As
\LPO(G)
is compact, upon passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
\leq_{n}
has a limit
\leq_{\infty}
. We shall prove that
\leq_{\infty}
dominates both
\preceq_{\infty}
and
\preceq_{\infty}^{\prime}
, and by Lemma
3.4
this amounts to show that
P_{\leq_{\infty}}\subseteq(P_{\preceq_{\infty}}\cap P_{\preceq_{\infty}^{\prime}})
. For this, note that by definition of the product topology, if
g\in P_{\leq_{\infty}}
then
g\in P_{\leq_{n}}
for
n
large enough, which by Lemma
3.4
implies that
g\in P_{\preceq_{n}}\cap P_{\preceq_{n}^{\prime}}
for
n
large enough. This implies that
g\in P_{\preceq_{\infty}}\cap P_{\preceq_{\infty}^{\prime}}
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Consider left-invariant preorders
(\leq_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\leq
on a finitely generated group
G
. Assume that for every finite subset
F\subset P_{\leq}
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F\subset P_{\leq_{n}}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
. Then
[\leq_{n}]\to[\leq]
in
[\LPO](G)
as
n\to\infty
.

In particular, if
\varphi_{n},\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
are representations such that
\varphi_{n}\to\varphi
in the compact-open topology, then we get
[\leq_{\varphi_{n}}]\to[\leq_{\varphi}]
in
[\LPO](G)
, as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, we have that the limit of every convergent subsequence
\leq_{{k_{n}}}\to\leq_{\infty}
satisfies
P_{\leq}\subseteq P_{\leq_{\infty}}
. This implies that
\leq
dominates
\leq_{\infty}
and therefore, by Proposition
3.9
, we get
[\leq]=[\leq_{\infty}]
. Since every convergent subsequence of
(\leq_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a preorder equivalent to
\leq
, we conclude that
[<_{n}]\to[<]
in
[\LPO](G)
, as desired.
∎

4.
Identification with the space of normalized harmonic actions and harmonic retraction

As in Assumption
1.1
, we fix a finitely generated group
G
and a symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
G
whose support is finite and generates
G
.
We will show that
[\LPO](G)
with the quotient topology is homeomorphic to
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. In particular, this will show that the topology of the space of normalized harmonic actions does not depend on the choice of the probability measure
\mu
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Under Assumption
1.1
, the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
is homeomorphic to
[\LPO](G)
. In particular, the homeomorphism type of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
does not depend on
\mu
.

Proof.

Consider the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}I\colon&\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})&\to&[\LPO](G)\\
&\varphi&\mapsto&[\leq_{\varphi}].\end{array}

We will show that
I
is a homeomorphism. By Proposition
3.11
we know that if
\preceq\in\LPO(G)
is a minimal model, then
\varphi_{\preceq}
is a canonical model. On the other hand, by Proposition
1.3
, we can choose
\varphi_{\preceq}
to be
\mu
-harmonic.
We introduce the map

J\colon[\LPO](G)\to\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),

(1)

where
J
assigns to
[\preceq]
the
\mu
-harmonic dynamical realization of
\preceq^{\ast}
, whose associated good embedding satisfies
\iota_{\preceq^{\ast}}([1]_{\preceq^{\ast}})=0
.
We will show that
J
is the inverse of
I
.
The equality
I\circ J=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}
is given by the fact that

\leq_{\varphi_{\leq}}
coincides with
\leq
for every preorder
\leq\in\LPO(G)
, which is a direct consequence of the definitions.

To verify that
J\circ I=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}
, fix
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. Since
\varphi
is a canonical model (Proposition
1.3
), Proposition
3.10
implies that
\leq_{\varphi}
is a minimal model. Therefore,
J([\leq_{\varphi}])
is a dynamical realization of
\leq_{\varphi}
. Set
\Omega=\varphi(G)(0)
, and notice that there exists an order-preserving map
j_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
which is equivariant for the actions
\varphi
and
J([\leq_{\varphi}])
. Since both actions are canonical models, it follows from Lemma
1.8
and Corollary
1.15
that
j_{0}
can be extended to a positive conjugacy. Moreover, since
j_{0}(0)=0
, this conjugacy fixes
0
and therefore, by
(i)
in Corollary
1.7
, we conclude that
\varphi=J([\leq_{\varphi}])
, as desired.

The continuity of
I
follows directly from Lemma
3.15
. Finally, since
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
is compact (Theorem
1.6
) and
[\LPO](G)
is a Hausdorff topological space (Lemma
3.14
), we conclude that
I
is a homeomorphism.
∎

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

We consider the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}r\colon&\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))&\to&\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})\\
&\varphi&\mapsto&J([\leq_{\varphi}]),\end{array}

where
J
is the map introduced in (
1
) and
\leq_{\varphi}
is the preorder induced by
0
. It follows directly from the definitions that
r
is the identity in restriction to
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. Also, by Lemma
3.15
and Theorem
3.16
we conclude that
r
is continuous. Finally, to check that
r
preserves positive semi-conjugacy classes note that
\varphi
is positively semi-conjugate to the dynamical realization of
\leq_{\varphi}
and that, by Corollary
3.13
, the dynamical realizations of
\leq_{\varphi}
and
\leq_{\varphi}^{\ast}
are also positively semi-conjugate.
∎

4.
Application to the Borel complexity of the semi-conjugacy relation

For a given group
G
it is natural to try to determine how difficult is to distinguish actions in
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
up to semi-conjugacy, and whether it is possible to classify them.
To conclude this chapter, we highlight that Theorem
2.1
sheds light on this question, and suggests a line of research for group actions on the line.
An appropriate framework to formalize the question is the theory of
Borel reducibility
of equivalence relations. Most notions of isomorphisms can be naturally interpreted as equivalence relations on some standard Borel space, so that this theory offers tools to study various classification problems and compare the difficulty of one with respect to another.
Let us recall some basic notions from this setting following the exposition of Kechris
[
65
]
(to which we refer for more details). Recall that a standard Borel space
\mathcal{Z}
is a measurable space isomorphic to a complete separable metric space endowed with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. A subset of such a space is
analytic
if it is the image of a Borel set under a Borel map from a standard Borel space. An analytic equivalence relation on
\mathcal{Z}
(henceforth just
equivalence relation
) is an equivalence relation
\mathcal{R}
which is an analytic subset
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}
. This standing assumption is convenient to develop the theory, and general enough to model most natural isomorphism problems. It includes in particular the class of Borel equivalence relations (those which are Borel subsets of
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}
), which is much better behaved, but too restricted for some purposes (see below). We write
x\mathcal{R}y
when
(x,y)\in\mathcal{R}

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mathcal{R}_{i}
be an equivalence relation on a standard Borel space
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. We say that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is
reducible
to
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, and write
\mathcal{R}_{1}\leq_{B}\mathcal{R}_{2}
, if there exists a Borel map
q\colon\mathcal{Z}_{1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2}
such that
x\mathcal{R}_{1}y
occurs if and only if
q(x)\mathcal{R}_{2}q(y)
. This means that distinguishing classes of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is “easier” than for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
. If
\mathcal{R}_{1}\leq_{B}\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}\leq_{B}\mathcal{R}_{1}
, we say that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are
bireducible
.

The simplest type of equivalence relations from the perspective of reducibility are the
smooth
ones. An equivalence relation
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{Z}
is said to be smooth if there exist a standard Borel space
\mathcal{W}
and a Borel map
q\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{W}
which is a complete invariant for the equivalence classes of
\mathcal{R}
, in the sense that
x\mathcal{R}y
if and only if
q(x)=q(y)
.

The next class of equivalence relations are the
hyperfinite
ones. An equivalence relation
\mathcal{R}
is hyperfinite if it is a countable union
\mathcal{R}=\bigcup\mathcal{R}_{n}
of equivalence relations whose classes are finite, and
essentially hyperfinite
if it is bireducible to a hyperfinite one. An example of a hyperfinite equivalence relation which is not smooth, denoted as
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, is the equivalence relation on the set of one-sided binary sequence
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, where
(x_{n})
and
(y_{n})
are equivalent if
x_{n}=y_{n}
for large enough
n
. In fact (after Harrington, Kechris, and Louveau
[
56
]
), up to bireducibility, the relation
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the unique essentially hyperfinite equivalent relation which is not smooth, and moreover every Borel equivalence relation
\mathcal{R}
is either smooth, or satisfies
\mathcal{E}_{0}\leq_{B}\mathcal{R}
, so that
\mathcal{E}_{0}
can be thought of as the simplest non-smooth Borel equivalence relation.
The essentially hyperfinite equivalence relations form a strict subset of the essentially countable ones (those that are bireducible to a relation whose classes are countable). These are precisely those induced by orbits of actions of countable groups, and the poset of bireducibility types of such relations is quite complicated (see Adams ans Kechris
[
2
]
). Essentially countable relations are themselves a strict subset of Borel equivalence relations, after which we find general (analytic) equivalence relations.

The bireducibility type of the conjugacy relation of various classes of dynamical systems and group actions has been extensively studied. For many classes of topological or measurable dynamical systems, the conjugacy relations is known or conjectured to be quite complicated from the perspective of bireducibility; see for instance the works of Foreman, Rudolph, and Weiss
[
46
]
, Gao, Jackson, and Seward
[
47
]
, Sabok and Tsankov
[
114
]
, and Le Roux
[
73
]
. As an example, the conjugacy relation of elements of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is bireducible to the isomorphism relation of all countable (but not necessarily locally finite) graphs, which is analytic but not Borel (see Hjorth
[
58
, Theorem 4.9]
), and the conjugacy relation of homeomorphisms of the plane is strictly more complicated by a result of Hjorth
[
58
, Theorem 4.17]
. As a consequence, one may expect that the semi-conjugacy relation on the space of irreducible action of a given group
G
should also be complicated and might not even be Borel. In contrast, the existence of the space of normalized harmonic actions and Theorem
2.1
imply the following.

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated group. Then the semi-conjugacy relation on the space
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is essentially hyperfinite (in particular, it is Borel).

Proof.

Theorem
2.1
shows that this relation is bireducible to the orbit equivalence relation on the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
induced by the translation flow
\Phi
.
On the other hand, the orbit equivalence relation of any Borel flow on a standard Borel space is essentially hyperfinite (see Kechris
[
65
, Theorem 8.33]
). ∎

After Corollary
4.1
and the previous discussion, we may further distinguish two cases: either the semi-conjugacy relation on
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is smooth, or it is not, in which case it is bireducible to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. As mentioned above, the first case corresponds to groups for which actions up to semi-conjugacy can be completely classified by a Borel invariant, so that it is natural to ask which groups have this property. This can be characterized in terms of a very restricted behavior for the dynamics of the flow
\Phi
on
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. Recall that given a flow
(\Psi,Y)
on a topological space, a point
y\in Y
is said to be
recurrent
if for every neighborhood
U
of
y
, the set of times
\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\Psi^{t}(y)\in U\}
is unbounded (above or below), and
periodic
if
\Psi^{t}(y)=y
for some
t\neq 0
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Under Assumption
1.1
, the semi-conjugacy relation on the space of irreducible actions
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
of a finitely generated group
G
is smooth if, and only if, every recurrent point of the flow
(\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\Phi)
is periodic.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.1
, the semi-conjugacy relation on
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is smooth if and only if so is the orbit equivalence relation of the flow
(\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\Phi)
. A result of Effros implies that the orbit equivalence relation induced by a flow
\Phi
on a Polish space is smooth if and only if every recurrent point of
\Phi
is periodic, see the book of Becker and Kechris
[
8
, Theorem 3.4.2]
.
∎

Corollary
4.2
suggests the following general question on groups acting on the line.

Question 4.3
.

Which finitely generated groups satisfy the properties in Corollary
4.2
?

We will present some results in this direction in §
1
.

Remark 4.4
.

There do exist finitely generated groups
G
for which the semi-conjugacy relation on
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is not smooth (and in particular, the space of semi-conjugacy classes is not a standard measurable space). For example, it is not difficult to show this for the free group
\mathbb{F}_{2}
as follows. Fix a ping-pong pair of homeomorphisms
g,h
of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, with
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(g)=\{0,1/2\}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(h)=\{1/4,3/4\}
, where both
g,h
have one attracting and one repelling fixed point, and such that
\langle g,h\rangle
acts minimally on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\tilde{g},\tilde{h}\in\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be two lifts, with
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(\tilde{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}(\tilde{h})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}
. Given a sequence
\omega=(\omega_{n})\in\{+1,-1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, define an element
\tilde{g}_{\omega}\in\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
by
\tilde{g}_{\omega}(x)=\tilde{g}^{\omega_{n}}(x)
if
x\in[\frac{1}{2}n,\frac{1}{2}n+1]
, and if
\mathbb{F}_{2}
is the free group with free generators
a,b
, define a representation
\varphi_{\omega}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
by
\varphi_{\omega}(a)=\tilde{g}_{\omega}
and
\varphi_{\omega}(b)=\tilde{h}
. It is not difficult to check that the map
\omega\mapsto\varphi_{\omega}
is Borel (actually continuous).
Since
\varphi_{\omega}(\mathbb{F}_{2})=\langle\tilde{g}_{\omega},\tilde{h}\rangle
acts on
\mathbb{R}
with the same orbits as
\langle\tilde{g},\tilde{h}\rangle
, every action
\varphi_{\omega}
is minimal, and this implies that for
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, the actions
\varphi_{\omega}
and
\varphi_{\omega^{\prime}}
are positively semi-conjugate if and only if they are positively conjugate, and this happens if and only if
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
belong to the same orbit of the bilateral shift. Since the orbit equivalence relation of the shift is not smooth, the conclusion follows.

Remark 4.5
.

In contrast, for every countable group
G
the semi-conjugacy relation on the space
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
of irreducible actions on the circle is always smooth. One way to prove this is to repeat the above argument by fixing a generating probability measure
\mu
on
G
, and note that every action is semi-conjugate to an action for which the Lebesgue measure is stationary (unlike for the case of the real line, this is a straightforward consequence of compactness). This can be used to construct an analogue of the space of normalized harmonic actions, where the translation flow should be replaced by an action of the group
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and since this is a compact group, the action must always admit a Borel cross section. Another route would be to prove that the bounded Euler class is a Borel complete invariant under semi-conjugacy.
This difference can be seen as a formalization of the observation, mentioned in the introduction, that studying actions on the circle up to semi-conjugacy is easier than for the real line thanks to compactness.

Chapter 14
Spaces of harmonic actions and laminar actions

In this chapter, we develop some technical tools that relate the framework of laminar actions and horograding, developed in Part
2
, with the space of normalized harmonic actions

1.
Horogradings as partial semi-conjugacies

We have already seen, in Chapter
7
, that if a laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
admits a horograding by another action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{+}(\mathbb{R})
, then various properties of
\varphi
are governed by
\rho
, especially as far as the behavior near
\infty
is concerned (see for instance Proposition
2.10
, or Lemma
2.12
). Here we explain that when
G
is finitely generated, a horograding can be thought of as a partially-defined semi-conjugacy between
\varphi
and
\rho
. Namely, the choice of a finite symmetric generating set
S
determines canonically a decomposition of the line into a bounded interval
I_{S}
(the
central leaf
), and two half-lines
J_{S}^{\pm}
(the
outer rays
) on which the horograding defines a semi-conjugacy with the horograding action
\rho
, except that the equivariance is lost when orbits visit
I_{S}
. We first explain how to canonically select such a leaf
I_{S}
.

Lemma 1.1

(Central leaf)
.

Let
G
be a group generated by a finite symmetric set
S
, and consider a focal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
with invariant lamination
\mathcal{L}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{S}
be the set of leaves
I\in\mathcal{L}
such that:

(CL1)

the
\varphi
-orbit of
I
is cofinal in
\mathcal{L}
;

(CL2)

we have
s.I\cap I\neq\varnothing
for every
s\in S
.

Then, there exists a leaf
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{S}=\{I\in\mathcal{L}:I_{S}\subseteq I\}
.

Definition 1.2
.

With notation and assumptions as in Lemma
1.1
, the leaf
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}
will be called the
central leaf
associated with the generating set
S
.

Proof of Lemma
1.1
.

As
\varphi
is focal, we can find a cofinal orbit in
\mathcal{L}
, thus the collection of leaves satisfying
(CL1)
is non-empty.
Now, both conditions
(CL1)
and
(CL2)
are stable under passing to a larger leaf, and clearly any sufficiently large element of
(\mathcal{L},\subseteq)
satisfies
(CL2)
(it is enough to choose an element which contains a given point
\xi
and its
\varphi
-images under elements of
S
). We conclude that
\mathcal{L}_{S}
is non-empty.

Observe next that for every
I\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, there must exists
s\in S
such that
s.I\neq I
, since the action is irreducible. Upon replacing
s
by
s^{-1}
, there exist
s\in S
with
s.I\supsetneq I
. It follows that we can find a sequence
(s_{n})
of elements of
S
such that
I_{n}:=s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.I
is an increasing exhaustion of
\mathbb{R}
. Such a sequence can be constructed by induction, by choosing at each step
s_{n}\in S
such that
s_{n}.I_{n-1}
is maximal. If
(I_{n})
does not exhaust
\mathbb{R}
, then it must converge to some
J\in\mathcal{L}
such that there exists no
s\in S
with
s.J\supsetneq J
, contradicting what was argued before.

We now show that
\mathcal{L}_{S}
is a totally ordered subset of
\mathcal{L}
. Suppose by contradiction that
I_{0},J\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
are disjoint, and let
I_{n}:=s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.I_{0}
be an increasing exhaustion as above. There is a smallest
m\geq 1
such that
I_{m}\supseteq J
. Then
I_{m-1}\cap J=\varnothing
, and it follows that
s_{m}.J\cap J=\varnothing
, contradicting that
J\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
. Thus
\mathcal{L}_{S}
is totally ordered.

It follows that
K=\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}{\overline{I}}
is non-empty. Note that
K
cannot be reduced to a point
\{\xi\}
, for if this was the case, by irreducibility of
\varphi
, there would exist
s\in S
such that
s.\xi\neq\xi
, and thus
s.I\cap I=\varnothing
for any sufficiently small
I\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, violating
(CL2)
. Hence
K=\overline{I}_{S}
for some
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}
. To conclude, we need to check that
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, i.e. that it satisfies
(CL1)
and
(CL2)
. It is easy to see that
(CL2)
is a closed condition (or more precisely that its negation, namely that
s.I\cap I=\varnothing
for some
s\in S
, is an open condition), and thus is satisfied by
I_{S}
. As for
(CL2)
, arguing as above we can choose
s\in S
such that
s.I_{S}\supsetneq I_{S}
, and thus
s.I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
. In particular
s.I_{S}
has a cofinal orbit (condition
(CL2)
), and thus so does
I_{S}
.
∎

We now explain how the additional data of a horograding determines a partially defined semi-conjugacy on each connected component of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus I_{S}
.

Proposition 1.3

(Partial semi-conjugacies)
.

Let
G
be a group generated by a finite symmetric set
S
, and consider a focal laminar action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
with a positive horograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
by an irreducible action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}
be the central leaf associated with
S
, and denote by
J_{S}^{-}
and
J_{S}^{+}
the connected components of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus I_{S}
adjacent, respectively, to
-\infty
and
+\infty
. Then there exist maps

\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}\colon J^{\pm}_{S}\to[\mathfrak{h}(I_{S}),+\infty),

satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}
is non-decreasing,
\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}
is non-increasing, and

\lim_{\xi\to+\infty}\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)=\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}(\xi)=\infty.

(ii)

For any
m\geq 1
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}\in S
, if
\xi\in J_{S}^{+}
is such that

s_{j}\cdots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)>\mathfrak{h}(I_{S})\quad\text{for every }j\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

then
s_{m}\cdots s_{1}.\xi\in J_{S}^{+}
and

\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(s_{m}\cdots s_{1}.\xi)=s_{m}\cdots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi).

The analogous statement holds for
\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}
.

Remark 1.4
.

The analogous statement for negative horogradings also holds. In this case, the map
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}
is non-increasing while the map
\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}
is non-decreasing.

Proposition
1.3
is a useful technical tool to work with horograded actions, so that it is again worth to give a specific name to the objects appearing in the statement.

Definition 1.5
.

With notation and assumptions as in Proposition
1.3
, the two connected components
J^{-}_{S}
and
J^{+}_{S}
of
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus{I}_{S}
will be called the
outer rays
associated with
S
.
The maps
\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}
are called the
partial semi-conjugacies
associated with
S
. The point
c_{S}=\mathfrak{h}(I_{S})
will be called the
central value
of the horograding
\mathfrak{h}
(associated with
S
).

Roughly speaking, Proposition
1.3
states that, as a point
\xi
gets further away from the central leaf, the action
\varphi
around
\xi
resembles more and more to an action semi-conjugate to the action
\rho
, in the sense that the difference is undetectable on longer and longer elements of
G
(in the word metric determined by
S
). Moreover one has a control on the “good” group elements in terms of the action
\rho
only (and the point
c_{S}
).

Proof of Proposition
1.3
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a positive horograding of
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
by an irreducible action
\rho\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
J^{-}_{S}
and
J^{+}_{S}
be the outer rays. Similarly to what we have done in the proof of Lemma
2.12
, for each
\xi\in J^{\pm}_{S}
, let
I_{\xi}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
be the smallest leaf such that
\xi\in\overline{I}_{\xi}
.
Then we consider the maps

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}\colon&J^{\pm}_{S}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
&\xi&\mapsto&\mathfrak{h}(I_{\xi}).\end{array}

By construction, both maps take values in the half-line
[\mathfrak{h}(I_{S}),+\infty)
. The map
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}
is non-decreasing,
\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}
is non-increasing, and by Lemma
2.7
, we have

\lim_{\xi\to+\infty}\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)=\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}(\xi)=+\infty,

so that
(i)
is verified. We next discuss
(ii)
.
By induction, it is enough to establish the property for
m=1
. Fix
\xi\in J^{+}_{S}
and
s\in S
such that
{c}_{S}<s.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)=\mathfrak{h}\left(s.I_{\xi}\right)
. We need to justify that this implies that
s.\xi\in J^{+}_{S}
.
It cannot be the case that
s.\xi\in J^{-}_{S}
, as this would imply that
s.I_{S}\cap I_{S}=\varnothing
, contradicting
(CL2)
. Thus the only possibility is that
s.\xi\in\overline{I}_{S}
, i.e.
\xi\in s^{-1}.\overline{I}_{S}
. Note that
J:=s^{-1}.I_{S}
and
I_{S}
must be related by inclusion, by
(CL2)
, and since
\overline{J}
contains
\xi\notin\overline{I}_{S}
, we deduce that
I_{S}\subset J\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
. This implies that
I_{\xi}\subseteq J
, by definition of
I_{\xi}
, and
s.I_{\xi}\subset I_{S}
. We deduce that
c_{s}<\mathfrak{h}(s.I_{\xi})\leq\mathfrak{h}(I_{S})=c_{S}
, a contradiction; hence
\xi\in J^{+}_{S}
. It follows immediately that
I_{s.\xi}=s.I_{\xi}
, and thus
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(s.\xi)=s.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)
. ∎

2.
Laminar actions in harmonic coordinates

Starting from now, we discuss properties of central leaves and partial semi-conjugacies when working with normalized harmonic actions.

Assumption 2.1
.

Throughout the section, we fix a finitely generated group
G
together with a finite symmetric generating set
S
, and a symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
G
whose support is finite and generates
G
.

1.
Uniformity of central leaves

The following lemma says that central leaves of laminar actions in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
have uniformly controlled diameter. Given an interval
J=(a,b)
, we denote by
|J|=b-a
its length, as customary.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, there exist constants
0<C_{1}<C_{2}
such that for every laminar action
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi
-invariant lamination
\mathcal{L}
, the central leaf
I_{S}\in\mathcal{L}
satisfies
C_{1}<|I_{S}|<C_{2}
.

Proof.

For any irreducible action
\varphi:G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, define

\delta(\varphi)=\max\{\varphi(s)(0):s\in S\},

which is non-zero (by irreducibility of
\varphi
), and actually positive (since
S
is symmetric). On the one hand,

\delta
defines a continuous function on
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
, so that by compactness of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, we can
set

\delta_{+}=\min_{\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})}\delta(\varphi),

(1)

which is therefore positive. On the other hand,
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
, the relation
\Phi^{\xi}(\varphi)(g)(0)=\varphi(g)(\xi)-\xi
,
gives

\delta(\Phi^{\xi}(\varphi))=\max\{\varphi(s)(\xi)-\xi:s\in S\}.

By
\Phi
-invariance of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, we deduce that

\min_{\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\max\{\varphi(s)(\xi)-\xi:s\in S\}=\min_{\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})}\delta(\varphi)=\delta_{+}.

Similarly, we can introduce the well-defined positive constant

\displaystyle\Delta=

\displaystyle\max\{|\varphi(s)(\xi)-\xi|:\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G),s\in S,\xi\in\mathbb{R}\}

(2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\{|\varphi(s)(0)|:\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G),s\in S\}>0.

Fix now a laminar action
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
together with a
\varphi
-invariant lamination
\mathcal{L}
, and let
I_{S}=(I_{S}^{-},I_{S}^{+})
be its associated central leaf. After the previous discussion, we can take
s\in S
with
s.I_{S}^{-}-I_{S}^{-}\geq\delta_{+}
. By definition of
I_{S}
, we get
s.I_{S}^{+}\subset I_{S}^{+}
, and we conclude that
|I_{S}|\geq\delta_{+}
.
For the other inequality, we claim that
|I_{S}|\leq 3\Delta
. Indeed, since
S
is symmetric, we can take
s\in S
so that
s.I_{S}^{+}<I_{S}^{+}
. In this case, by definition of
I_{S}
, we have that
s.I_{S}\subset I_{S}
. On the one hand, this gives
|s.I_{S}|\geq|I_{S}|-2\Delta
. On the other hand, recalling the properties of
I_{S}
(see Lemma
1.1
), this also gives that for some
s^{\prime}\in S
we must have
s^{\prime}s.I_{S}\cap s.I_{S}=\varnothing
, and consequently
|s.I_{S}|\leq\Delta
. Putting both inequalities together, we get that
|I_{S}|\leq 3\Delta
.
∎

2.
Limits of centered laminar actions

In general, a limit point of laminar actions in the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
need not be laminar. The next result provides a sufficient condition under which this is the case.

Lemma 2.3
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, for any
K>0
, the subset of focal laminar actions in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
admitting an invariant lamination whose central leaf is contained in the interval
(-K,K)
, is closed.

Proof.

Given an action
\varphi\colon G\to\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
and a
\varphi
-invariant lamination
\mathcal{L}
, we will write
I_{S,\varphi}
for the central leaf.
Consider a sequence of laminar actions
\varphi_{n}\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, with
\varphi_{n}
-invariant laminations
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, such that
I_{S,\varphi_{n}}\subset(-K,K)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and assume that it converges, as
n\to\infty
, to an action
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. By Lemma
2.2
, we have that there exists a positive constant
C_{1}>0
so that
|I_{S,\varphi_{n}}|>C_{1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
I_{S,\varphi_{n}}\subset(-K,K)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, upon passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
I_{S,\varphi_{n}}
converges to an interval
J\subset[-K,K]
with non-empty interior. Moreover, since crossing is an open condition, we have that the
\varphi
-orbit of the interior of
J
is cross free and therefore gives a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination. Furthermore, since
I_{S,\varphi_{n}}
is related by inclusion with
\varphi_{n}(s)(I_{S,\varphi_{n}})
for every
s\in S
, the same happens with
J
and
\varphi(s)(J)
. On the one hand, this implies that
\varphi
is not a cyclic action, and since it belongs to
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, it is minimal, so that it is a focal laminar action (Proposition
1.15
). On the other hand, it gives that
I_{S,\varphi}\subset J\subset[-K,K]
, concluding the proof.
∎

3.
Horogradings in harmonic coordinates

Lemma 2.4
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, there exists
C>0
such that if
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
is laminar, and
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
a horograding of
\varphi
by an action
\rho\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
, then the associated partial semi-conjugacies
\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}\colon J^{\pm}_{S}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\frac{1}{C}|\xi-\eta|-C\leq|\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}(\xi)-\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S}(\eta)|\leq C|\xi-\eta|+C,

for every
\xi,\eta\in J^{\pm}_{S}
.

Proof.

Let us prove the statement for
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}
. Let
\delta_{+}
and
\Delta
be the constants defined in the proof of Lemma
2.2
, and assume that
\xi,\eta\in J_{S}^{+}
, with
\xi<\eta
. Choose inductively a sequence of generators
(s_{i})
such that
s_{i+1}\cdots s_{1}.\xi-s_{i}\cdots s_{1}.\xi
is maximal for every
i
(and hence bounded from below by
\delta_{+}
, but still bounded from above by
\Delta
). Then Proposition
1.3
implies that
s_{i+1}\ldots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)-s_{i}\ldots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)
is also maximal (and hence also bounded from below by
\delta_{+}
, and from above by
\Delta
). Let
n
be such that
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.\xi<\eta\leq s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}.\xi
. Then

s_{n}\cdots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)\leq\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\eta)\leq s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}.\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi),

so that
\eta-\xi
and
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\eta)-\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S}(\xi)
are both bounded from below by
\delta_{+}n
and from above by
\Delta n+\Delta
. The conclusion follows easily. ∎

We conclude this chapter with an important technical statement which relates horograded actions and their horograding actions in the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. By Proposition
1.3
, if a laminar action
\varphi
has a horograding by
\rho
, then
\varphi
and
\rho
look semi-conjugate far away from the central leaf (up to the orientation). In the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, this implies that the
\Phi
-orbits of
\varphi
and
\rho
are eventually close (with a uniform control on constants).

Lemma 2.5
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, let
\mathsf{dist}
be any distance inducing the topology of
\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
T>0
such that the following hold. Suppose that
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
is laminar, and that
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
is a positive horograding of
\varphi
by an action
\rho\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
, with central leaf
I_{S}
and partial semi-conjugacies
\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}\colon J_{S}^{\pm}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, the following hold.

(i)

If
t\in J^{+}_{S}
is such that
t-\sup I_{S}>T
, then
\mathsf{dist}(\Phi^{t}(\varphi),\Phi^{\mathfrak{h}^{+}(t)}(\rho))<\varepsilon
.

(ii)

If
t\in J^{-}_{S}
is such that
t-\inf I_{S}<-T
, then
\mathsf{dist}(\Phi^{t}(\varphi),\Phi^{\mathfrak{h}^{-}_{S}(t)}(\widehat{\rho}))<\varepsilon
, where
\widehat{\rho}
is the conjugate of
\rho
by the map
x\mapsto-x
.

Remark 2.6
.

Note that any negative horograding
(\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{h})
of
\varphi
by
\rho
is a positive horograding of
\varphi
by
\widehat{\rho}
, so the same result holds for negative horogradings, upon exchanging the roles of
\rho
and
\hat{\rho}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

We will prove
(i)
, the proof of
(ii)
being analogous. Suppose by contradiction that the conclusion does not hold. Then we can find sequences of laminar actions
(\varphi_{n})
in
\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
, with horograding
(\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathfrak{h}_{n})
of
\varphi_{n}
by an action
\rho_{n}\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
, with central leaves
I_{n}
and partial semi-conjugacies
\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{\pm}\colon J_{n}^{\pm}\to\mathbb{R}
, and a sequence of real numbers
t_{n}\in J_{n}^{+}
, such that
t_{n}-\sup I_{n}\to\infty
, but
\mathsf{dist}(\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n}),\Phi^{u_{n}}(\rho_{n}))
is bounded away from 0, where
u_{n}:=\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{+}(t_{n})
. After extracting a subsequence, we can suppose that
\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
and
\Phi^{u_{n}}(\rho_{n})
converge to different limits in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. We will work with the homeomorphism

\begin{array}[]{cccc}I\colon&\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})&\to&[\LPO](G)\\
&\varphi&\mapsto&[\leq_{\varphi}],\end{array}

defined in the proof of Theorem
3.16
(recall that
\leq_{\varphi}
is the preorder induced by the
\varphi
-orbit of
0
). Denote by
\leq_{n}
the preorder induced by the
\varphi_{n}
-orbit of
t_{n}
, which equals the preorder induced by the
\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
-orbit of
0
, and let
\preceq_{n}
be the preorder induced by the
\rho_{n}
-orbit of
u_{n}
, which equals the preorder induced by the
\Phi^{u_{n}}(\rho_{n})
-orbit of
0
.

Assume now that
\Phi^{u_{n}}(\rho_{n})
converges to an action
\rho_{\infty}
; then, this is equivalent to saying that
[\preceq_{n}]\to[\preceq_{\infty}]
, where
\preceq_{\infty}
is the preorder induced by the
\rho_{\infty}
-orbit of
0
. To obtain a contradiction, we will prove that in this case
\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
also converges to
\rho_{\infty}
, by proving that
[\leq_{n}]\to[\preceq_{\infty}]
. Note that by Lemma
3.15
, in order to prove that
[\leq_{n}]\to[\preceq_{\infty}]
, it is enough to show that for every
g\in P_{\preceq_{\infty}}
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
g\in P_{\leq_{n}}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
. So, let us fix an element
g\in P_{\preceq_{\infty}}
, and let us first show that
g\in P_{\preceq_{n}}
for any
n
big enough. If it were not the case, by compactness of
\mathsf{LPO}(G)
we could find a convergent subsequence
\preceq_{n_{k}}\to\preceq_{\ast}
with
g\notin P_{\preceq_{*}}
.
Then, on the one hand, since
[\preceq_{n}]\to[\preceq_{\infty}]
, we must have that
\preceq_{\ast}\in[\preceq_{\infty}]
. On the other hand, since
\rho_{\infty}\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
is a canonical model, Proposition
3.10
implies that
\preceq_{\infty}
is a minimal model of
[\preceq_{\infty}]
. These two facts together give that
P_{\preceq_{\infty}}\subset P_{\preceq_{\ast}}
, contradicting that
g\notin P_{\preceq_{\ast}}
. Conversely, we show the following.

Claim
.

There exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
n\geq n_{0}
and
g\in P_{\preceq_{n}}
, then
g\in P_{\leq_{n}}
.

Proof of claim.

We want to prove that for any sufficiently large
n
,
if
\rho(g)(u_{n})>u_{n}
, then
\varphi_{n}(g)(t_{n})>t_{n}
. Assume that
g
satisfies so, and let
c_{n}:=\mathfrak{h}_{n}(I_{n})
be the central value of the horograding. Since
t_{n}-\sup I_{n}
tends to
+\infty
, Lemma
2.4
implies that
u_{n}-c_{n}
tends to
+\infty
. Consider the constant
\Delta
defined as in (
2
), and write
g=s_{k}\cdots s_{1}
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S
. Then for
n
large enough, we have
u_{n}-c_{n}>k\Delta
, and hence
\rho_{n}(s_{i}\cdots s_{1})(u_{n})\geq u_{n}-i\Delta>c_{n}
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. For such an
n
, Proposition
1.3
implies that
\varphi_{n}(g)(t_{n})\in J_{n}^{+}
and

\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{+}(\varphi_{n}(g)(t_{n}))=\rho_{n}(g)(\mathfrak{h}^{+}(t_{n}))=\rho_{n}(g)(u_{n})>u_{n}.

Since
\mathfrak{h}_{n}^{+}
is non-decreasing, this gives
\varphi_{n}(g)(t_{n})\geq t_{n}
,
as desired.
∎

Thus, since
g\in P_{\preceq_{n}}
for any
n
large enough, the claim gives that
g\in P_{\leq_{n}}
for any
n
large enough. This finishes the proof that
\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
converges to
\rho_{\infty}
, and thus the proof of the lemma.
∎

Chapter 15
Spaces of harmonic actions for locally moving groups

In this chapter we prove our main results in the setting of locally moving groups. In particular, we prove a criterion for the local rigidity of the standard action.

1.
Preliminary considerations

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open interval, and suppose that
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
is a finitely generated micro-supported subgroup acting minimally on
X
. Fix a symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
G
with finite generating support, and consider the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
of normalized
\mu
-harmonic actions. The latter admits a decomposition

\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{Q}\sqcup\mathcal{I}\sqcup\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\sqcup\mathcal{E},

(1)

where the subsets in this partition are defined by what follows:

—

we let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
be the subset consisting of actions
\varphi
that are not faithful, or equivalently factor through
G/[G_{c},G_{c}]
(by Proposition
2.1
);

—

we fix a harmonic representative
\iota\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
of the standard action on
X
, and let
\mathcal{I}=\{\Phi^{t}(\iota):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be its
\Phi
-orbit, and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
the
\Phi
-orbit of
\widehat{\iota}
, the conjugate of
\iota
under the reflection
x\mapsto-x
;

—

we let
\mathcal{E}
be the subset of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
of exotic actions, that is, all actions that are faithful, minimal, and not conjugate to the standard action. Recall that these are all laminar by Theorem
1.1
.

The notation introduced above will be used throughout the chapter.
The following proposition states some elementary properties of the decomposition in (
1
) (which do not rely on any of our main results). Recall that for a micro-supported subgroup
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
acting on
X=(a,b)
, we denote by
G_{-}
(respectively,
G_{+}
) the subgroups of elements with trivial germ at
a
(respectively,
b
).

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
, be a finitely generated micro-supported subgroup acting minimally on
X
, and fix a symmetric probability measure with finite generating support
\mu
on
G
. Consider the decomposition (
1
) of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
. Then all sets in the decompositions are invariant under the translation flow
\Phi
, and the following hold:

(i)

The set
\mathcal{Q}
is closed.

(ii)

All accumulation points of the
\Phi
-orbits
\mathcal{I},\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
are contained in
\mathcal{Q}
. More precisely:

—

if
\psi
belongs to the
\omega
-limit set of
\mathcal{I}
, or to the
\alpha
-limit set of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\psi\supset G_{+}
;

—

if
\psi
belongs to the
\alpha
-limit set of
\mathcal{I}
, or to the
\omega
-limit set of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\psi\supset G_{-}
.

(iii)

The set
\mathcal{E}
is open.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.1
, an action
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})
belongs to
\mathcal{Q}
if and only if
\varphi([G_{c},G_{c}])=\{\mathsf{id}\}
; the latter is a closed condition, showing
(i)
.
To show
(ii)
, consider, for instance, the case where
\psi
is an accumulation point of
\Phi^{t}(\iota)
, for
t\to+\infty
(the other cases being analogous). For simplicity, we assume that
\iota
is the defining action of
G
on
X=\mathbb{R}
. For every finite subset
\Sigma\subset G_{+}
, we can find
x\in X
such that
\Sigma\subset G_{(-\infty,x)}
. Since
\Phi^{t}(\iota)
is the conjugate of
\iota
by the translation
x\mapsto x-t
, it follows that for any arbitrary compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}
and
t>x-\min K
, the image
\Phi^{t}(\iota)(\Sigma)
acts trivially on
K
, so that
\psi(\Sigma)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}\}
. Since
\Sigma
is an arbitrary finite subset of
G_{+}
, we deduce that
G_{+}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{ker}}\psi
(in particular
\psi
is not faithful, i.e. it belongs to
\mathcal{Q}
).
Finally,
(i)
and
(ii)
together imply that
\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{I}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
is closed, i.e. that
\mathcal{E}
is open, showing
(iii)
.
∎

2.
Main result

1.
Statement and main corollaries

The main result of Part
3
is Theorem
2.3
below. It shows that for a class of locally moving subgroups
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
, the dynamics of the flow
(\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\Phi)
satisfies some strong restrictions, leading in particular to the local rigidity of the standard action. This result relies on a technical but important assumption on
G
, related to the generating sets of the subgroups
G_{I}
for
I\subsetneq X
. We will use the following terminology (in the lack of a better one).

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X=(a,b)
. We say that a subgroup
G\subseteq\homeo_{0}(X)
satisfies condition
(

F

)
if

(
\mathcal{F}
)

there exists
x,y\in X
such that the subgroup
G_{(a,x)}
is contained in a finitely generated subgroup of
G_{+}
, and
G_{(y,b)}
is contained in a finitely generated subgroup of
G_{-}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Condition
(

F

)
is satisfied provided there exists
x,y
such that
G_{(a,x)},G_{(y,b)}
are themselves finitely generated. For example, this condition is satisfied by Thompson’s group
F
, and many relatives. Various sources of examples of locally moving groups with
(

F

)
will be discussed in §
3
.
In §
4
, we will construct an example showing that the main results in this section become false if
(

F

)
is dropped.

Recall that a flow
\Psi
on a locally compact space
Z
is
proper
if for every compact subset
K\subseteq Z
, the set
\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\Psi^{t}(K)\cap K\neq\varnothing\}
is compact.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a finitely generated locally moving subgroup satisfying
(

F

)
, and fix a symmetric probability measure
\mu
with finite generating support on
G
. Consider the decomposition of the space
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
as in (
1
). Then the following hold:

(i)

The orbits
\mathcal{I}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
are open subsets of
\Der_{\mu}(G)
.

(ii)

The translation flow restricted to the open subset
\Der_{\mu}(G,\mathbb{R})\smallsetminus\mathcal{Q}
of faithful actions is proper.

Theorem
2.3
has the following immediate corollary, which implies Theorem
D
from the introduction.

Corollary 2.4

(Local rigidity of the standard action)
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a finitely generated locally moving subgroup satisfying
(

F

)
. Then, the subset of actions in
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
which are positively semi-conjugate to the standard action of
G
on
X
is open. In particular, the standard action of
G
on
X
is locally rigid.

Proof.

The retraction

r\colon\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))\to\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})

from Theorem
2.1
preserves the positive semi-conjugacy classes; hence, the subset of actions positively semi-conjugate to the standard action coincides with the preimage
r^{-1}(\mathcal{I})
. As by Theorem
2.3
, we have that the subset
\mathcal{I}
is open, the result follows.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Notice that the reversed standard action is also locally rigid. We point out that in the setting of Corollary
2.4
, these actions are not always the unique locally rigid actions of
G
: the analysis of actions of Bieri–Strebel groups in Chapter
9
provides examples of groups satisfying Corollary
2.4
(see §
2
), that admit finitely many conjugacy classes of faithful minimal laminar actions. These actions correspond to finitely many
\Phi
-orbits in
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, and are therefore locally rigid as well.

Another consequence of Theorem
2.3
is that the structure of the space of semi-conjugacy classes in
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is particularly well behaved from a topological and Borel perspective. In particular, faithful minimal actions of
G
, considered up to conjugacy, give rise to a well-behaved topological space. (Recall the discussion in §
4
for the notion of smoothness of an equivalence relation in the Borel setting, and its significance to classification problems).

Corollary 2.6

(Tameness of the space of semi-conjugacy classes)
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a finitely generated locally moving subgroup satisfying
(

F

)
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the subset of
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
consisting of actions that are faithful and minimal, and denote by
\sim
the equivalence relation on
\mathcal{M}
of positive conjugacy. Then the following hold:

(i)

The quotient space
\mathcal{M}/\sim
is a locally compact metrizable space, in which the conjugacy class of the the standard action is an isolated point.

(ii)

The equivalence relation
\sim
on
\mathcal{M}
is smooth.
The semi-conjugacy relation on the space
\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is smooth if and only if the same holds true for the space
\Homirr(G/[G_{c},G_{c}],\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
of irreducible actions of the largest quotient.

Proof.

Let
\sim
be the conjugacy relation on
\mathcal{M}
, and let

\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{M}\cap\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})=\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})\smallsetminus\mathcal{Q}.

The harmonic retraction

r\colon\Homirr(G,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))\to\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})

defined in Theorem
2.1
satisfies that
r(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{U}
, and two actions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
are conjugate if and only if
r(\varphi_{1})
and
r(\varphi_{2})
are in the same
\Phi
-orbit. From this it follows that the quotient space
\mathcal{M}/\sim
is naturally homeomorphic to the quotient space
\mathcal{U}/\Phi
. Note that
\mathcal{U}
is a locally compact metrizable space (as it is an open subset in a compact metrizable space). Theorem
2.3
implies that
\mathcal{I}/\Phi
and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}/\Phi
are isolated points in the quotient, and that the flow
\Phi
on
\mathcal{U}
is proper. This implies that
\mathcal{U}/\Phi
is Hausdorff, locally compact (Abels
[
1
, Corollary 1.15]
), and regular (Palais
[
107
, Proposition 1.2.8]
), hence metrizable by the Urysohn lemma, showing
(i)
. For
(ii)
, first note that
(i)
implies in particular that
\mathcal{M}/\sim
is a standard Borel space, so
\sim
is smooth on
\mathcal{M}
.
For the remaining statement, we apply Corollary
4.2
. Theorem
2.3
, implies
\mathcal{E}
does not contain any recurrent point for the flow
\Phi
, and the same conclusion holds for
\mathcal{I},\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
by Lemma
1.1
. Hence all recurrent points of
(\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\Phi)
are contained in
\mathcal{Q}
. To conclude, note that
\mathcal{Q}
naturally identifies with the space of harmonic actions
\Der_{\overline{\mu}}(G/[G_{c},G_{c}];\mathbb{R})
, where
\overline{\mu}
is the push-forward of
\mu
by the quotient map.
∎

2.
The proof

The main tool for proving Theorem
2.3
is Theorem
2.1
. It is applicable thanks to the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a locally moving subgroup satisfying
(

F

)
. Then the fragmentable subgroup
G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is finitely generated.

Proof.

Since
G
satisfies condition
(

F

)
, there exist
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and finite subsets
S_{1}\subset G_{-}
and
S_{2}\subset G_{+}
, such that
G_{(a,x_{2})}\subset\langle S_{2}\rangle
and
G_{(x_{1},b)}\subset\langle S_{1}\rangle
, and upon conjugating
G_{(a,x_{2})}
by some
g\in G
, we can assume that
x_{1}<x_{2}
. We set
H=\langle S_{1},S_{2}\rangle
, and we want to prove that
H=G_{\mathsf{frag}}
. The inclusion
H\subseteq G_{\mathsf{frag}}
is clear. To show the other inclusion, first notice that since
x_{1}<x_{2}
, the subgroup
\langle G_{(a,x_{2})},G_{(x_{1},b)}\rangle\subseteq H
acts on
X
without fixed points. Thus, the union

\bigcup_{h\in H}hG_{(a,x_{2})}h^{-1}=\bigcup_{h\in H}G_{(a,h(x_{2}))},

contained in
H
, coincides with the subgroup
G_{+}=\bigcup_{x\in X}G_{(a,x)}
. A similar argument shows that
G_{-}
is also contained in
H
. Since
G_{\mathsf{frag}}=G_{-}G_{+}
, we get the desired conclusion.
∎

Through the rest of this subsection, let
G
be as in Theorem
2.3
, and fix a finite symmetric generating subset
S\subset G
. As before, we choose a representative
\iota\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
of the standard action of
G
. For simplicity, we will assume that
X=\mathbb{R}
and identify the standard action with
\iota
(although we keep the notation
X
, to avoid confusion with actions
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
). We consider the decomposition
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{Q}\sqcup\mathcal{I}\sqcup\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\sqcup\mathcal{E}
given by (
1
).
By Theorem
2.1
, every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
is (positively or negatively) horograded by
\iota
. Thus we may further decompose
\mathcal{E}
as
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{-}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{+}
(respectively,
\mathcal{E}_{-}
) is the subset of actions that are positively (respectively, negatively) horograded by
\iota
.

For every action
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, the proof of Theorem
2.1
provides an explicit prehorograding
(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi})
of
\varphi
by
\iota
, described in Remark
2.9
, that we recall now. The subgroups
G_{(a,x)}
are totally bounded for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
(recall that this means that each connected component of
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
is bounded). This allows to define a
\varphi
-invariant prelamination given by
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}=\{\Iphi(x,\xi):x\in X,\xi\in\Xi_{\varphi}\}
where

—

\Xi_{\varphi}=\bigcap_{x\in X}\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
(which is a
G_{\delta}
-dense subset of
\mathbb{R}
), and

—

\Iphi(x,\xi)
is the connected component of
\suppphi(G_{(a,x)})
containing
\xi
.

The prehorograding
\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}
is then given by
\Iphi(x,\xi)\mapsto x
, and it extends to a horograding defined on the closure
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}
. These objects can be introduced in an analogous way when
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}
is negatively horograded by the standard action, by replacing the instances of
G_{(a,x)}
by
G_{(x,b)}
. We will discuss the case of positive horograding below.

As we have fixed the generating subset
S
, we can consider the associated central leaf
I_{S,\varphi}\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}
, the outer rays
J_{S,\varphi}^{\pm}
, and the partial semi-conjugacies
\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}_{S,\varphi}\colon J^{\pm}_{S,\varphi}\to[c_{S,\varphi},+\infty)
, where
c_{S,\varphi}=\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}(I_{S,\varphi})
is the central value (see Definition
1.5
).

With this notation at disposal, the main idea in the proof of Theorem
2.3
is to control the accumulation points of
\mathcal{E}
by discussing separately the case where the central leaf is at bounded distance from the origin, that can be settled using Lemma
2.3
, and the case where the central leaf is far from the origin, which can be studied using Lemma
2.5
. For this, the crucial missing argument is contained in the following lemma. Its proof uses condition
(

F

)
in an essential way.

Lemma 2.8

(Key lemma)
.

Retain the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
, and the notation above. Then, there exist
c_{+},c_{-}\in X
such that
c_{S,\varphi}\geq c_{+}
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, and
c_{S,\varphi}\leq c_{-}
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}

Proof.

We first see that
\mu
-harmonicity of actions provides a uniform control on the size of the intervals
\Iphi(x,\xi)
.

Claim
.

For every
x\in X
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|\Iphi(x,\xi)|<C
for every laminar action
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
and
\xi\in\Xi_{\varphi}
.

Proof of claim.

We only detail the case where
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
is positively horograded by the standard action (working with
\mathcal{E}_{-}
is totally analogous). Fix
x\in X
. Since
G
satisfies condition
(

F

)
, we can find a finitely generated subgroup
H=\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\rangle\subset G
and a point
y\in[x,b)
such that
G_{(y,b)}\subseteq H\subseteq G_{(x,b)}
. We will show that the constant

C=\max\left\{|\varphi(h_{i})(\xi)-\xi|:\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R}),\xi\in\mathbb{R},\text{ and }i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\right\},

which is well defined and positive (by compactness and
\Phi
-invariance of
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
, see the argument in the proof of Lemma
2.2
),
gives the desired uniform control, and we will do this by way of contradiction. For this, assume there exist
\varphi\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
and
\xi\in\Xi_{\varphi}
with
|\Iphi(x,\xi)|>C
.

On the one hand, for any
h\in H
, we have either
h.\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(x,\xi)
, or
h.\Iphi(x,\xi)\cap\Iphi(x,\xi)=\varnothing
: indeed, since
H\subseteq G_{(x,b)}
, we have that
h.\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(x,h.\xi)
for every
h\in H
; as
\Iphi(x,\xi)
and
\Iphi(x,h.\xi)
are components of the support of
G_{(a,x)}
, they are either equal or disjoint.
On the other hand, by definition of
C
and the assumption
|\Iphi(x,\xi)|>C
, generators of
H
cannot move
\Iphi(x,\xi)
disjoint from itself.

These two remarks together give that
h_{i}.\mathrm{\Iphi}(x,\xi)=\Iphi(x,\xi)
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and therefore
H.\Iphi(x,\xi)=\Iphi(x,\xi)
. In particular, we must have
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fix}}^{\varphi}(G_{(y,b)})\neq\varnothing
. This gives the desired contradiction since, by Proposition
2.5
, the subgroups
G_{(y,b)}
must act without fixed points.
∎

Now to prove the lemma, consider the positive constant
\delta_{+}
as in (
1
), and take a laminar action
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
. For any leaf
I\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}
containing the central leaf
I_{S,\varphi}
, the intersection
s.I\cap I
is non-empty for every
s\in S
(see Lemma
1.1
). By the choice of
\delta_{+}
, we can choose
s_{I}\in S
so that
|s_{I}.I|\geq|I|+\delta_{+}
. Let us apply this inductively to the sequence of intervals
I_{0}=I_{S,\varphi}
,
I_{n+1}=s_{n}.I_{n}
, where
s_{n}=s_{I_{n}}\in S
. Writing
g_{n}=s_{n}\cdots s_{0}
, we thus have
|g_{n}.I_{S,\varphi}|\geq|I_{S,\varphi}|+n\delta_{+}>n\delta_{+}
.

Fix now a point
x\in X
, and the constant
C>0
from the claim. We can then choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N\delta_{+}>C
. Next, we take a point
\xi\in\Xi_{\varphi}\cap g_{N}.I_{S,\varphi}
. On the one hand, we have
|\Iphi(x,\xi)|<C
, while on the other hand we have
|g_{N}.I_{S,\varphi}|>N\delta_{+}>C
. As both intervals contain
\xi
, we must have
\Iphi(x,\xi)\subset g_{N}.I_{S,\varphi}
.

When
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, the horograding
\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}
is non-decreasing, and we conclude that
\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}(g_{N}.I_{S,\varphi})\geq x
. By equivariance of
\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}
, we obtain that
\mathfrak{h}_{\varphi}(I_{S,\varphi})=c_{S,\varphi}\geq g_{N}^{-1}(x)
. Since
x
and
N
are fixed and
\varphi
was arbitrary,
c=g_{N}^{-1}(x)
gives the desired uniform lower bound. When
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}
, a similar reasoning gives
c_{S,\varphi}\leq g_{N}^{-1}(x)
, leading to the desired conclusion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

We first show that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\subset\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{Q}
. For this, consider a convergent sequence
\varphi_{n}\to\varphi
with
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Write
\varphi_{n}=\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi^{\prime}_{n})
with
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
such that
0\in I_{S,\varphi^{\prime}_{n}}
. Then, up to considering a subsequence we can assume that we are in one of the following cases:

(1)

either
t_{n}
is bounded, or

(2)

t_{n}\to\pm\infty
.

Assume first that we are in case
(1)
. Consider
K\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|t_{n}|<K
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and take the constant
C_{2}
from Lemma
2.2
, so that
|I_{S,\varphi_{n}}|<C_{2}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then we have
I_{S,\varphi_{n}}\subset(-K-C_{2},K+C_{2})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and Lemma
2.3
gives that
\varphi
is laminar, and thus
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{Q}
. In case
(2)
, Lemma
2.5
implies that
\varphi=\lim\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi^{\prime}_{n})=\Phi^{\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S,\varphi_{n}}(t_{n})}(\iota)
. On the other hand, Lemma
2.4
implies that
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S,\varphi_{n}}(t_{n})-c_{S,\varphi_{n}}\to+\infty
, and thus, by Lemma
2.8
, we have
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S,\varphi_{n}}(t_{n})\to\infty
. Then, by Lemma
1.1
, we have
\varphi=\lim\Phi^{\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{S,\varphi_{n}}(t_{n})}(\iota)\in\mathcal{Q}
. This shows that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{E}
. The fact that
\mathcal{I}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
are open now follows from this and from Lemma
1.1
, since

\mathcal{I}=\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})\smallsetminus(\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\cup\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{E})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathcal{I}}=\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})\smallsetminus(\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{E}).

This shows
(i)
. To show
(ii)
, by
(i)
, it is enough to show that the restriction of the translation flow to
\mathcal{E}
is proper. For this, fix a compact subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{E}
, and a distance
\mathsf{dist}
on
\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
inducing the topology. Since
\mathcal{K}\cap\overline{\mathcal{I}}=\varnothing
, the distance between points in
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{I}
is uniformly bounded from below by some
\varepsilon>0
. By Lemma
2.5
, we can fix
t_{0}>0
such that
\mathsf{dist}(\Phi^{t}(\varphi),\mathcal{I})<\varepsilon
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
with
0\in I_{S,\varphi}
, and every
|t|>t_{0}
. In particular, every
\varphi\in\mathcal{K}
can be written as
\varphi=\Phi^{t}(\varphi^{\prime})
, for some
|t|\leq t_{0}
and
\varphi^{\prime}\in\Der_{\mu}(G;\mathbb{R})
such that
0\in I_{S,\varphi^{\prime}}
. Thus, for every
|t|>2t_{0}
,
\Phi^{t}(\mathcal{K})
is contained in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{I}
, so that
\Phi^{t}(\mathcal{K})\cap\mathcal{K}=\varnothing
. This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

3.
Examples

1.
Thompson’s group
F

As already mentioned, Thompson’s group
F\subset\homeo_{0}((0,1))
satisfies condition
(

F

)
; namely, the subgroups
F_{(0,x)}
and
F_{(x,1)}
are isomorphic to
F
(hence finitely generated) whenever
x
is a dyadic number. Therefore all the discussion in this chapter applies to
F
. In particular Corollary
2.4
implies the following.

Theorem 3.1

(Local rigidity of the standard action)
.

The standard piecewise linear action of
F
on
(0,1)
is locally rigid.

Moreover, Corollary
2.6
implies that actions of
F
on the line can be distinguished by a Borel complete invariant up to semi-conjugacy.

Theorem 3.2

(Smoothness of the semi-conjugacy relation)
.

The semi-conjugacy relation on the space
\Homirr(F,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is smooth. In particular, the conjugacy relation on the subspace of minimal actions of
F
on
\mathbb{R}
is smooth.

Proof.

After Corollary
2.6
, the semi-conjugacy relation on
\Homirr(F,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
is smooth if and only if the same is true for the quotient
F/[F_{c},F_{c}]\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Now, every irreducible action of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is semi-conjugate to an action by translations, thus any
\mu
-harmonic action of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(for any appropriate measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) is actually an action by translations. Since the translation flow on
\Der_{\mu}(\mathbb{Z}^{2};\mathbb{R})
corresponds to conjugating actions by translations, it is actually trivial. We conclude using Corollary
4.2
.
∎

In fact, the proof of Theorem
2.3
can be specialized to the case of
F
to obtain a more precise description of the space
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
. A cartoon of this space
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
appears in Figure
1
; the purpose of the rest of this subsection is to give a detailed description of this picture.

Figure 1.
The space of normalized
\mu
-harmonic actions of Thompson’s group
F
. See §
1
for a detailed explanation.

From now on, we fix a symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
F
supported on a finite generating set
S
of
F
