, and consider the associated space of normalized
\mu
-harmonic actions
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
, with its translation flow
\Phi
. We will follow the discussion in the previous chapter, and refine it when possible to obtain more precise information in this special case. As in (
1
), we have a decomposition of
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
into
\Phi
-invariant subspaces

\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{Q}\sqcup\mathcal{I}\sqcup\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\sqcup\mathcal{E},

defined as follows:

—

we let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
is the set of non-faithful actions, induced from actions of
F^{ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
;

—

we fix a representative
\iota\in\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
of the standard action on
(0,1)
, and let
\mathcal{I}=\{\Phi^{t}(\iota):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
denote its
\Phi
-orbit, whilst
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
denotes the
\Phi
-orbit of the reversed action
\widehat{\iota}
;

—

we let
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{+}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{-}
be the subset of
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
of laminar actions, where
\mathcal{E}_{+}
(respectively,
\mathcal{E}_{-}
) is the subset of laminar actions which are positively (respectively, negatively) horograded by the standard action on
(0,1)
.

Let
\tau_{0},\tau_{1}\colon F\to\mathbb{Z}
be the two homomorphisms defined as in (
1
):

\tau_{0}(g)=-\log_{2}D^{+}g(0)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{1}(g)=-\log_{2}D^{-}g(1).

Note that these induce identifications of the groups of germs
\Germ(F,0)
and
\Germ(F,1)
with
\mathbb{Z}
. We may identify
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
with two points of
\mathcal{Q}
given by their corresponding cyclic actions. As usual, we denote by
\widehat{\tau}_{0},\widehat{\tau}_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}
their conjugate by the map
x\mapsto-x
.

We begin by observing that Lemma
1.1
can be refined in this case to obtain the following description of the sets
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{I},\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
inside
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The subset
\mathcal{Q}\subset\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
is homeomorphic to the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
and is fixed by the flow
\Phi
. The actions
\iota
and
\widehat{\iota}
satisfy the following:

\ \lim_{t\to+\infty}\Phi^{t}(\iota)=\tau_{1},\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Phi^{t}(\iota)=\widehat{\tau}_{0},\quad\lim_{t\to+\infty}\Phi^{t}(\widehat{\iota})=\tau_{0},\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Phi^{t}(\widehat{\iota})=\widehat{\tau}_{1}.

The set
\mathcal{Q}
corresponds to the circle shown in red in Figure
1
. The orbit
\mathcal{I}
is therefore a copy of
\mathbb{R}
inside
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
which connects the points
\widehat{\tau}_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
, while
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
connects
\tau_{0}
to
\widehat{\tau}_{1}
as shown in Figure
1
.

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

The set
\mathcal{Q}
is homeomorphic to
\Der_{\bar{\mu}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2};\mathbb{R})
, where
\bar{\mu}
is the projection of
\mu
to
F^{ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Every element of
\Der_{\bar{\mu}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2};\mathbb{R})
corresponds to a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action by translations given by a non-trivial homomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to(\mathbb{R},+)
up to rescaling by a positive real, so that
\Der_{\bar{\mu}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2};\mathbb{R})
is homeomorphic to the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and it consists of points which are fixed by the translation flow
\Phi
. We deduce that
\mathcal{Q}
is a closed subset of
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
, homeomorphic to a circle, and it consists of points which are fixed by the translation flow
\Phi
.
Let us show, for instance, that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\Phi^{t}(\iota)=\tau_{1}
(the other cases are analogous). By Lemma
1.1
, if
\psi
is a limit point of
\Phi^{t}(\iota)
for
t\to\infty
, then
\psi
factors through
F/F_{+}\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so
\psi
is necessarily a cyclic action, and we only need to determine its sign. This is done by noting that the homomorphism
\tau_{1}
has been defined in such a way that
\tau_{1}(g)>0
if and only if
g(x)>x
for every
x
close enough to 1 (in the standard action of
F
on
X=(0,1)
).
∎

To complete the explanation of Figure
1
, we give a description of the dynamics of the flow
\Phi
on the set
\mathcal{E}
. For this it is convenient to fix an element
f\in F
which in the standard action satisfies
f(x)>x
for every
x\in(0,1)
. For definiteness, we can choose as
f
the element of the standard generating pair of
F
defined in (
2
).

By Proposition
2.10
, for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
, the element
\varphi(f)
must be a homothety, which is expanding if
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, and contracting if
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
, we let
\xi_{\varphi}
be the unique fixed point of
\varphi(f)
. We say that
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
is
f
-centered if
\xi_{\varphi}=0
, and let
\mathcal{E}^{0}\subset\mathcal{E}
be the subset of
f
-centered laminar actions. Finally we set
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}=\mathcal{E}^{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
.
With this notation, we have the following more precise version of Theorem
2.3
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Retain all notation as above. Then both sets
\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
\mathcal{E}_{-}
are open. Each subset
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{0}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
is closed in
\Der_{\mu}(F;\mathbb{R})
(hence compact), and it is a cross section for the flow
\Phi
to
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
(namely it intersects every orbit in exactly one point). Moreover, for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
the limits
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\Phi^{t}(\varphi)
exist and the following hold:

—

if
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
, then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\Phi^{t}(\varphi)=\tau_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Phi^{t}(\varphi)=\widehat{\tau}_{1},

where the convergence is uniform over
\varphi
in the cross section
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{+}
;

—

if
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}
, then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\Phi^{t}(\varphi)=\tau_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Phi^{t}(\varphi)=\widehat{\tau}_{0},

where the convergence is uniform over
\varphi
in the cross section
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{-}
.

As a consequence
\overline{\mathcal{E}_{+}}=\mathcal{E}_{+}\cup\{\tau_{1},\widehat{\tau}_{1}\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}_{-}}=\mathcal{E}_{-}\cup\{\tau_{0},\widehat{\tau}_{0}\}
.

Proof.

We already know that
\mathcal{E}
is open, by Lemma
1.1
. Moreover, for
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{+}
(respectively,
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{-}
) we have that
\varphi(f)
is an expanding (respectively, contracting) homothety. This clearly implies that the sets
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
are both open. For every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
, we have
\Phi^{\xi_{\varphi}}(\varphi)\in\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{0}
, so each set
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}
is a cross section of the flow
\Phi
on
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
.

Choose a generating set
S
of
F
containing
f
. Then for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
and
\varphi
-invariant lamination
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
, the central leaf
I_{S,\varphi}\in\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
contains
\xi_{\varphi}
(the fixed point of
f
), since otherwise
\varphi(f)(I_{S,\varphi})
cannot be related by inclusion with
I_{S,\varphi}
(see Lemma
1.1
). Hence, Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
imply that if
\psi
is any action in the closure of
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}
, then it is laminar and thus belongs to
\mathcal{E}
(since the complement of
\mathcal{E}
does not contain any laminar action). Moreover,
\psi(f)
fixes
0
. This implies that
\overline{\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}
, and thus the subsets
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}
are closed. The statements on convergence now follow from Lemmas
2.5
and
2.8
, by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
2.3
.
∎

To conclude this discussion, we observe that it is a tantalizing problem to obtain further results on the topology of the compact cross sections
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{+}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{-}
, which is at the moment quite mysterious. Note that each section
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\pm}
is homeomorphic to the quotient space
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}/\Phi
, and thus it is independent, up to homeomorphism, on the choice of the generator
f\in F
made above, and by symmetry the two sections are homeomorphic one to the other. The constructions of laminar actions in Chapter
12
show that the spaces
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{+}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{-}
are uncountable, and contain homeomorphic copies of a Cantor set. However we were not able to construct any non-trivial connected subset of
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{+}
, and we do not know whether they are totally disconnected. We also do not know the answer to the following question.

Question 3.5
.

Do the cross sections
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{+}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{-}
admit isolated points?

By Corollary
2.4
, this is equivalent to the question of whether
F
admits minimal laminar actions which are locally rigid.

2.
Other groups of piecewise linear and projective homeomorphisms

Beyond Thompson’s group
F
, some other examples of finitely generated locally moving groups which satisfy
(

F

)
are provided by other well-studied groups of piecewise linear and projective homomorphisms. Let us mention a few.

—

The Thompson–Brown–Stein groups
G=F_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
. Indeed, they satisfy that the subgroups
G_{(0,a)}
and
G_{(a,1)}
are finitely generated for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}[1/n_{1},\ldots,1/n_{k}]
. This follows from
[
11
, Corollary B9.10]
.

—

All Bieri–Strebel groups of the form
G:=G(\mathbb{R};A,\Lambda)
with finitely generated fragmentable subgroup. Recall that this is equivalent to the conditions that
A
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda]
-module,
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
a finitely generated subgroup, and the quotient
A/I\Lambda\cdot A
is finite (compare with Lemma
2.1
). In this case condition
(

F

)
follows from
[
11
, Theorem B.8.2]
, which shows that the subgroups
G_{-(\infty,x)}
and
G_{(x,+\infty)}
are finitely generated for
x\in A
.

—

Similar examples satisfying all assumptions of Theorem
2.3
arise as subgroups of the group of piecewise projective homeomorphisms of the real line. For instance, the group introduced by Lodha and Moore in
[
74
]
(condition
(

F

)
can be shown using the symbolic description derived in
[
74
]
).

3.
Groups with cyclic germs

The following criterion provides many examples of groups satisfying Theorem
2.3
, outside the realm of piecewise linear and piecewise projective homeomorphisms. For example, it applies to the class of (pre-)chain groups studied by Kim, Koberda, and Lodha
[
66
]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
G\subset\homeo(X)
be a group acting minimally on
X=(a,b)
. Suppose that
G
has a finite generating set
S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}
such that
s_{1}
is supported on an interval of the form
(a,x)
with
x\in X
, the supports of
s_{2},\ldots,s_{k-1}
are relatively compact in
X
, and
s_{k}
is supported on an interval of the form
(y,b)
with
y\in X
. Then
G
is locally moving and satisfies
(

F

)
.

Thus any group
G
as in Proposition
3.6
satisfies Theorem
2.3
and its consequences. In particular, Corollary
2.4
implies the following.

Corollary 3.7
.

For any group
G
as in Proposition
3.6
, the standard action of
G
on
X
is locally rigid.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Since elements of
S
are contained in
G_{-}\cup G_{+}
, the group
G
is fragmentable, and since it is finitely generated and acts minimally on
X
, it is locally moving (by Lemma
2.7
). We need to check condition
(

F

)
.
We will find
z\in X
such that
G_{(a,z)}
is contained in a finitely generated subgroup of
G_{+}
(the symmetric case is analogous). For this, choose
z\in X
such that
s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1}\in G_{(a,z)}
. Note that necessarily
s_{k}(z)\neq z
(else
z
would be fixed by
G
). Let
y\in X
be as in the statement, and choose
g\in G_{c}
such that
g(y)>\max\{s_{k}(z),s_{k}^{-1}(z)\}
.
Let
\tilde{s}_{k}=[s_{k},g]=s_{k}(gs_{k}g^{-1})^{-1}
. Note that
gs_{k}g^{-1}
coincides with
s_{k}
on a neighborhood of
b
(since
g\in G_{c}
), and is supported on
G_{(g(y),b)}
. The former remark gives that
\tilde{s}_{k}\in G_{+}
, and the latter that
\tilde{s}_{k}^{\pm 1}\restriction_{(a,z]}=s_{k}^{\pm 1}\restriction_{(a,z]}
.
Set
H=\langle s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1},\tilde{s}_{k}\rangle
, and note that
H\subset G_{+}
. We will show that
H
contains
G_{(a,z)}
. Since
H
is finitely generated, this will give the desired conclusion. Let
I=(c,d)
be the component of
\supp(\tilde{s}_{k})
containing
z
. Since
\tilde{s}_{k}
and
s_{k}
and their inverses agree on
(a,z)
, we have that
J:=(c,b)
is a component of the support of
s_{k}
(indeed
s_{k}
cannot have any fixed points in
(z,b)
, as these would be fixed by the whole group
G
). Thus we can find a homeomorphism
f\colon J\to I
, equal to the identity on
(c,z)
, such that
fs_{k}f^{-1}=\tilde{s}_{k}\restriction_{I}
(it is enough to define
f
to be the identity on any fundamental domain of
s_{k}
contained in
(c,z)
, and extend it by equivariance). We extend
f
to a homeomorphism
f\colon X\to(a,d)
, equal to the identity on
(a,z)
.

Take now
g\in G_{(a,z)}
, and write
g=t_{n}\cdots t_{1}
, with
t_{i}\in S\cup S^{-1}
. Let
\tilde{g}=\tilde{t}_{n}\cdots\tilde{t}_{1}\in H
, where
\tilde{t}_{i}=\tilde{s}_{k}^{\pm 1}
if
t_{i}=s_{k}^{\pm 1}
, and
\tilde{t}_{i}=t_{i}
otherwise. We claim that
\tilde{g}=g
. First of all note that all the elements
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1},\tilde{s}_{k}
fix
c
, and only
\tilde{s}_{k}
acts non-trivially on
(c,b)
. Since
g\in G_{(a,z)}
has trivial germ at
b
, and the group of germs of
G
at
b
is generated by the image of
s_{k}
, we have that the total sum of exponents of occurrences of
s_{k}
in the word
t_{n}\cdots t_{1}
is 0. Hence the same is true for occurrences of
\tilde{s}_{k}
in
\tilde{t}_{n}\cdots\tilde{t}_{1}
, and thus

\tilde{g}\restriction_{(c,b)}=\mathsf{id}\restriction_{(c,b)}=g\restriction_{(c,b)}.

(1)

To determine
\tilde{g}\restriction_{(a,c)}
note that, since
f=\mathsf{id}
on
(a,z)
, and each
s_{i}
is supported on
(a,z)
for
i\leq k-1
, we have
\tilde{t}_{i}\restriction_{(a,c)}=ft_{i}f^{-1}
for every
i
. Thus

\tilde{g}\restriction_{(a,c)}=\textstyle\prod_{i}(\tilde{t}_{i}\restriction_{(a,c)})=\prod_{i}(ft_{i}f^{-1})=f(\prod_{i}t_{i})f^{-1}=fgf^{-1}=g\restriction_{(a,c)}.

(2)

The last equality uses that
g\in G_{(a,z)}
and that
f
is the identity on
(a,z)
. Now (
1
) and (
2
) together imply that
g=\tilde{g}\in H
. Since
g\in G_{(a,z)}
was arbitrary, we have
G_{(a,z)}\subset H
, as desired. ∎

4.
Extending arbitrary actions to locally rigid ones

The proof of the following proposition is based on an idea similar to that for the proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For
X=(a,b)
, let
H\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
be a countable subgroup. Then
H\subseteq G
for some finitely generated locally moving subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(X)
satisfying
(

F

)
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
H
is finitely generated, because every countable subgroup of
\homeo_{0}(X)
is contained in a finitely generated one, by a result of Le Roux and Mann
[
72
]
. Assume also for simplicity that
X=(0,1)
. Let
b_{\ell}\colon(0,1)\to(0,{3/4})
and
b_{r}\colon(0,1)\to(1/4,1)
be the homeomorphisms defined respectively by

b_{\ell}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}x&\text{if }x\in(0,1/2),\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}x+\frac{1}{4}&\text{if }x\in[1/2,1),\end{array}\right.\quad b_{r}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\frac{1}{2}x+\frac{1}{4}&\text{if }x\in(0,1/2),\\[5.0pt]
x&\text{if }x\in[1/2,1).\end{array}\right.

Set also
b_{0}=b_{\ell}b_{r}
.
That is,
b_{0}
is the homothety of slope
1/2
centered at the point
1/2
, while each of
b_{\ell}
and
b_{r}
fixes half of the interval
(0,1)
and coincides with
b_{0}
on the other half. See Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
The maps
b_{r}
(red) and
b_{\ell}
(blue).

Set
H_{0}=b_{0}Hb_{0}^{-1}
,
H_{\ell}=b_{\ell}Hb_{\ell}^{-1}
, and
H_{r}=b_{r}Hb_{r}^{-1}
. Note that each of these groups is a group of homeomorphisms of a subinterval of
(0,1)
, and we see them as groups of homeomorphisms of
(0,1)
by extending them to the identity outside their support. Set
G=\langle H,H_{\ell},H_{r},H_{0},F\rangle
, where
F
is the standard copy of Thompson’s group
F
acting on
(0,1)
. As
F
and
H
are finitely generated, so is
G
; moreover,
G
is locally moving because
F
is so. It remains to prove that
G
satisfies
(

F

)
.

Let us show that
G_{(0,1/2)}
is contained in a finitely generated subgroup of
G_{+}
. To this end, consider the group
\Gamma=b_{\ell}Gb_{\ell}^{-1}
. Then
\Gamma
is a finitely generated subgroup of
\homeo_{0}((0,1))
supported in
(0,3/4)
(we again extend it as the identity on
(3/4,1)
). Moreover,
\Gamma
contains
G_{(0,1/2)}
: indeed since
b_{\ell}
acts trivially on
(0,1/2)
, for
g\in G_{(0,1/2)}
we have
g=b_{\ell}gb_{\ell}^{-1}\in\Gamma
. Thus the desired conclusion follows if we show that
\Gamma
is contained in
G
(hence in
G_{+}
).

For this, note that
b_{\ell}Fb_{\ell}^{-1}=F_{(0,3/4)}\subset F\subset G
and
b_{\ell}Hb_{\ell}^{-1}=H_{\ell}\subset G
; we also easily observe

b_{\ell}H_{r}b_{\ell}^{-1}=b_{\ell}b_{r}H(b_{\ell}b_{r})^{-1}=b_{0}Hb_{0}^{-1}=H_{0}\subset G.

Choose an element
f\in F
which coincides with
b_{\ell}
in restriction to
(0,3/4)
. Then the conjugation action of
f
on the subgroups
H_{\ell},H_{0}
coincides with the conjugation by
b_{\ell}
, thus
b_{\ell}H_{\ell}b_{\ell}^{-1}=fH_{\ell}f^{-1}
and
b_{\ell}H_{0}b_{\ell}^{-1}=fH_{0}f^{-1}
are also contained in
G
. Since

\Gamma=b_{\ell}\langle H,H_{0},H_{\ell},H_{r},F\rangle b_{\ell}^{-1},

we conclude that
\Gamma
is contained in
G
. Thus
\Gamma\subset G_{+}
is a finitely generated subgroup that contains
G_{(0,1/2)}
. Since for every
x\in(0,1)
the group
G_{(0,x)}
is conjugate to a subgroup of
G_{(0,1/2)}
, we have that
G_{(0,x)}
is contained in a finitely generated subgroup of
G_{+}
for every
x
. The case of the subgroups
G_{(x,1)}
is analogous. ∎

We point out the following consequence, which shows that it is not possible in general to perturb non-trivially a group action on the line, by looking only at its restriction to a subgroup.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
H\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
be a countable group. Then, there exists a finitely generated subgroup
G\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
containing
H
such that the action of
G
on
\mathbb{R}
is locally rigid.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.8
there exists a finitely generated group
G\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
that contains
H
and satisfies all assumptions in Corollary
2.4
, so that its standard action is locally rigid.
∎

4.
A non-locally rigid example

The assumption
(

F

)
in Theorem
2.3
might seem just a technical requirement. However, this assumption is substantial and cannot be dropped. Here we construct an example of a subgroup of subgroup of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
which finitely generated, locally moving, and fragmentable (in particular, it satisfies Theorem
2.1
), but does not satisfy the main consequences of Theorem
2.3
(due to the failure of
(

F

)
).

Proposition 4.1
.

There exists a finitely generated, fragmentable locally moving subgroup
H\subset\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the following.

(i)

The standard action of
H
is not locally rigid.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\Homirr(H,\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R}))
be the subspace of faithful minimal actions. Then the quotient of
\mathcal{M}
by the equivalence relation of positive conjugacy is not Hausdorff.

Proof.

The example is based on a variation of the jump preorder construction for groups of piecewise linear homeomorphisms, introduced in §
2
.
Recall that for a piecewise linear map
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with a discrete (but possibly infinite) set
\BP(f)
of discontinuity points for the derivative, we define the associated jump cocycle as the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}j_{g}\colon&\mathbb{R}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
&x&\mapsto&\dfrac{D^{+}g^{-1}(x)}{D^{-}g^{-1}(x)}.\end{array}

The desired example is given by the subgroup
H=\langle G,f\rangle
of
\homeo_{0}(\mathbb{R})
generated by the Bieri–Strebel group
G=G(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{Z}[1/2],\langle 2\rangle_{*})
and one extra element
f
defined as the piecewise linear map, with an infinite set of breakpoints
\BP(f)
, satisfying the following conditions (see Figure
1
):

—

f(x)=x
for
x\geq 0
,

—

\BP(f)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, and
f(x)=x
for
x\in\BP(f)
.

—

j_{f}(-n)=p_{n}
and
j_{f}(-n-\frac{1}{2})=1/p_{n}
for every integer
n\geq 0
, where
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}\subset\mathbb{N}
denotes the increasing enumeration of primes (we keep this convention for the rest of the proof).

Figure 1.
The piecewise linear map
f
for the non-locally rigid example.

Let us first check that
H
is finitely generated and fragmentable.
On the one hand, finite generation of
G
, and therefore of
H
, follows from
[
11
, Theorem B7.1]
. On the other hand, as
f\in H_{+}
, to show that
H
is fragmentable, it is enough to show that
G
is. To see this, recall from Lemma
2.1
that we have
G/G_{\mathsf{frag}}\cong A/I\Lambda\cdot A
, where
\Lambda=\langle 2\rangle_{*}
and
A=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
, and then notice that
I\Lambda\cdot A=A
(see Example
2.2
). This gives the desired conclusion.

For the rest of the proof, we need to define a sequence of minimal laminar actions, that accumulate on the standard action of
H
.
To start with, we remark that from the definition of the subgroup
H
, for every
g\in H
, the jump cocycle
j_{g}
can take only rational values, and
is a function whose support is discrete, and can only accumulate at
-\infty
.
The multiplicative abelian group
\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
is free abelian, with basis given by the primes
p_{n}
. We denote by
j_{g}^{n}
the jump cocycle
j_{g}
post-composed with the projection homomorphism
\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\to\langle p_{n}\rangle
, for fixed
n\geq 0
. We will denote by
\mathsf{S}_{n}=\{j_{g}^{n}:g\in H\}
the collection of the (reduced) jump cocycles. Note that any element of
\mathsf{S}_{n}
is a function on
\mathbb{R}
whose support is discrete, and can only accumulate on
-\infty
, so that when
g,h\in H
are such that
j_{g}^{n}\neq j_{h}^{n}
, the point

\overline{x}^{n}_{g,h}=\max\{x\in\mathbb{R}:j_{g}(x)\not\equiv_{n}j_{h}(x)\}

is well defined, and we can declare

j_{g}^{n}\prec_{n}j_{h}^{n}
if
j_{g}^{n}(\overline{x}^{n}_{g,h})<j_{h}^{n}(\overline{x}^{n}_{g,h})
. As in Lemma
2.2
, one checks that the action of
H
on
\mathsf{S}_{n}
defined by
g\cdot j_{h}^{n}=j_{gh}^{n}
preserves the order
\prec_{n}
. Let us denote by
\Psi_{n}\colon H\to\Aut(\mathsf{S}_{n},\prec_{n})
this order-preserving action.
We proceed to define a
\Psi_{n}
-invariant prelamination
\mathcal{L}^{n}
on
(\mathsf{S}_{n},\prec_{n})
, analogous to the one appearing in the proof of Proposition
1.5
. For this, given
g\in H
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the subset

L^{n}_{g,x}=\{j_{h}^{n}\in\mathsf{S}_{n}:j_{h}^{n}(y)=j_{g}^{n}(x)\text{ for any }y>x\}

and define
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n}=\{L_{g,x}:g\in H,\,x\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
1.5
, one can check that
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n}
is a
\Psi_{n}
-invariant covering prelamination with action of
H
given by
\Psi_{n}(g)(L^{n}_{h,x})=L^{n}_{gh,g(x)}
. Moreover, the map
\mathfrak{h}_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
so that
\mathfrak{h}_{n}(L^{n}_{g,x})=x
, gives a positive prehorograding of
\Psi_{n}
by the standard action of
H
.
We will need the following.

Claim 1
.

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in H
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Psi_{n}(g)(L^{n}_{\mathsf{id},x})\cap L^{n}_{\mathsf{id},x}\neq\varnothing

for every
n\geq n_{0}
.

Proof of claim.

On the one hand, we have
\Psi_{n}(g)(L^{n}_{\mathsf{id},x})=L^{n}_{g,g(x)}
. On the other hand, since the support of
j_{g}\restriction_{[g(x),+\infty)}
is finite, there are only finitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j_{g}^{n}\restriction_{[g(x),+\infty)}
is non-trivial. As the action
\Psi_{n}
is given by
g\cdot j_{h}^{n}=j_{g}^{n}j_{h}^{n}\circ g^{-1}
, one can easily deduce that
L^{n}_{g,g(x)}=L^{n}_{\mathsf{id},g(x)}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
, as wanted.
∎

With a similar argument, we have that if
n\geq 1
(equivalently, if
p_{n}\geq 3
), then
j_{g}^{n}(x)=1
for every
g\in G
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for such choices, we have
\Psi_{n}(g)(L_{\mathsf{id},x}^{n})=L_{\mathsf{id},g(x)}^{n}
. Then, with a routine argument, one checks that the conditions in Proposition
2.17
are satisfied, and therefore the dynamical realization
\varphi_{n}
of
\Psi_{n}
is minimal. Thus, after conjugating, we can assume that
\varphi_{n}\in\Der_{\mu}(H;\mathbb{R})
, for a fixed symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
H
with finite support, and
n\geq 1
. Also, denote by
\iota
a representative of the standard action of
H
in
\Der_{\mu}(H;\mathbb{R})
. Consider the
\varphi_{n}
-invariant prelamination
\mathcal{L}^{n}
defined from
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n}
, as described in Remark
1.7
. Namely, considering an equivariant good embedding
i_{n}\colon(\mathsf{S}_{n},\prec_{n})\to(\mathbb{R},<)
associated with
\varphi_{n}
, one considers the collections of intervals
l^{n}_{g,x}\subset\mathbb{R}
, obtained as the interior of the convex hull of
i_{n}(L^{n}_{g,x})
, for
g\in H
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
By abuse of notation, denote its closure by
\mathcal{L}^{n}
, and by
\mathfrak{h}_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
the horograding extending the map
l^{n}_{g,x}\mapsto x
. Then,
(\mathcal{L}^{n},\mathfrak{h}_{n})
is a positive horograding of
\varphi_{n}
by
\iota
.

Consider now a finite symmetric generating set
S_{0}
of
G
, and the finite symmetric generating set of
H
defined as
S=S_{0}\cup\{f,f^{-1}\}
. Denote by
I_{n}
the central leaf of
\mathcal{L}^{n}
associated with
S
, and
J^{\pm}_{n}
its associated outer rays (see Definition
1.5
).

Claim 2
.

We have
\mathfrak{h}_{n}(I_{n})\to-\infty
as
n
goes to
+\infty
.

Proof of claim.

To see this, notice that by Claim
1
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{n}(s)(l^{n}_{1,x})\cap l^{n}_{1,x}\neq\varnothing
for every
n\geq n_{1}
and
s\in S
. This implies that for
n\geq n_{1}
we must have
I_{n}\subset l^{n}_{1,x}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is a positive horograding, we conclude that
\mathfrak{h}_{n}(I_{n})\leq x
for every
n\geq n_{1}
.
∎

Denote by
{\mathfrak{h}}^{+}_{n}\colon J^{+}_{n}\to[c_{n},+\infty)
the partial semi-conjugacy (see Definition
1.5
), where
c_{n}=\mathfrak{h}_{n}(I_{n})
is the central value, and set
t_{n}:=\sup l^{n}_{1,0}
. Notice that for sufficiently large
n
, we have that
I_{n}\subset l^{n}_{1,0}
, so
t_{n}\in J^{+}_{n}
, and moreover
\mathfrak{h}^{+}_{n}(t_{n})=0
. Then, by Lemma
2.5
and Claim
2
, we have that the limit of the sequence
\Phi^{t_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
is
\Phi^{0}(\iota)=\iota
. This shows
(i)
.
On the other hand, setting
u_{n}:=\inf l^{n}_{1,0}
, then the same reasoning shows (by Lemma
2.5
) that
\Phi^{u_{n}}(\varphi_{n})
converges to
\widehat{\iota}
. It follows that for any neighborhoods
U
of
\iota
and
V
of
\widehat{\iota}
, both
U
and
V
intersect the positive conjugacy class of
\varphi_{n}
for sufficiently large
n
. This shows
(ii)
.
∎
